\section{Inverting CDF}\label{inverting-cdf}

If we can generate uniform random variables \(U \sim \text{Unif}(0,1)\),
we can generate a random variable \(X\) from any continuous distribution
using the \textbf{Inverse Transform Method}. If \(F(x)\) is the
cumulative distribution function (CDF) of \(X\), then \(X = F^{-1}(U)\)
has the desired distribution.

For an Exponential distribution with rate \(\lambda = 1\), the CDF is
\(F(x) = 1 - e^{-x}\). Setting \(U = 1 - e^{-x}\) and solving for \(x\)
gives:

\[
x = -\ln(1-U)
\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\# generate exponential random numbers}
\DocumentationTok{\#\# use method of inverse cdf to generate iid sample from exp(1)}
\NormalTok{gen\_exp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n)}
\NormalTok{\{}
    \CommentTok{\#generate unif(0,1) random numbers}
\NormalTok{    u }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(n)}
    \CommentTok{\#transform the random numbers}
    \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{u)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{b }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{gen\_exp}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10000}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{),}\AttributeTok{mar=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(b, }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Custom gen\_exp"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{acf}\NormalTok{(b, }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Custom acf"}\NormalTok{)}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# r built{-}in generator for comparison}
\FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{rexp}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{10000}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Built{-}in rexp"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit03_sampling_basics_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-2-1.pdf}}

\section{A Special Transformation for Generating Normal
Sample}\label{a-special-transformation-for-generating-normal-sample}

The \textbf{Box-Muller Transform} is an elegant method for generating
pairs of independent Standard Normal random variables from independent
Uniform random variables. It relies on converting cartesian coordinates
\((X,Y)\) into polar coordinates \((R, \theta)\).

If \(X, Y \sim N(0,1)\) are independent, their squared radius
\(R^2 = X^2 + Y^2\) follows an Exponential distribution with rate
\(\lambda = 1/2\), and their angle \(\theta\) is uniformly distributed
over \([0, 2\pi]\). By generating \(R\) and \(\theta\), we can map them
back to Normal variables:

\[
X = R\cos(\theta) \quad \text{and} \quad Y = R\sin(\theta)
\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{gen\_normal }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n)}
\NormalTok{\{}
    \CommentTok{\# calculates size of random samples (we generate pairs, so we need half)}
\NormalTok{    size\_sample }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{ceiling}\NormalTok{(n}\SpecialCharTok{/}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
    
    \CommentTok{\# R\^{}2 \textasciitilde{} Exp(1/2), so we take the square root to get R}
\NormalTok{    R }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{*}\FunctionTok{rexp}\NormalTok{(size\_sample))}
\NormalTok{    theta }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(size\_sample,}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{pi)}
    
\NormalTok{    X }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ R}\SpecialCharTok{*}\FunctionTok{cos}\NormalTok{(theta)  }
\NormalTok{    Y }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ R}\SpecialCharTok{*}\FunctionTok{sin}\NormalTok{(theta)}
    
    \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(X,Y)[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{n]  }
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{normal\_sample }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{gen\_normal}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}\AttributeTok{mar=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(normal\_sample,}\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Custom Normal"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{qqnorm}\NormalTok{(normal\_sample, }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Custom QQ"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{qqline}\NormalTok{(normal\_sample)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit03_sampling_basics_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-3-1.pdf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\# R\textquotesingle{}s built{-}in generator for comparison}
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}\AttributeTok{mar=}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{nsample2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{) }
\FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(nsample2, }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Built{-}in Normal"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{qqnorm}\NormalTok{(nsample2, }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Built{-}in QQ"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{qqline}\NormalTok{(nsample2)}
\end{Highlighting}

\section{Demonstration of CLT and
LLN}\label{demonstration-of-clt-and-lln}

The \textbf{Law of Large Numbers (LLN)} states that as the sample size
\(n\) grows, the sample mean \(\bar{X}_n\) converges to the expected
value \(\mu\): \[
\bar{X}_n \to \mu \quad \text{as} \quad n \to \infty
\]

The \textbf{Central Limit Theorem (CLT)} states that the standardized
sample mean converges in distribution to a Standard Normal \(N(0,1)\):
\[
Z = \frac{\bar{X}_n - \mu}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} \xrightarrow{d} N(0,1) \quad \text{as} \quad n \to \infty
\]

Here we draw from a Gamma distribution with shape parameter
\(\alpha = 2\) and rate \(\beta = 1\). The theoretical mean is
\(\mu = \alpha/\beta = 2\), and the variance is
\(\sigma^2 = \alpha/\beta^2 = 2\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{n }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{100}
\NormalTok{rn }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rgamma}\NormalTok{(n, }\AttributeTok{shape =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(rn[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Raw Gamma Draws"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab=}\StringTok{"Value"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(rn, }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Histogram of Draws"}\NormalTok{)}

\DocumentationTok{\#\# Calculate running averages (LLN)}
\NormalTok{xbar.rep }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{cumsum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{rgamma}\NormalTok{(n, }\AttributeTok{shape =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{n))}
\DocumentationTok{\#\# Calculate running standardized means (CLT)}
\NormalTok{se.rep }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, (}\FunctionTok{cumsum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{rgamma}\NormalTok{(n, }\AttributeTok{shape =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{n) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{n)))}

\DocumentationTok{\#\# Plot LLN: should converge to mu = 2}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(xbar.rep[,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"Demonstrating LLN"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{log =} \StringTok{"x"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"b"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab=}\StringTok{"Sample Mean"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{:}\FunctionTok{ncol}\NormalTok{(xbar.rep)) }\FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(xbar.rep[,i], }\AttributeTok{col =}\NormalTok{ i, }\AttributeTok{pch =}\NormalTok{ i, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"b"}\NormalTok{)}

\DocumentationTok{\#\# Plot CLT: should stabilize within roughly [{-}3, 3] standard deviations}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(se.rep[,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{type=}\StringTok{"b"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{log =} \StringTok{"x"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{main=}\StringTok{"Demonstrating CLT"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab=}\StringTok{"Z{-}Score"}\NormalTok{)}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{:}\FunctionTok{ncol}\NormalTok{(se.rep)) }\FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(se.rep[,i], }\AttributeTok{col =}\NormalTok{ i, }\AttributeTok{pch =}\NormalTok{ i, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"b"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{lty =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{lwd=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit03_sampling_basics_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-4-1.pdf}}

\section{\texorpdfstring{An Example of Monte Carlo for Estimating
\(\pi\)}{An Example of Monte Carlo for Estimating \textbackslash pi}}\label{an-example-of-monte-carlo-for-estimating-pi}

We can estimate the value of \(\pi\) by throwing random darts at a
square with side length 2 (from \(-1\) to \(1\)) and counting how many
land inside the inscribed circle of radius \(1\).

The area of the square is \((2)^2 = 4\), and the area of the circle is
\(\pi(1)^2 = \pi\). Therefore, the probability that a uniform random
point \((X,Y)\) falls inside the circle is: \[
P(X^2 + Y^2 \le 1) = \frac{\text{Area of Circle}}{\text{Area of Square}} = \frac{\pi}{4}
\]

\subsubsection{Visualizing the Method}\label{visualizing-the-method}

Before we estimate \(\pi\), let's visually simulate throwing 1,000
darts:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{123}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{n\_visual }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1000}
\NormalTok{x\_pts }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(n\_visual, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{y\_pts }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(n\_visual, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{inside\_circle }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ (x\_pts}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ y\_pts}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{1}

\DocumentationTok{\#\# Plot points}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(x\_pts, y\_pts, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{ifelse}\NormalTok{(inside\_circle, }\StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"tomato"}\NormalTok{), }
     \AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{asp =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"X"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"Y"}\NormalTok{, }
     \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{paste}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(inside\_circle), }\StringTok{"out of"}\NormalTok{, n\_visual, }\StringTok{"points in circle"}\NormalTok{))}

\DocumentationTok{\#\# Draw the boundaries}
\FunctionTok{rect}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{border =} \StringTok{"black"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{theta\_seq }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{pi, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{lines}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{cos}\NormalTok{(theta\_seq), }\FunctionTok{sin}\NormalTok{(theta\_seq), }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"black"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit03_sampling_basics_files/figure-pdf/unnamed-chunk-5-1.pdf}}

\subsubsection{The Monte Carlo Estimate}\label{the-monte-carlo-estimate}

If we define an indicator variable \(I_i\) that equals 1 if the \(i\)-th
dart lands in the circle and 0 otherwise, then \(E[I_i] = \pi/4\). We
define \(Z_i = 4 \times I_i\), which gives \(E[Z_i] = \pi\).

Our Monte Carlo estimate \(\hat{\pi}\) is the sample average of the
\(Z_i\) values: \[
\hat{\pi} = \bar{Z} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n Z_i
\]

The Standard Error (SE) measures the uncertainty of our estimate. Using
the sample standard deviation \(S_Z\), the 95\% margin of error is
calculated as: \[
\text{Margin of Error} = 1.96 \times \text{SE} = 1.96 \times \frac{S_Z}{\sqrt{n}}
\]

\subsubsection{Results by Sample Size}\label{results-by-sample-size}

We encapsulate the logic in a function and track how precision improves
as \(n\) increases. The table below outlines our findings.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(knitr)}

\DocumentationTok{\#\# n is the number of samples drawn uniformly from the rectangle ({-}1,1)  * ({-}1,1)}
\DocumentationTok{\#\# an estimate of pi is returned}
\NormalTok{pi\_est\_mc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n)}
\NormalTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    X }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(n,}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    Y }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(n,}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
    
    \CommentTok{\# 4 times the indicator function}
\NormalTok{    Z }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{4} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (X}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ Y}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
    
\NormalTok{    mu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(Z)}
\NormalTok{    error }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{1.96} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(Z) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(n)}
    
    \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n =}\NormalTok{ n, }\AttributeTok{pi.est =}\NormalTok{ mu, }\AttributeTok{error.95perc =}\NormalTok{ error, }\AttributeTok{ci.lower =}\NormalTok{ mu }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ error, }\AttributeTok{ci.upper =}\NormalTok{ mu }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ error)}
\NormalTok{\}  }

\DocumentationTok{\#\# Run the estimation for varying sample sizes}

\NormalTok{results\_list }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{lapply}\NormalTok{(sample\_sizes, pi\_est\_mc)}

\NormalTok{)}

      \AttributeTok{col.names =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Sample Size (N)"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Estimated Pi"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"95\% Margin of Error"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Lower CI"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Upper CI"}\NormalTok{), }
      \AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }

\chapter{Point Estimation and Hypothesis
Testing}\label{point-estimation-and-hypothesis-testing}

\section{Point Estimation}\label{point-estimation}

Assume data \(X = (X_1, \ldots, X_n)\) is drawn from a distribution
\(f(x; \theta)\) indexed by an unknown parameter \(\theta \in \Theta\).
An estimator \(\hat\theta = T(X)\) is a function derived exclusively
from the data. Because it relies on random samples, \(\hat\theta\) is
itself a random variable. Its behavior is defined by its sampling
distribution.

The two fundamental summaries of an estimator's performance are bias and
variance:

\[\mathrm{bias}(\hat\theta) = E_{\theta}(\hat\theta) - \theta, \qquad \mathrm{Var}_{\theta}(\hat\theta) = E_{\theta}\left[\left(\hat\theta - E_{\theta}(\hat\theta)\right)^{2}\right].\]

The most comprehensive single-number summary is the Mean Squared Error
(MSE), defined as
\(\mathrm{MSE}_{\theta}(\hat\theta) = E_{\theta}[(\hat\theta - \theta)^{2}]\).
By adding and subtracting \(E_{\theta}(\hat\theta)\) inside the square,
the cross term vanishes, yielding the MSE decomposition:

\[\mathrm{MSE}_{\theta}(\hat\theta) = \mathrm{Var}_{\theta}(\hat\theta) + \left[\mathrm{bias}_{\theta}(\hat\theta)\right]^{2}.\]

\textbf{Key Insight:} Unbiasedness is not strictly necessary. An
estimator that introduces a small bias in exchange for a massive
reduction in variance will frequently achieve a lower MSE than a
strictly unbiased competitor.

Because MSE is a \emph{function} of \(\theta\), it acts as the risk
function under squared-error loss. Two estimators rarely have one
absolute winner; their risk curves often cross. To choose a single
estimator globally, statisticians summarize the risk curve via two main
criteria:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Minimax:} Minimize the worst-case scenario:
  \(\sup_{\theta \in \Theta} \mathrm{MSE}_{\theta}(\hat\theta)\)
\item
  \textbf{Bayes Risk:} Average the error against a prior distribution
  \(\pi\):
  \(\int_{\Theta} \mathrm{MSE}_{\theta}(\hat\theta)\, \pi(\theta)\, d\theta\)
\end{itemize}

Asymptotically, \(\hat\theta_n\) is consistent if it converges to
\(\theta\) in probability. Under regularity conditions, Maximum
Likelihood Estimators (MLEs) are consistent and asymptotically normal,
achieving the Cramér--Rao lower variance bound \(1/I(\theta)\). However,
in small samples, the MLE can often be outperformed.

\subsection{Monte Carlo Evaluation of an
Estimator}\label{monte-carlo-evaluation-of-an-estimator}

Risk functions are rarely available in closed form, but because they are
expectations, we can estimate them efficiently using Monte Carlo
simulation.

\textbf{The Monte Carlo Recipe:}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Define a grid of \(\theta\) values.
\item
  For each \(\theta\), generate \(M\) independent datasets.
\item
  Compute the estimate \(\hat\theta^{(m)}\) for each dataset.
\item
  Average the squared errors:
\end{enumerate}

\[\widehat{\mathrm{MSE}}_{\theta} = \frac{1}{M} \sum_{m=1}^{M} \left(\hat\theta^{(m)} - \theta\right)^{2}\]

Because this represents an average of independent, identically
distributed (i.i.d.) quantities, its standard error is \(s/\sqrt{M}\),
where \(s^{2}\) is the sample variance of the squared errors. Always
report the Monte Carlo error boundaries
(\(\widehat{\mathrm{MSE}}_{\theta} \pm 1.96\, s/\sqrt{M}\)) to
distinguish true differences in risk curves from mere simulation noise.

\subsection{Two Estimators of
Proportions}\label{two-estimators-of-proportions}

Consider estimating a binomial proportion \(p\) from \(n\) independent
trials.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Maximum Likelihood Estimator (\(\hat{p}_1\)):} The sample
  mean, \(\hat{p}_1 = \bar{X}\). This is unbiased but exhibits high
  variance in small samples, especially near boundaries (\(p \approx 0\)
  or \(1\)).
\item
  \textbf{Smoothed Estimator (\(\hat{p}_2\)):} Often called the Laplace
  or ``add-one-in'' estimator, \(\hat{p}_2 = (\sum_i X_i + 1)/(n + 2)\).
  This introduces bias by shrinking the estimate toward \(1/2\) (the
  posterior mean under a uniform prior), but reduces variance by a
  factor of \([n/(n+2)]^2\).
\end{itemize}

We can estimate the risk curves on a grid of \(p\) values using the
Monte Carlo method. Both estimators rely strictly on the sufficient
statistic \(S = \sum_i X_i \sim \mathrm{Binomial}(n, p)\), allowing us
to vectorize the simulation without looping over replicates.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\#Estimators, written in terms of the sufficient statistic S = sum(X\_i)}
\NormalTok{p\_est1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(s, n) s }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ n}
\NormalTok{p\_est2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(s, n) (s }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (n }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# Monte Carlo estimate of the MSE and its standard error.  All no\_sim}
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# replicates are generated in a single vectorized draw.}
\NormalTok{mse\_est\_mc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n, p, no\_sim, p\_est)}
\NormalTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    sq\_error }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{p\_est}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{rbinom}\NormalTok{(no\_sim, }\AttributeTok{size =}\NormalTok{ n, }\AttributeTok{prob =}\NormalTok{ p), n) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ p)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}
    \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mse =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(sq\_error), }\AttributeTok{sd =} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{var}\NormalTok{(sq\_error) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ no\_sim))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# MSE over a grid of p values, returned as a 2 by length(p\_set) matrix}
\NormalTok{mse\_curve }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n, p\_set, no\_sim, p\_est)}
    \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(p\_set, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(p) }\FunctionTok{mse\_est\_mc}\NormalTok{(n, p, no\_sim, p\_est), }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mse =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sd =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{no\_sim }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{10000}
\NormalTok{p\_set }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =} \FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{n\_set }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (n }\ControlFlowTok{in}\NormalTok{ n\_set)}
\NormalTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    r1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{mse\_curve}\NormalTok{(n, p\_set, no\_sim, p\_est1)}
\NormalTok{    r2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{mse\_curve}\NormalTok{(n, p\_set, no\_sim, p\_est2)}

\NormalTok{    bands }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(r1[}\StringTok{"mse"}\NormalTok{, ], r1[}\StringTok{"mse"}\NormalTok{, ] }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FloatTok{1.96} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ r1[}\StringTok{"sd"}\NormalTok{, ], r1[}\StringTok{"mse"}\NormalTok{, ] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FloatTok{1.96} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ r1[}\StringTok{"sd"}\NormalTok{, ],}
\NormalTok{                   r2[}\StringTok{"mse"}\NormalTok{, ], r2[}\StringTok{"mse"}\NormalTok{, ] }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FloatTok{1.96} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ r2[}\StringTok{"sd"}\NormalTok{, ], r2[}\StringTok{"mse"}\NormalTok{, ] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FloatTok{1.96} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ r2[}\StringTok{"sd"}\NormalTok{, ])}

    \FunctionTok{matplot}\NormalTok{(p\_set, bands, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{each =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{),}
            \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($p$)"}\NormalTok{),}
            \AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($E[(\textbackslash{}hat\{p\} {-} p)\^{}2]$)"}\NormalTok{),}
            \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($n = \%d$)"}\NormalTok{, n)))}

    \FunctionTok{legend}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"top"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{bty =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{0.9}\NormalTok{,}
           \AttributeTok{legend =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($\textbackslash{}hat\{p\}\_1 = S/n$)"}\NormalTok{, r}\StringTok{"($\textbackslash{}hat\{p\}\_2 = (S+1)/(n+2)$)"}\NormalTok{)))}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit04_simulation_files/figure-pdf/prop-mse-plot-1.pdf}}

When \(n\) is small, the smoothed estimator significantly outperforms
the MLE across most of the parameter space. As \(n\) grows, the curves
converge, reflecting the asymptotic efficiency of the MLE. The exception
occurs strictly at the extremes (\(p \approx 0\) or \(1\)), where the
shrinkage penalty overrides the variance reduction, making \(\hat{p}_1\)
superior. The confidence bands confirm this crossing is a true
mathematical property, not simulation variance.

\section{Hypothesis Testing}\label{hypothesis-testing}

A hypothesis test maps data to a binary decision regarding a null
hypothesis (\(H_0\)) versus an alternative (\(H_1\)). It relies on a
test statistic \(T(X)\), where extreme values serve as evidence against
\(H_0\).

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Type I Error:} Rejecting a true \(H_0\).
\item
  \textbf{Type II Error:} Failing to reject a false \(H_0\).
\item
  \textbf{Power Function \(\beta(\theta)\):} The probability of
  rejecting \(H_0\). It represents the Type I error rate when
  \(\theta \in H_0\), and the statistical power when \(\theta \in H_1\).
\item
  \textbf{Size \& Level:} The maximum Type I error probability across
  all parameters in \(H_0\). A test has \emph{level \(\alpha\)} if its
  size is at most \(\alpha\).
\end{itemize}

The \textbf{p-value} repackages the test rule without locking into a
fixed \(\alpha\) in advance. It represents the probability of observing
a test statistic at least as extreme as the one observed, assuming
\(H_0\) is true:

\[pv = \Pr_{H_0}\left(T(X^{*}) \ge T(x^{obs})\right)\]

A functional p-value must maintain \textbf{validity}:
\(\Pr_{H_0}(pv \le \alpha) \le \alpha\) for every \(\alpha \in (0, 1)\).
If \(T\) possesses a continuous null distribution and is applied
exactly, this inequality becomes an equality, meaning the p-values will
follow a uniform distribution on \([0,1]\) under \(H_0\).

\textbf{Diagnostic:} Simulate multiple datasets under \(H_0\), compute
the p-value for each, and plot the histogram.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Flat histogram:} The test is valid.
\item
  \textbf{Left-skewed (mass near 0):} The test is invalid and liberal;
  true size exceeds nominal level.
\item
  \textbf{Right-skewed (mass near 1):} The test is excessively
  conservative; sacrifices statistical power.
\end{itemize}

P-values generally fail uniformity for two reasons:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Distributional Misspecification:} A foundational assumption
  (e.g., normality) fails, skewing the null distribution.
\item
  \textbf{Optimization Bias (Data-dependent procedures):} The null
  distribution is computed assuming a fixed procedure, but the actual
  procedure was chosen by inspecting the data (e.g., feature selection).
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Permutation Tests}\label{permutation-tests}

Permutation tests offer a non-parametric alternative when analytical
assumptions are dubious. If the null hypothesis states two variables are
independent, the exact null distribution can be mapped without assuming
normality.

Because the variables are independent under \(H_0\), their labels can be
scrambled without altering the joint distribution (exchangeability). The
true p-value is simply the rank of the observed statistic among all
permuted statistics:

\[\widehat{pv} = \frac{1 + \#\{j : T^{(j)} \ge T^{obs}\}}{N + 1}\]

The \(+1\) correction counts the observed dataset alongside the \(N\)
permutations, ensuring \(\widehat{pv}\) remains mathematically valid for
any finite \(N\).

\subsection{Example: The t-test on Non-Normal
Observations}\label{example-the-t-test-on-non-normal-observations}

The standard Student's t-test assumes normally distributed data. Here,
we test its robustness when exposed to heavy-tailed distributions. This
highlights the first failure mode: applying the wrong null distribution
to the test statistic.

We simulate data using a t-distribution across varying degrees of
freedom (df). Lower df creates heavier tails, with df=1 representing the
Cauchy distribution.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{t.test.pvalue }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{ (x, }\AttributeTok{mu =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.numeric}\NormalTok{(x) }\SpecialCharTok{||} \SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.numeric}\NormalTok{(mu) }\SpecialCharTok{||} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(mu) }\SpecialCharTok{!=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) \{ }\FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Invalid argument"}\NormalTok{) \}}
\NormalTok{    n }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(x)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (n }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) \{ }\FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Can\textquotesingle{}t do a t test with less than two data points"}\NormalTok{) \}}
    
\NormalTok{    t }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(x) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mu) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{var}\NormalTok{(x) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ n)}
    \DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{pt}\NormalTok{ (}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(t), n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{t.test.sim }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{ (N, n, df, }\AttributeTok{mu.true =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mu =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    X }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{rt}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ N, df), n, N) }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ mu.true}
\NormalTok{    m }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{colMeans}\NormalTok{(X)}
\NormalTok{    v }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{colSums}\NormalTok{((X }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(m, }\AttributeTok{each =}\NormalTok{ n))}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
    \DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{pt}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{((m }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mu) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(v }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ n)), n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{hist.pv }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{ (pv, n, df, }\AttributeTok{bins =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\{}
    \FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(pv, }\AttributeTok{nclass =}\NormalTok{ bins, }\AttributeTok{prob =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"grey90"}\NormalTok{,}
         \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($\%d$ p{-}values, $n = \%d$, $}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{u = \%d$)"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(pv), n, df)),}
         \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(size at $}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}a}\StringTok{lpha = 0.05$: $\%.3f$)"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(pv }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{))))}
    \FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{Null Distribution
Diagnostics}\label{null-distribution-diagnostics}

We expect these histograms to sit perfectly flat against the blue
uniform density line.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{N }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{2000}
\NormalTok{bins }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{20}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{100 Degrees of Freedom (Nearly Normal)} With 100 df, the
distribution is practically Normal. The p-values form a perfect uniform
line, confirming validity.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{ (}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{hist.pv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, bins)}
\FunctionTok{hist.pv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, bins)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit04_simulation_files/figure-pdf/t-test-df100-1.pdf}}

\textbf{3 Degrees of Freedom (Heavy Tails)} Heavier tails warp the
p-value distribution. The nominal Type I error rate is no longer
perfectly calibrated, leading to false confidence.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{ (}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{hist.pv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, bins)}
\FunctionTok{hist.pv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, bins)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit04_simulation_files/figure-pdf/t-test-df3-1.pdf}}

\textbf{1 Degree of Freedom (Cauchy Distribution)} With an undefined
mean and variance, extreme outliers dominate. The t-test entirely breaks
down, clustering p-values near 1.0 and rendering the test virtually
useless.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{ (}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{hist.pv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, bins)}
\FunctionTok{hist.pv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, bins)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit04_simulation_files/figure-pdf/t-test-df1-1.pdf}}

\textbf{Increasing Sample Size (\(n=30\))} As sample size scales, the
Central Limit Theorem rescues the underlying math. The p-value
distributions for df=5 and df=10 flatten back into uniformity.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{ (}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{hist.pv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, bins)}
\FunctionTok{hist.pv}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, bins)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit04_simulation_files/figure-pdf/t-test-n30-1.pdf}}

\subsubsection{Power of the t-test}\label{power-of-the-t-test}

Statistical power represents the ability to successfully detect a true
effect. Here, we inject a true mean of \(\mu = 0.5\) while testing
against \(H_0: \mu = 0\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{hist.power }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{ (pv, n, df, mu.true)}
    \FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(pv, }\AttributeTok{nclass =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"grey90"}\NormalTok{,}
         \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($\%d$ p{-}values, $n = \%d$, $}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{u = \%d$, $\textbackslash{}mu = \%.1f$)"}\NormalTok{,}
                            \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(pv), n, df, mu.true)),}
         \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(power at $}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}a}\StringTok{lpha = 0.05$: $\%.3f$)"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(pv }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{ (}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{hist.power}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mu.true =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mu.true =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{hist.power}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mu.true =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mu.true =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit04_simulation_files/figure-pdf/t-test-power-df5-1.pdf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{ (}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{hist.power}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mu.true =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mu.true =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{hist.power}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t.test.sim}\NormalTok{(N, }\DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mu.true =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mu.true =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit04_simulation_files/figure-pdf/t-test-power-df3-1.pdf}}

\subsection{Example: A Permutation Test for
Correlation}\label{example-a-permutation-test-for-correlation}

When evaluating the correlation between variables \(X\) and \(Y\),
dropping the analytical normality assumption in favor of a permutation
test avoids the first failure mode entirely.

\textbf{The Procedure:}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Calculate the observed correlation \(\rho^{obs} = \hat\rho(X, Y)\).
\item
  For \(j = 1, \ldots, N\): randomly shuffle \(Y\) to create
  \(Y^{(j)}\), then calculate \(\rho^{(j)} = \hat\rho(X, Y^{(j)})\).
\item
  Calculate the permutation p-value as the proportion of shuffled
  correlations whose absolute value meets or exceeds the observed
  correlation.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{perm.cor.test }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{ (x, y, }\AttributeTok{no.perm =} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    Yp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(no.perm, }\FunctionTok{sample}\NormalTok{(y))          }\CommentTok{\# n by no.perm permuted responses}
\NormalTok{    permuted.abs.cor }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{as.vector}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{cor}\NormalTok{(x, Yp)))}
\NormalTok{    real.cor }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{cor}\NormalTok{(x, y)}

    \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{ (}\AttributeTok{rho =}\NormalTok{ real.cor,}
          \AttributeTok{pv =}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(permuted.abs.cor }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(real.cor))) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (no.perm }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
          \AttributeTok{perm.abs.cor =}\NormalTok{ permuted.abs.cor)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsubsection{Performance on Non-Normal Dependent
Data}\label{performance-on-non-normal-dependent-data}

We generate non-normal exponential variables with a direct dependency.
The observed \(\vert{}\hat\rho\vert{}\) rests squarely in the far right
tail of the randomized permutation distribution.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{ (}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}

\NormalTok{x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rexp}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{); y }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{0.4} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ x }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{rexp}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{)     }\CommentTok{\# non{-}normal, dependent}
\NormalTok{out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{perm.cor.test}\NormalTok{(x, y, }\AttributeTok{no.perm =} \DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(out}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{perm.abs.cor, }\AttributeTok{breaks =} \DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"grey90"}\NormalTok{,}
     \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($|\textbackslash{}hat\{}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}r}\StringTok{ho\}|$ from permuted data)"}\NormalTok{),}
     \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(Permutation distribution of $|\textbackslash{}hat\{}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}r}\StringTok{ho\}|$; $\textbackslash{}hat\{}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}r}\StringTok{ho\}\^{}\{obs\} = \%.3f$)"}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                        out}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{rho)))}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{v =} \FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(out}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{rho), }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit04_simulation_files/figure-pdf/perm-cor-illustration-1.pdf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{rho\_obs =}\NormalTok{ out}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{rho, }\AttributeTok{perm\_pv =}\NormalTok{ out}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{pv, }\AttributeTok{pearson\_pv =} \FunctionTok{cor.test}\NormalTok{(x, y)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{p.value)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
   rho_obs    perm_pv pearson_pv 
0.33310306 0.06396802 0.07206288 
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Evaluating Null Validity}\label{evaluating-null-validity}

Under an identical, normal distribution, the permutation test perfectly
mirrors a standard analytical test, maintaining true uniformity under
\(H_0\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{test.perm.norm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{ (no.sim, no.perm, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{)}
    \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(no.sim, }\FunctionTok{perm.cor.test}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(n), }\FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(n), no.perm)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{pv)}

\NormalTok{pv }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{test.perm.norm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{ (}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(pv, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{prob =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"grey90"}\NormalTok{,}
     \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($2000$ permutation p{-}values, normal data)"}\NormalTok{),}
     \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(size at $}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}a}\StringTok{lpha = 0.05$: $\%.3f$)"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(pv }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{))))}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit04_simulation_files/figure-pdf/perm-cor-normal-h0-1.pdf}}

Crucially, when testing non-normal, entirely independent data, the
permutation test remains flawless. The standard Pearson test (relying on
a normal approximation) struggles, while the permutation test yields a
perfect uniform distribution without needing normality.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{res }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{, \{}
\NormalTok{  x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rexp}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{); y }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rexp}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{)                    }\CommentTok{\# independent, non{-}normal}
  \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{perm    =} \FunctionTok{perm.cor.test}\NormalTok{(x, y, }\AttributeTok{no.perm =} \DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{pv,}
    \AttributeTok{pearson =} \FunctionTok{cor.test}\NormalTok{(x, y)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{p.value)}
\NormalTok{\})}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (m }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"perm"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"pearson"}\NormalTok{)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(res[m, ], }\AttributeTok{breaks =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{prob =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"grey90"}\NormalTok{,}
       \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($500$ \%s p{-}values under $H\_0$)"}\NormalTok{, m)),}
       \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(size at $}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}a}\StringTok{lpha = 0.05$: $\%.3f$)"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(res[m, ] }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{))))}
  \FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit04_simulation_files/figure-pdf/perm-cor-nonnormal-h0-1.pdf}}

\subsection{Example: Variable Selection and Optimization
Bias}\label{example-variable-selection-and-optimization-bias}

This section addresses the second failure mode: calculating a p-value as
if the model was fixed in advance, when the model was actually chosen by
actively querying the data (p-hacking).

\textbf{The Scenario:} You have a response \(Y\) and 20 candidate
predictors, \(X_1 \dots X_{20}\), with a sample size of \(n=50\). You
test the null hypothesis that \(Y\) has no relationship with any \(X\).

A deeply flawed but common practice is to:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Scan for the single predictor \(X_{k^*}\) that happens to correlate
  highest with \(Y\).
\item
  Run a standard linear regression \(Y \sim X_{k^*}\).
\item
  Report the standard t-test p-value for the slope.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{test\_lm\_sel }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(y, X) \{}
\NormalTok{  k }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{which.max}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{cor}\NormalTok{(X, y)))}
  \FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{lm}\NormalTok{(y }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ X[, k]))}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{coefficients[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{]    }\CommentTok{\# t{-}test p{-}value of selected slope}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# two{-}sided p{-}value of the slope in a simple linear regression, vectorized in r}
\NormalTok{cor\_pvalue }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(r, n) }\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{pt}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(r }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{((n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ r}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))), n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{test\_lm\_sel\_fast }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(y, X) }\FunctionTok{cor\_pvalue}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{cor}\NormalTok{(X, y))), }\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(y))}
\NormalTok{sim\_null }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{p =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{) }\FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y =} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(n), }\AttributeTok{X =} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ p), n, p))}

\NormalTok{d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sim\_null}\NormalTok{()}
\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{lm =} \FunctionTok{test\_lm\_sel}\NormalTok{(d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{y, d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{X), }\AttributeTok{fast =} \FunctionTok{test\_lm\_sel\_fast}\NormalTok{(d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{y, d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{X))   }\CommentTok{\# identical}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
        lm       fast 
0.03412572 0.03412572 
\end{verbatim}

By intentionally fishing for the maximum correlation first, the naive
test is overwhelmingly likely to discover ``significant'' correlations
strictly out of random noise.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{pv\_sel }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{, \{ d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sim\_null}\NormalTok{(); }\FunctionTok{test\_lm\_sel\_fast}\NormalTok{(d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{y, d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{X) \})}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{ (}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(pv\_sel, }\AttributeTok{breaks =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{prob =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"grey90"}\NormalTok{,}
     \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($2000$ naive p{-}values after selection, $H\_0$ true)"}\NormalTok{),}
     \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(size at $}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}a}\StringTok{lpha = 0.05$: $\%.3f$)"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(pv\_sel }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{))))}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit04_simulation_files/figure-pdf/test-lm-sel-h0-1.pdf}}

As the histogram proves, the false-positive rate under this naive
approach explodes far above the accepted 0.05 threshold.

\subsubsection{The Permutation Fix}\label{the-permutation-fix}

To repair the p-value, we must embed the \emph{entire selection process}
inside the permutation loop.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Record the naive p-value from the observed data.
\item
  Shuffle \(Y\), re-select the best predictor, and compute a new
  p-value. Repeat \(N\) times.
\item
  The true valid p-value is the proportion of permuted runs that beat
  the original observed run.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{perm\_lm\_sel }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(y, X, }\AttributeTok{N =} \DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{  n }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(y)}
\NormalTok{  pobs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{test\_lm\_sel\_fast}\NormalTok{(y, X)}

\NormalTok{  Yp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(N, }\FunctionTok{sample}\NormalTok{(y))            }\CommentTok{\# n by N permuted responses}
\NormalTok{  rmax }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{apply}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{cor}\NormalTok{(X, Yp)), }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, max)   }\CommentTok{\# selection redone in each column}
\NormalTok{  pperm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{cor\_pvalue}\NormalTok{(rmax, n)}

\NormalTok{  (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(pperm }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=}\NormalTok{ pobs)) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (N }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sim\_alt }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{p =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{beta =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{  X }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ p), n, p); }\FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ beta }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ X[, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(n), }\AttributeTok{X =}\NormalTok{ X)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{pv\_perm0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{, \{ d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sim\_null}\NormalTok{(); }\FunctionTok{perm\_lm\_sel}\NormalTok{(d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{y, d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{X) \})}
\NormalTok{pv\_perm1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{, \{ d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sim\_alt}\NormalTok{();  }\FunctionTok{perm\_lm\_sel}\NormalTok{(d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{y, d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{X) \})}
\NormalTok{pv\_sel1  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{, \{ d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sim\_alt}\NormalTok{();  }\FunctionTok{test\_lm\_sel\_fast}\NormalTok{(d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{y, d}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{X) \})}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{ (}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}

\begin{verbatim}
                  size     power
test_lm_sel 0.65700000 0.9733333
permutation 0.06666667 0.6033333
\end{verbatim}

The permutation distribution restores the flat, uniform distribution.
Because power can only be honestly compared between tests of the same
size, the permutation test provides the mathematically sound baseline.

\subsection{Example: A Power Regression
Test}\label{example-a-power-regression-test}

The same optimization bias occurs when iterating through continuous
power transformations (e.g., \(X^{0.5}, X^2\)) to maximize correlation
between \(X^q\) and \(Y\). Because the selection rule adapts to the
data, applying standard analytical p-values directly to the final
``optimized'' model will yield artificially inflated significance.

We employ the same fix: encapsulate the entire search process inside the
permutation randomization.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{pow.matrix }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{ (x, }\AttributeTok{powers =} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =} \FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{\{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.vector}\NormalTok{(x, }\StringTok{"numeric"}\NormalTok{)) }\FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Argument should be a numeric vector"}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{any}\NormalTok{(x }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)) }\FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Predictors must be non{-}negative"}\NormalTok{)}
    \FunctionTok{outer}\NormalTok{(x, powers, }\StringTok{"\^{}"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{\{}
\NormalTok{    Y }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{as.matrix}\NormalTok{(Y)}
\NormalTok{    rmax }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{apply}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{cor}\NormalTok{(Xp, Y)), }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, max)}
    \FunctionTok{cor\_pvalue}\NormalTok{(rmax, }\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(Y))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{test.lm.power }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{ (Xp, N)}

\NormalTok{pvalue.perm.lm.pow }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{ (y, Xp, }\AttributeTok{no.perm =} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\{}

\NormalTok{\{}

\subsubsection{Diagnostic Uniformity and Statistical
Power}\label{diagnostic-uniformity-and-statistical-power}

As observed previously, the naive approach causes a massive
false-positive cascade (left). The permutation algorithm absorbs the
optimization phase, correcting the bias and yielding perfectly valid,
uniform p-values (right).

\chapter{Univariate Optimization for
MLE}\label{univariate-optimization-for-mle}

\section{Maximum Likelihood: Theory}\label{maximum-likelihood-theory}

\subsection{Likelihood, Score, and
Hessian}\label{likelihood-score-and-hessian}

A model specifies the density or probability of the data \(D\) given a
parameter \(\theta\), written \(f(D \mid \theta)\). As a function of
\(D\) with \(\theta\) fixed it is the sampling distribution; as a
function of \(\theta\) with \(D\) observed it is the likelihood. For
independent observations \(x_1, \ldots, x_n\),
\[L(\theta) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} f(x_i \mid \theta), \qquad \ell(\theta) = \log L(\theta) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \log f(x_i \mid \theta).\]

We always work with \(\ell\): the product underflows to zero for even
moderate \(n\), and sums are easier to differentiate. The maximum
likelihood estimator maximizes \(\ell\), and where \(\ell\) is smooth
with an interior maximum, \(\hat\theta\) solves the \textbf{score
equation}
\[S(\theta) = \ell'(\theta) = 0, \qquad \ell''(\hat\theta) < 0 .\]

Three quantities recur throughout the chapter:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the \textbf{score} \(S(\theta) = \ell'(\theta)\), which locates the
  maximum;
\item
  the \textbf{observed information} \(J(\theta) = -\ell''(\theta)\), the
  curvature of the log-likelihood at \(\theta\);
\item
  the \textbf{expected information}
  \(n I(\theta) = -E_D\left[\ell''(\theta; D)\right]\), its expectation
  under the model.
\end{itemize}

The second derivative therefore plays a double role. It determines how a
Newton-type algorithm steps toward the maximum, and it determines how
precisely the data pin the parameter down. An optimizer that computes
the curvature has computed the standard error at the same time.

\subsection{Closed Form: the Normal
Mean}\label{closed-form-the-normal-mean}

Let \(X_1, \ldots, X_n\) be iid \(N(\mu, \sigma_0^{2})\) with
\(\sigma_0^{2}\) known. Using the identity
\(\sum_i (x_i - \mu)^2 = \sum_i (x_i - \bar x)^2 + n(\bar x - \mu)^2\),
\[\ell(\mu) = -\frac{n}{2}\log\left(2\pi\sigma_0^{2}\right) - \frac{1}{2\sigma_0^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(x_i - \mu)^{2} = C - \frac{n}{2\sigma_0^{2}}(\mu - \bar x)^{2} .\]

Differentiating,
\[S(\mu) = \frac{n}{\sigma_0^{2}}\left(\bar x - \mu\right), \qquad \ell''(\mu) = -\frac{n}{\sigma_0^{2}}, \qquad \hat\mu = \bar x .\]

Every feature of the general theory is visible here in exact form. The
log-likelihood is a downward parabola, so it has a unique maximum. The
score is linear, so the score equation is solved in one line. The
curvature \(J(\mu) = n/\sigma_0^{2}\) does not depend on \(\mu\) or on
the data, so observed and expected information coincide, \(J = nI\), and
both grow linearly with \(n\): doubling the sample doubles the sharpness
of the peak and halves the variance of \(\hat\mu\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{x20 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mean =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{); x50 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mean =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{mu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{ll\_norm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(mu, x) }\FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(mu, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(m) }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x, m, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}

\FunctionTok{matplot}\NormalTok{(mu, }\FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{ll\_norm}\NormalTok{(mu, x20) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{ll\_norm}\NormalTok{(mu, x20)),}
                  \FunctionTok{ll\_norm}\NormalTok{(mu, x50) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{ll\_norm}\NormalTok{(mu, x50))),}

\FunctionTok{matplot}\NormalTok{(mu, }\FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{20} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(x20) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mu), }\DecValTok{50} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(x50) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mu)),}

        \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($\textbackslash{}mu$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($S(\textbackslash{}mu)$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(score)"}\NormalTok{))}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit05_MLE1_files/figure-pdf/normal-loglik-score-curvature-1.pdf}}

The score crosses zero exactly where \(\ell\) peaks, and the larger
sample gives a more peaked log-likelihood, a steeper score, and a larger
curvature.

\subsection{Consistency, Asymptotic Normality, and Fisher
Information}\label{consistency-asymptotic-normality-and-fisher-information}

Under regularity conditions --- the support of \(f\) not depending on
\(\theta\), the true value interior to the parameter space, and two
derivatives passing under the integral sign --- the MLE has three
properties as \(n \to \infty\), with \(\theta_*\) the true parameter.

\textbf{Consistency.} \(\hat\theta \to \theta_*\) in probability. The
reason is that \(\ell(\theta)/n\) converges to
\(E_{\theta_*}[\log f(X \mid \theta)]\), which by the information
inequality is maximized at \(\theta = \theta_*\): the log-likelihood
surface converges to one whose peak is at the truth.

\textbf{Asymptotic normality.}
\[\hat\theta \;\dot\sim\; N\left(\theta_*,\ \left[n I(\theta_*)\right]^{-1}\right), \qquad I(\theta) = -E_X\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}\log f(X \mid \theta)\right] = E_X\left[\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\log f(X\mid\theta)\right)^{2}\right].\]

A sketch: expand \(S(\hat\theta) = 0\) about \(\theta_*\) to get
\(\hat\theta - \theta_* \approx S(\theta_*)/\{-\ell''(\theta_*)\}\). The
numerator is a sum of iid mean-zero terms, hence asymptotically normal
with variance \(nI(\theta_*)\); the denominator converges to
\(nI(\theta_*)\) by the law of large numbers. Dividing gives variance
\([nI(\theta_*)]^{-1}\).

\textbf{Asymptotic efficiency.} No consistent estimator has smaller
asymptotic variance --- the Cramér--Rao bound is attained.

Since \(\theta_*\) is unknown, the variance is estimated by substituting
\(\hat\theta\). Two versions are available: the expected information
\(nI(\hat\theta)\), which requires the expectation analytically, and the
observed information \(J(\hat\theta) = -\ell''(\hat\theta)\), which
requires only the curvature that a Newton step already computes. By the
law of large numbers \(J(\hat\theta)/n \approx I(\hat\theta)\), and both
give
\[\widehat{\mathrm{SE}}(\hat\theta) = J(\hat\theta)^{-1/2} \quad\text{or}\quad \left[nI(\hat\theta)\right]^{-1/2}, \qquad \hat\theta \pm 1.96\,\widehat{\mathrm{SE}}(\hat\theta).\]

These are asymptotic statements. A flat log-likelihood --- small
curvature --- gives a large standard error, and a log-likelihood that is
far from quadratic gives a standard error that should not be trusted at
all; the last section of this chapter shows a case of exactly that.

\subsection{Demonstration: the Cauchy Location
Model}\label{demonstration-the-cauchy-location-model}

The Cauchy density \(f(x \mid \theta) = 1/\{\pi[1 + (x - \theta)^2]\}\)
has no closed-form MLE:
\[\ell(\theta) = -n\log\pi - \sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\left(1 + (x_i - \theta)^{2}\right), \qquad
S(\theta) = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{2(x_i - \theta)}{1 + (x_i - \theta)^{2}} .\]

The score equation is a polynomial equation of degree \(2n - 1\).
Plotting \(\ell\) for samples of increasing size shows the peak
sharpening around the truth, which is consistency and growing
information in one picture.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{log\_like\_cauchy }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(theta, x) }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{dcauchy}\NormalTok{(x, theta, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}

\NormalTok{thetas }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{15}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{25}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{n\_set }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{ll\_cauchy }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(n\_set, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n) \{}
\NormalTok{  d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rcauchy}\NormalTok{(n, }\AttributeTok{location =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{)}
  \FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(thetas, log\_like\_cauchy, }\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ d)}
\NormalTok{\})}
\NormalTok{ll\_cauchy }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sweep}\NormalTok{(ll\_cauchy, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{apply}\NormalTok{(ll\_cauchy, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, max))   }\CommentTok{\# align peaks at 0}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{matplot}\NormalTok{(thetas, ll\_cauchy, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{),}
        \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{heta$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}e}\StringTok{ll(}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{heta) {-} }\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}e}\StringTok{ll(\textbackslash{}hat\{}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{heta\})$)"}\NormalTok{),}
        \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(Cauchy log{-}likelihood, $}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{heta\_* = 20$)"}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{v =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{legend}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"bottomright"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{bty =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{),}
       \AttributeTok{legend =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($n = \%d$)"}\NormalTok{, n\_set)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}
