The variability of the MLE across repeated samples is the sampling
distribution the theory describes. Repeating the experiment 50 times at
two sample sizes shows the curves clustering more tightly, and their
maxima concentrating, as \(n\) grows.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{loglik\_curves }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n, }\AttributeTok{nrep =} \DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{)}
  \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(nrep, \{}
\NormalTok{    d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rcauchy}\NormalTok{(n, }\AttributeTok{location =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    lc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(thetas, log\_like\_cauchy, }\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ d)}
\NormalTok{    lc }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(lc)}
\NormalTok{  \})}

\NormalTok{curves\_small }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{loglik\_curves}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{curves\_large }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{loglik\_curves}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (obj }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{m =}\NormalTok{ curves\_small, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{), }\FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{m =}\NormalTok{ curves\_large, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)))}
  \FunctionTok{matplot}\NormalTok{(thetas, obj}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{m, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{adjustcolor}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.35}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{),}
          \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{heta$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}e}\StringTok{ll(}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{heta) {-} }\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}e}\StringTok{ll(\textbackslash{}hat\{}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{heta\})$)"}\NormalTok{),}
          \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(sample size $n = \%d$)"}\NormalTok{, obj}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{n)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit05_MLE1_files/figure-pdf/cauchy-loglik-variability-1.pdf}}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# sampling distribution of the MLE at the two sample sizes}
\NormalTok{mle\_at }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(m) thetas[}\FunctionTok{apply}\NormalTok{(m, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, which.max)]}
\FunctionTok{rbind}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{n = 20}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}  \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mean =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mle\_at}\NormalTok{(curves\_small)), }\AttributeTok{sd =} \FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mle\_at}\NormalTok{(curves\_small))),}
      \StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{n = 200}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}} \OtherTok{=} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mean =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mle\_at}\NormalTok{(curves\_large)), }\AttributeTok{sd =} \FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mle\_at}\NormalTok{(curves\_large))))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
            mean        sd
n = 20  20.05930 0.3843737
n = 200 20.01005 0.0920729
\end{verbatim}

The standard deviation falls by roughly the factor \(\sqrt{10}\)
predicted by \([nI(\theta_*)]^{-1/2}\). Two features of these curves
also warn us about computation: for small \(n\) the log-likelihood has
several local maxima, and its shape away from the peak is far from
quadratic. Numerical optimization is required, and it must be used with
care.

\section{Univariate Optimization
Algorithms}\label{univariate-optimization-algorithms}

\subsection{Three Equivalent
Formulations}\label{three-equivalent-formulations}

The same task can be posed three ways, and the algorithms below attack
different versions of it:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  maximize \(\ell(\theta)\);
\item
  minimize \(f(\theta) = -\ell(\theta)\);
\item
  find a root of \(S(\theta) = \ell'(\theta)\).
\end{itemize}

Root-finders are usually faster because they use derivative information,
but they locate any stationary point, including minima and saddle
points. Direct optimizers need only function values and always move
toward a maximum, but converge more slowly. Which to prefer depends on
whether derivatives are available and whether the function is smooth.

Each method below is illustrated with a schematic showing its first few
iterations. To keep the pictures comparable they all use the same small
problem: the per-observation log-likelihood of the zero-truncated
Poisson model of the next section, with \(\bar x\) fixed at 1.8. The
root of its score is marked in every figure.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{xbar\_d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{1.8}                                             \CommentTok{\# fixed sample mean}

\NormalTok{ll\_d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) xbar\_d }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(l) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ l }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{l))          }\CommentTok{\# log{-}likelihood}
\NormalTok{S\_d  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) xbar\_d }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ l }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{l))                  }\CommentTok{\# score}
\NormalTok{dS\_d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{xbar\_d }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ l}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{l) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{l))}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}       \CommentTok{\# its derivative}
\NormalTok{I\_d  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) }\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (l }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{l))) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{l) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{l))}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}   \CommentTok{\# information}
\NormalTok{g\_d  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) xbar\_d }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{l))                          }\CommentTok{\# fixed{-}point map}

\NormalTok{lstar }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{uniroot}\NormalTok{(S\_d, }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{))}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{root}
\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{root =}\NormalTok{ lstar, }\AttributeTok{contraction\_factor =}\NormalTok{ xbar\_d }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{lstar))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
              root contraction_factor 
          1.318364           0.481631 
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Fixed-Point (Simple)
Iteration}\label{fixed-point-simple-iteration}

Rearrange the score equation algebraically into the form
\(\theta = g(\theta)\) and iterate \(\theta_{k+1} = g(\theta_k)\). If
\(g\) maps an interval into itself and \(|g'(\theta^{*})| < 1\) at the
fixed point, the mean value theorem gives
\[\left|\theta_{k+1} - \theta^{*}\right| = \left|g(\theta_k) - g(\theta^{*})\right| \le \left|g'(\xi)\right| \left|\theta_k - \theta^{*}\right| ,\]
so the error contracts by roughly the constant factor
\(|g'(\theta^{*})|\) at each step. This is \textbf{linear convergence}:
a fixed number of digits is gained per iteration, and the closer
\(|g'|\) is to 1 the slower the progress.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{fixed\_point }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(g, x0, }\AttributeTok{tol =} \FloatTok{1e{-}10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{maxit =} \DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{) \{}
  \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (k }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{maxit) \{}
\NormalTok{    x1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{g}\NormalTok{(x0)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(x1 }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ x0) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ tol) }\FunctionTok{return}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{root =}\NormalTok{ x1, }\AttributeTok{iter =}\NormalTok{ k))}
\NormalTok{    x0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ x1}
\NormalTok{  \}}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"not converged"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The mechanism is easiest to see as a \textbf{cobweb}: from \(\lambda_k\)
on the diagonal, move vertically to the curve \(g\) to obtain
\(\lambda_{k+1}\), then horizontally back to the diagonal to start the
next step. The iterates walk toward the intersection of \(g\) with the
\(45^{\circ}\) line, and the shallower \(g\) is at that crossing --- the
smaller \(|g'|\) --- the larger each stride toward it.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lam }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(lam, }\FunctionTok{g\_d}\NormalTok{(lam), }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{),}
     \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($\textbackslash{}lambda\_k$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($g(\textbackslash{}lambda\_k) = \textbackslash{}lambda\_\{k+1\}$)"}\NormalTok{),}
     \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(fixed{-}point iteration: the cobweb)"}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{x0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{2.8}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (k }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{  x1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{g\_d}\NormalTok{(x0)}
  \FunctionTok{segments}\NormalTok{(x0, x0, x0, x1, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)          }\CommentTok{\# up or down to the curve}
  \FunctionTok{segments}\NormalTok{(x0, x1, x1, x1, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)          }\CommentTok{\# across to the diagonal}
  \FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(x0, x1, }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{0.7}\NormalTok{)}
  \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (k }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\FunctionTok{text}\NormalTok{(x0, }\FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($\textbackslash{}lambda\_\%d$)"}\NormalTok{, k }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)), }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{0.9}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  x0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ x1}
\NormalTok{\}}
\FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(lstar, lstar, }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{1.2}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{text}\NormalTok{(lstar, lstar, }\FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($\textbackslash{}hat\{\textbackslash{}lambda\}$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{pos =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit05_MLE1_files/figure-pdf/scheme-fixed-point-1.pdf}}

The method needs no derivatives, but the rearrangement must be found by
hand, and different rearrangements of the same equation can converge at
different speeds or diverge outright. Geometrically, a rearrangement
with \(|g'| > 1\) at the crossing produces a cobweb that spirals outward
instead of inward.

\subsection{Bisection}\label{bisection}

If \(S\) is continuous and \(S(a)\) and \(S(b)\) have opposite signs, a
root lies in \([a, b]\). Evaluate \(S\) at the midpoint and retain
whichever half still changes sign.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{bisection }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(S, a, b, }\AttributeTok{tol =} \FloatTok{1e{-}10}\NormalTok{) \{}
  \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{S}\NormalTok{(a) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{S}\NormalTok{(b) }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) }\FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"endpoints do not bracket a root"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  k }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{0}
  \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ (b }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ a }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ tol) \{}
\NormalTok{    m }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ (a }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ b) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{S}\NormalTok{(a) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{S}\NormalTok{(m) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) b }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ m }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ a }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ m}
\NormalTok{    k }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ k }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{  \}}
  \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{root =}\NormalTok{ (a }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ b) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{iter =}\NormalTok{ k)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The schematic below draws the bracket at each iteration as a bar beneath
the score curve. The dot is the midpoint, and the dotted line carries it
up to the curve, where its sign decides which half survives.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(lam, }\FunctionTok{S\_d}\NormalTok{(lam), }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{1.6}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{3.5}\NormalTok{),}
     \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($\textbackslash{}lambda$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($S(\textbackslash{}lambda)$)"}\NormalTok{),}
     \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(bisection: each step halves the bracket)"}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(lstar, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{1.2}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{a }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{; b }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{3}
\NormalTok{ylev }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{1.4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (k }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{  m }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ (a }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ b) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}
  \FunctionTok{segments}\NormalTok{(a, ylev[k], b, ylev[k], }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
  \FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(a, b), }\FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(ylev[k], }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{pch =} \StringTok{"|"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
  \FunctionTok{segments}\NormalTok{(m, ylev[k], m, }\FunctionTok{S\_d}\NormalTok{(m), }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
  \FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(m, ylev[k], }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{)}
  \FunctionTok{text}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{, ylev[k], k, }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
  \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{S\_d}\NormalTok{(a) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{S\_d}\NormalTok{(m) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) b }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ m }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ a }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ m}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit05_MLE1_files/figure-pdf/scheme-bisection-1.pdf}}

The bracket halves at every step, so after \(k\) iterations the error is
at most \((b - a)/2^{k}\) and the iteration count is known in advance:
about \(\log_2\{(b-a)/\text{tol}\}\), regardless of the shape of \(S\).
Bisection cannot diverge, which makes it the natural fallback when a
faster method misbehaves. R's \texttt{uniroot} uses Brent's method,
which retains the bracketing guarantee while interpolating for speed.

\subsection{Newton--Raphson}\label{newtonraphson}

\subsubsection{Derivation}\label{derivation}

Approximate the score by its tangent line at \(\theta_k\) and solve for
where the tangent crosses zero:
\[S(\theta) \approx S(\theta_k) + S'(\theta_k)(\theta - \theta_k) = 0
\implies \theta_{k+1} = \theta_k - \frac{S(\theta_k)}{S'(\theta_k)} = \theta_k - \frac{\ell'(\theta_k)}{\ell''(\theta_k)} .\]

Equivalently, replace \(\ell\) by its second-order Taylor expansion at
\(\theta_k\) and jump to the maximum of that parabola. For an exactly
quadratic \(\ell(\theta) = -a(\theta - u)^2 + C\) with \(a > 0\) we have
\(\ell' = -2a(\theta - u)\) and \(\ell'' = -2a\), so
\[\theta_{k+1} = \theta_k - \frac{-2a(\theta_k - u)}{-2a} = u\] in one
step from any starting value. The normal-mean example of the previous
section is exactly of this form: Newton converges immediately.

The left panel below traces three steps. At each iterate, the tangent to
the score is followed down to the horizontal axis, and where it lands is
the next iterate. As the curve straightens near the root, the tangent
becomes an excellent approximation and the steps land almost exactly on
the answer.

The right panel contrasts one Newton step with one step of the method of
scoring from the same point. Newton uses the actual slope
\(S'(\lambda_k)\); scoring uses \(-nI(\lambda_k)\), the slope averaged
over hypothetical datasets. The two lines differ away from the root and
coincide as \(\lambda_k\) approaches it.

       \AttributeTok{legend =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(Newton: slope $S\textquotesingle{}(\textbackslash{}lambda\_k)$)"}\NormalTok{, r}\StringTok{"(scoring: slope ${-}nI(\textbackslash{}lambda\_k)$)"}\NormalTok{)))}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit05_MLE1_files/figure-pdf/scheme-newton-1.pdf}}

\subsection{Advantage: quadratic
convergence}\label{advantage-quadratic-convergence}

Let \(e_k = \theta_k - \hat\theta\) and expand \(S(\hat\theta) = 0\)
about \(\theta_k\):
\[0 = S(\theta_k) - S'(\theta_k)e_k + \tfrac12 S''(\xi)e_k^{2}
\implies e_{k+1} = e_k - \frac{S(\theta_k)}{S'(\theta_k)} = \frac{S''(\xi)}{2S'(\theta_k)}\,e_k^{2} .\]

The error is squared at each step, so the number of correct digits
roughly doubles. Two conditions appear in the constant:
\(S'(\hat\theta) \ne 0\), meaning the maximum is a genuine peak and not
a flat spot, and \(S''\) bounded near the root. Asymptotic normality
says that log-likelihoods are approximately quadratic near the MLE,
which is why Newton is so effective there.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{newton }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(S, dS, x0, }\AttributeTok{tol =} \FloatTok{1e{-}10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{maxit =} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{trace =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{) \{}
  \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (k }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{maxit) \{}
\NormalTok{    x1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ x0 }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{S}\NormalTok{(x0) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{dS}\NormalTok{(x0)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (trace) }\FunctionTok{cat}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"iter \%2d: \%.10f}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{, k, x1))}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.finite}\NormalTok{(x1)) }\FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"diverged"}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(x1 }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ x0) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ tol) }\FunctionTok{return}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{root =}\NormalTok{ x1, }\AttributeTok{iter =}\NormalTok{ k))}
\NormalTok{    x0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ x1}
\NormalTok{  \}}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"not converged"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Problem: divergence}\label{problem-divergence}

Newton uses only local information, so the quadratic model can be badly
wrong away from the maximum. Three failures occur:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{wrong curvature} --- where \(\ell'' > 0\) the model is a bowl,
  and the step heads toward a minimum or off to infinity;
\item
  \textbf{overshooting} --- where the score is nearly flat,
  \(S'(\theta_k) \approx 0\) produces an enormous step, possibly outside
  the parameter space;
\item
  \textbf{multiple roots} --- the iteration converges to whichever
  stationary point it happens to reach, which need not be the global
  maximum.
\end{itemize}

The single-observation Cauchy likelihood is the standard illustration.
With one observation at zero,
\(f(\theta) = -\ell(\theta) = \log(1 + \theta^{2})\) and
\(s(\theta) = f'(\theta) = 2\theta/(1 + \theta^{2})\), whose only root
is \(\theta = 0\). The score rises, turns over at \(\theta = 1\), and
decays to zero in the tails --- exactly the flat region that defeats
Newton.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lik }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(t) }\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ t}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)                    }\CommentTok{\# negative log{-}likelihood}
\NormalTok{s   }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(t) }\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ t }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ t}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)               }\CommentTok{\# its derivative}
\NormalTok{ds  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(t) }\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ t}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ t}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(lik, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{400}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{heta$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(${-}}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}e}\StringTok{ll(}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{heta)$)"}\NormalTok{),}
      \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(negative log{-}likelihood)"}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(s, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{400}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{heta$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($s(}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{heta)$)"}\NormalTok{),}
      \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(derivative, with the flat tails)"}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{); }\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{v =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit05_MLE1_files/figure-pdf/cauchy-single-obs-1.pdf}}

Tracing the iterates from three starting values shows the boundary at
\(|\theta_0| = 1\), where the derivative \(s\) turns over:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{newton\_trace }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(s, ds, x0, }\AttributeTok{iters =} \DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{  path }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(iters }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{); path[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ x0}
  \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (k }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{iters) \{}
\NormalTok{    x0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ x0 }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{s}\NormalTok{(x0) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{ds}\NormalTok{(x0)}
\NormalTok{    path[k }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{is.finite}\NormalTok{(x0)) x0 }\ControlFlowTok{else} \ConstantTok{NA}
\NormalTok{  \}}
\NormalTok{  path}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{starts }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{traces }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(starts, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(v) }\FunctionTok{newton\_trace}\NormalTok{(s, ds, v), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{colnames}\NormalTok{(traces) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"start = \%.1f"}\NormalTok{, starts)}
\FunctionTok{rownames}\NormalTok{(traces) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{paste}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"iter"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{signif}\NormalTok{(traces, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
       start = 0.5 start = 0.8 start = 1.2
iter 0  5.0000e-01      0.8000      1.2000
iter 1 -3.3333e-01     -2.8444      7.8545
iter 2  8.3333e-02     -6.4912     15.9680
iter 3 -1.1655e-03    -13.2980     32.0620
iter 4  3.1664e-09    -26.7470     64.1860
iter 5  0.0000e+00    -53.5690    128.4000
iter 6  0.0000e+00   -107.1800    256.8200
iter 7  0.0000e+00   -214.3700    513.6500
iter 8  0.0000e+00   -428.7500   1027.3000
\end{verbatim}

From \(\theta_0 = 0.5\), inside the region where the quadratic model is
adequate, Newton converges quadratically to zero. From
\(\theta_0 = 0.8\) the first step already overshoots into the tail, and
from \(\theta_0 = 1.2\) --- beyond the inflection point of \(s\) --- the
iterates run away, each step landing further out where the derivative is
flatter still.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\# Interactive versions of the same three trajectories (requires the animation}
\DocumentationTok{\#\# package and a graphics device that can write animations)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(animation)}
\FunctionTok{ani.options}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{interval =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{nmax =} \DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{newton.method}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{FUN =}\NormalTok{ s, }\AttributeTok{init =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{rg =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{newton.method}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{FUN =}\NormalTok{ s, }\AttributeTok{init =} \FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{rg =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{newton.method}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{FUN =}\NormalTok{ s, }\AttributeTok{init =} \FloatTok{1.2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{rg =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Safeguards}\label{safeguards}

Three remedies cover most cases. Start from a robust estimate --- the
sample median for a location model. Apply \textbf{step halving}: accept
the full Newton step only if it improves the log-likelihood, otherwise
halve it until it does. Or replace the observed curvature by the
expected information, giving the \textbf{method of scoring},
\[\theta_{k+1} = \theta_k + \frac{S(\theta_k)}{n I(\theta_k)} ,\] in
which the divisor is positive by construction, so every step ascends.
Near the MLE \(nI(\hat\theta) \approx -\ell''(\hat\theta)\), so scoring
inherits Newton's speed while being far more stable away from the
optimum. Its cost is that the expectation must be available in closed
form.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{safe\_newton }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(ll, S, dS, x0, }\AttributeTok{lower =} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{upper =} \ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{,}
                        \AttributeTok{tol =} \FloatTok{1e{-}10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{maxit =} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{max\_halve =} \DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{  f0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{ll}\NormalTok{(x0)}
  \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (k }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{maxit) \{}
\NormalTok{    step }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{S}\NormalTok{(x0) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{dS}\NormalTok{(x0)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{dS}\NormalTok{(x0) }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) step }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sign}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{S}\NormalTok{(x0))          }\CommentTok{\# wrong curvature: just move uphill}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (h }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{0}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{max\_halve) \{                     }\CommentTok{\# halve until feasible and improving}
\NormalTok{      x1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ x0 }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ step }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{h}
      \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (x1 }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ lower }\SpecialCharTok{\&\&}\NormalTok{ x1 }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ upper }\SpecialCharTok{\&\&} \FunctionTok{is.finite}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{ll}\NormalTok{(x1)) }\SpecialCharTok{\&\&} \FunctionTok{ll}\NormalTok{(x1) }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ f0) }\ControlFlowTok{break}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(x1 }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ x0) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ tol) }\FunctionTok{return}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{root =}\NormalTok{ x1, }\AttributeTok{iter =}\NormalTok{ k, }\AttributeTok{halvings =}\NormalTok{ h))}
\NormalTok{    x0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ x1; f0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{ll}\NormalTok{(x1)}
\NormalTok{  \}}
  \FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"not converged"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Golden Section Search}\label{golden-section-search}

Golden section minimizes \(f = -\ell\) directly, using \textbf{no
derivative at all}, which makes it the method of choice when \(\ell\) is
not differentiable or its derivative is unreliable. Keep a bracket
\([x_l, x_u]\) containing the minimum of a unimodal \(f\), with two
interior points placed at the golden ratio
\(g = (3 - \sqrt5)/2 \approx 0.382\) of the way in from each end.
Comparing the two interior values discards one end, and the ratio is
chosen precisely so that the surviving interior point is correctly
positioned for the next iteration: one new evaluation per step, with the
bracket shrinking by the factor \(0.618\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{golden }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(f, brack.int, }\AttributeTok{eps =} \FloatTok{1e{-}4}\NormalTok{, ...) \{}
\NormalTok{  g }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{3} \SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{  xl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{min}\NormalTok{(brack.int); xu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(brack.int)}
\NormalTok{  tmp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ g }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (xu }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ xl)}
\NormalTok{  xmu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ xu }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ tmp; xml }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ xl }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ tmp}
\NormalTok{  fl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{f}\NormalTok{(xml, ...); fu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{f}\NormalTok{(xmu, ...)}
  \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(xu }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ xl) }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1e{-}5} \SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(xl)) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ eps) \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (fl }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ fu) \{}
\NormalTok{      xu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ xmu; xmu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ xml; fu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ fl}
\NormalTok{      xml }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ xl }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ g }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (xu }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ xl); fl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{f}\NormalTok{(xml, ...)}
\NormalTok{    \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ \{}
\NormalTok{      xl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ xml; xml }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ xmu; fl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ fu}
\NormalTok{      xmu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ xu }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ g }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (xu }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ xl); fu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{f}\NormalTok{(xmu, ...)}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{  \}}
  \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (fl }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ fu) xml }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ xmu}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The schematic makes the reuse visible. Each bar is the current bracket
with its two interior points; the point that survives into the next
iteration is drawn in blue and sits at exactly the right position there,
so only the red point has to be evaluated afresh.

\begin{Shaded}

The first test is the negative log-likelihood of the classical genetic
linkage model, with cell counts \((1997, 906, 904, 32)\) and
probabilities

not differentiable at its minimum, so no root-finder applies --- golden
section handles it without modification.

\section{Example: Poisson Data With Zeros
Unobserved}\label{example-poisson-data-with-zeros-unobserved}

\subsection{The Model}\label{the-model}

Counts are often recorded only when positive: a study of hospital stays
sees admitted patients, a survey of litters sees litters that exist. If
\(Y \sim \mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda)\) and we observe
\(X = Y \mid Y > 0\),
\[P(x \mid \lambda) = \frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^{x}}{x!}\cdot\frac{1}{1 - e^{-\lambda}}, \qquad x = 1, 2, \ldots\]

Dropping the factorial terms, which do not involve \(\lambda\),
\[\ell(\lambda) = \left(\sum_i x_i\right)\log\lambda - n\left[\lambda + \log\left(1 - e^{-\lambda}\right)\right],\]
\[S(\lambda) = \frac{\sum_i x_i}{\lambda} - \frac{n}{1 - e^{-\lambda}}, \qquad
S'(\lambda) = -\frac{\sum_i x_i}{\lambda^{2}} + \frac{n e^{-\lambda}}{\left(1 - e^{-\lambda}\right)^{2}} .\]

Since \(E(X) = \lambda/(1 - e^{-\lambda})\), taking \(-E[S'(\lambda)]\)
gives the expected information
\[n I(\lambda) = \frac{n}{\lambda\left(1 - e^{-\lambda}\right)} - \frac{n e^{-\lambda}}{\left(1 - e^{-\lambda}\right)^{2}} .\]

The score equation has no closed-form root, but the likelihood is
unimodal --- an ideal problem on which to compare all the methods
against a single known answer.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]

  \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(n) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(lambda) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(n) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (lambda }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{lambda)))}

\subsection{The Three Iterations}\label{the-three-iterations}

Each method below returns the whole sequence of iterates together with
the log-likelihood attained, so the trajectories can be compared
directly.

\textbf{Simple iteration} rearranges \(S(\lambda) = 0\) as
\[\frac{\bar x}{\lambda} = \frac{1}{1 - e^{-\lambda}} \iff \lambda = \bar x\left(1 - e^{-\lambda}\right) = g(\lambda),\]
with contraction factor \(g'(\lambda) = \bar x e^{-\lambda}\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]

\subsection{Comparison From Several Starting
Values}\label{comparison-from-several-starting-values}

The three methods are run for 15 iterations from a sensible start, a
start far below, and a start far above.

 simple 
       start = 2.084 start = 0.1 start = 100
iter 0       2.08434    0.100000   100.00000
iter 1       1.82507    0.198351     2.08434
iter 2       1.74833    0.375010     1.82507
iter 3       1.72153    0.651809     1.74833
iter 4       1.71167    0.998184     1.72153
iter 5       1.70798    1.316160     1.71167

 newton 
       start = 2.084 start = 0.1 start = 100
iter 0       2.08434    0.100000      100.00
iter 1       1.63694    0.195239    -4597.69
iter 2       1.70334    0.371813         NaN
iter 3       1.70574    0.672763         NaN
iter 4       1.70574    1.099100         NaN
iter 5       1.70574    1.505560         NaN

 scoring 
       start = 2.084 start = 0.1 start = 100
iter 0       2.08434     0.10000   100.00000
iter 1       1.71600     2.10035     2.08434
iter 2       1.70575     1.71682     1.71600
iter 3       1.70574     1.70575     1.70575
iter 4       1.70574     1.70574     1.70574
iter 5       1.70574     1.70574     1.70574
\end{verbatim}

The pattern is the one the theory predicts. Newton--Raphson takes the
fewest iterations from a good start, and its digits lock in from the
left as the error is squared each step. Simple iteration converges from
every start, including the extreme ones, but slowly and at a steady
rate. The method of scoring is intermediate: always ascending, and
nearly as fast as Newton once close.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lhat }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{nzp.newton.raphson}\NormalTok{(xp, }\DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(xp))}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{lambda[}\DecValTok{31}\NormalTok{]}

\NormalTok{err }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(paths, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(p) }\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(p[, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ lhat))   }\CommentTok{\# from the mean(xp) start}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{matplot}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{15}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{pmax}\NormalTok{(err, }\FloatTok{1e{-}17}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{log =} \StringTok{"y"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"b"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}
        \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(iteration $k$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($|\textbackslash{}lambda\_k {-} \textbackslash{}hat\{\textbackslash{}lambda\}|$)"}\NormalTok{),}
        \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(error decay from $\textbackslash{}lambda\_0 = }\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}b}\StringTok{ar\{x\}$)"}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{legend}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"bottomleft"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{bty =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}
       \AttributeTok{legend =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"simple iteration"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Newton{-}Raphson"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"method of scoring"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit05_MLE1_files/figure-pdf/nzp-convergence-plot-1.pdf}}

On a log scale a linear method is a straight line; Newton's curve bends
downward until it reaches the floor set by machine precision.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{S\_xp  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) }\FunctionTok{nzp.scorefunction}\NormalTok{(xp, l)}
\NormalTok{dS\_xp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) }\FunctionTok{nzp.dscore}\NormalTok{(xp, l)}
\NormalTok{ll\_xp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) }\FunctionTok{nzp.log.likelihood}\NormalTok{(xp, l)}

\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{newton      =} \FunctionTok{unname}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{newton}\NormalTok{(S\_xp, dS\_xp, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(xp))[}\StringTok{"root"}\NormalTok{]),}
  \AttributeTok{fixed\_point =} \FunctionTok{unname}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{fixed\_point}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(xp) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{l)), }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(xp))[}\StringTok{"root"}\NormalTok{]),}
  \AttributeTok{bisection   =} \FunctionTok{unname}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{bisection}\NormalTok{(S\_xp, }\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{)[}\StringTok{"root"}\NormalTok{]),}
  \AttributeTok{golden      =} \FunctionTok{golden}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{ll\_xp}\NormalTok{(l), }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{eps =} \FloatTok{1e{-}8}\NormalTok{),}
  \AttributeTok{uniroot     =} \FunctionTok{uniroot}\NormalTok{(S\_xp, }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{tol =} \FloatTok{1e{-}10}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{root,}
  \AttributeTok{optimize    =} \FunctionTok{optimize}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{ll\_xp}\NormalTok{(l), }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{tol =} \FloatTok{1e{-}10}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{minimum)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
     newton fixed_point   bisection      golden     uniroot    optimize 
   1.705744    1.705744    1.705744    1.705744    1.705744    1.705744 
\end{verbatim}

All six agree, which is the point of running them together: the methods
differ in cost and robustness, not in the answer.

\subsection{Standard Error From the
Curvature}\label{standard-error-from-the-curvature}

The optimizer has already produced everything inference needs.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{se\_obs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{nzp.dscore}\NormalTok{(xp, lhat))}
\NormalTok{se\_exp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{nzp.information}\NormalTok{(xp, lhat))}

\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mle =}\NormalTok{ lhat, }\AttributeTok{se\_observed =}\NormalTok{ se\_obs, }\AttributeTok{se\_expected =}\NormalTok{ se\_exp)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
        mle se_observed se_expected 
   1.705744    0.164514    0.164514 
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lhat }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{1.96}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.96}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ se\_obs                     }\CommentTok{\# 95\% Wald interval}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

[1] 1.383297 2.028192
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lam }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(lhat }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{4} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ se\_obs, lhat }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{4} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ se\_obs, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{quad }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.5} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{nzp.dscore}\NormalTok{(xp, lhat)) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (lam }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ lhat)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(lam, }\FunctionTok{ll\_xp}\NormalTok{(lam) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{ll\_xp}\NormalTok{(lhat), }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{,}
     \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($\textbackslash{}lambda$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}e}\StringTok{ll(\textbackslash{}lambda) {-} }\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}e}\StringTok{ll(\textbackslash{}hat\{\textbackslash{}lambda\})$)"}\NormalTok{),}
     \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(log{-}likelihood and its quadratic approximation at $\textbackslash{}hat\{\textbackslash{}lambda\}$)"}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{lines}\NormalTok{(lam, quad, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{1.92}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{v =}\NormalTok{ lhat }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{1.96}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.96}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ se\_obs, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{legend}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"bottom"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{bty =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}
       \AttributeTok{legend =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}e}\StringTok{ll(\textbackslash{}lambda)$)"}\NormalTok{, r}\StringTok{"(quadratic from $J(\textbackslash{}hat\{\textbackslash{}lambda\})$)"}\NormalTok{)))}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit05_MLE1_files/figure-pdf/nzp-quadratic-approx-1.pdf}}

The parabola implied by the observed information tracks the
log-likelihood closely over the width of the interval, so the Wald
interval is trustworthy here.

\subsection{Evaluating the Naive
Estimator}\label{evaluating-the-naive-estimator}

All of this machinery is worth the effort only if the MLE beats the easy
alternative. The naive estimator ignores the truncation and reports
\(\bar x\). Simulating many truncated samples gives the sampling
distributions of both estimators.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mle\_nzp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n)}
  \FunctionTok{uniroot}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) }\FunctionTok{nzp.scorefunction}\NormalTok{(n, l), }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{1e{-}6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{tol =} \FloatTok{1e{-}10}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{root}

\NormalTok{sim\_nzp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(lambda, }\AttributeTok{n\_gen =} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{B =} \DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{)}
  \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{B, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(b) \{}
\NormalTok{    y }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rpois}\NormalTok{(n\_gen, lambda); n }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ y[y }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}
    \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{naive =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(n), }\AttributeTok{mle =} \FunctionTok{mle\_nzp}\NormalTok{(n))}
\NormalTok{  \}, }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{naive =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mle =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{))}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (lambda\_true }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{)) \{}
\NormalTok{  est }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sim\_nzp}\NormalTok{(lambda\_true)}
\NormalTok{  tab }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{t}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{apply}\NormalTok{(est, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(e)}
    \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{bias =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(e) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ lambda\_true, }\AttributeTok{sd =} \FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(e), }\AttributeTok{rmse =} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{((e }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ lambda\_true)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)))))}
  \FunctionTok{cat}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{lambda ="}\NormalTok{, lambda\_true, }\StringTok{"}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{); }\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(tab, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

lambda = 0.5 
         bias     sd   rmse
naive  0.7666 0.0845 0.7712
mle   -0.0101 0.1448 0.1450

lambda = 1.5 
         bias     sd   rmse
naive  0.4201 0.1162 0.4359
mle   -0.0169 0.1590 0.1597
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{est }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sim\_nzp}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (m }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"naive"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"mle"}\NormalTok{)) \{}
  \FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(est[m, ], }\AttributeTok{breaks =} \DecValTok{25}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"grey90"}\NormalTok{,}
       \AttributeTok{xlim =} \FunctionTok{range}\NormalTok{(est), }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($\textbackslash{}hat\{\textbackslash{}lambda\}$)"}\NormalTok{),}
       \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(\%s, bias $= \%.3f$)"}\NormalTok{, m, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(est[m, ]) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{)))}
  \FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{v =} \FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit05_MLE1_files/figure-pdf/nzp-mle-vs-naive-1.pdf}}

The naive estimator is biased upward, and severely so when \(\lambda\)
is small, since that is when most of the probability mass sits on the
discarded zero. Its bias does not shrink with sample size: it reflects
the wrong model, not too little data. The MLE is centred on the truth
with a standard deviation matching the information-based standard error
of the previous section. The optimization was the means; the correct
estimator was the end.

\section{Postscript: Interval Data and a Flat
Likelihood}\label{postscript-interval-data-and-a-flat-likelihood}

Not every likelihood is well approximated by a parabola, and when it is
not, the information-based standard error misleads. Suppose each Poisson
observation is known only to lie in an interval \([l_i, h_i]\). Then
\[\ell(\lambda) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \log \Pr\left(l_i \le X_i \le h_i \mid \lambda\right)
= \sum_{i=1}^{n} \log \sum_{x = l_i}^{h_i} \frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^{x}}{x!} .\]

The inner sum is computed on the log scale with the log-sum-exp trick to
avoid underflow, and the maximization is delegated to \texttt{nlm},
which returns a numerical Hessian and hence a standard error.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{log\_sum\_exp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(lx) \{ mlx }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(lx); mlx }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(lx }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mlx))) \}}

\NormalTok{poisson\_log\_likelihood }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(lambda, low, high)}
  \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{seq\_along}\NormalTok{(low),}
             \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(i) }\FunctionTok{log\_sum\_exp}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{dpois}\NormalTok{(low[i]}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{high[i], lambda, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)),}
             \FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)))}
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# equivalently, and faster: sum(log(ppois(high, lambda) {-} ppois(low {-} 1, lambda)))}

\NormalTok{poisson\_mle }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(low, high) \{}
  \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(low) }\SpecialCharTok{!=} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(high)) }\FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"low and high have different lengths"}\NormalTok{)}
  \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{any}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{floor}\NormalTok{(low) }\SpecialCharTok{!=}\NormalTok{ low) }\SpecialCharTok{||} \FunctionTok{any}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{floor}\NormalTok{(high) }\SpecialCharTok{!=}\NormalTok{ high)) }\FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"interval ends are not integers"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{  fit }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{nlm}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{poisson\_log\_likelihood}\NormalTok{(l, low, high),}
             \AttributeTok{p =}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(low) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(high)) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{hessian =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{  est }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ fit}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{estimate; sd }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ fit}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{hessian[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
  \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{est =}\NormalTok{ est, }\AttributeTok{sd =}\NormalTok{ sd, }\AttributeTok{CI =}\NormalTok{ est }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{1.96}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.96}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ sd)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{pn }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rpois}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lambda =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{tight }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{poisson\_mle}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{pmax}\NormalTok{(pn }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{), pn }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)      }\CommentTok{\# narrow intervals: much information}
\NormalTok{wide  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{poisson\_mle}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{pmax}\NormalTok{(pn }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{), pn }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)     }\CommentTok{\# wide intervals: little information}

\FunctionTok{rbind}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{tight =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{est =}\NormalTok{ tight}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{est, }\AttributeTok{sd =}\NormalTok{ tight}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sd, tight}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{CI),}
      \AttributeTok{wide  =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{est =}\NormalTok{ wide}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{est,  }\AttributeTok{sd =}\NormalTok{ wide}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sd,  wide}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{CI))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
           est        sd                  
tight 21.53996 0.1565054 21.23321 21.84671
wide  20.00541 0.1752922 19.66183 20.34898
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lam\_grid }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (iv }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{lo =} \FunctionTok{pmax}\NormalTok{(pn }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{hi =}\NormalTok{ pn }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lab =} \StringTok{"narrow intervals"}\NormalTok{),}
                \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{lo =} \FunctionTok{pmax}\NormalTok{(pn }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{hi =}\NormalTok{ pn }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lab =} \StringTok{"wide intervals"}\NormalTok{))) \{}
\NormalTok{  lp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(lam\_grid, poisson\_log\_likelihood, }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{low =}\NormalTok{ iv}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{lo, }\AttributeTok{high =}\NormalTok{ iv}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{hi)}
  \FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(lam\_grid, lp }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(lp), }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{),}
       \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($\textbackslash{}lambda$)"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"($}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}e}\StringTok{ll(\textbackslash{}lambda) {-} }\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}e}\StringTok{ll(\textbackslash{}hat\{\textbackslash{}lambda\})$)"}\NormalTok{),}
       \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{TeX}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(r}\StringTok{"(\%s)"}\NormalTok{, iv}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{lab)))}
  \FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{1.92}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit05_MLE1_files/figure-pdf/interval-poisson-loglik-1.pdf}}

With narrow intervals the log-likelihood is sharply peaked and the
standard error is small and reliable. With wide intervals, most of which
include the lower boundary at zero, the log-likelihood is nearly flat
over a broad range: the curvature at the maximum is tiny, the reported
standard error is large, and --- because the curve is visibly asymmetric
and non-quadratic --- even that large value understates the true
uncertainty. The information-based standard error is an asymptotic
approximation resting on local quadratic behaviour, and this is what its
failure looks like. When the log-likelihood is this shape, report a
likelihood-based interval, read off where \(\ell\) drops by 1.92,
instead of a Wald interval.

\section{\texorpdfstring{A Practical Newton-Raphson Algorithm for
Finding
\(\sqrt{a}\)}{A Practical Newton-Raphson Algorithm for Finding \textbackslash sqrt\{a\}}}\label{a-practical-newton-raphson-algorithm-for-finding-sqrta}

\textbf{Derivation.} To find \(\sqrt{a}\), take \(f(x) = x^2 - a\),
whose positive root is \(\sqrt a\). Then \(f'(x) = 2x\), and the Newton
step is

\[x_{k+1} = x_k - \frac{f(x_k)}{f'(x_k)} = x_k - \frac{x_k^{2} - a}{2x_k} = \frac{1}{2}\left(x_k + \frac{a}{x_k}\right).\]

The final form is the Babylonian rule: if \(x_k\) is too small then
\(a/x_k\) is too large, and the root lies between them, so average the
pair. Newton's tangent line lands exactly on the midpoint.

The error follows the general quadratic law. Writing
\(e_k = x_k - \sqrt a\),

\[e_{k+1} = \frac{1}{2}\left(x_k + \frac{a}{x_k}\right) - \sqrt a = \frac{\left(x_k - \sqrt a\right)^{2}}{2x_k} = \frac{e_k^{2}}{2x_k},\]

so the number of correct digits roughly doubles each step.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{babylonian }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(a, }\AttributeTok{x0 =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{iters =} \DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{)}
  \FunctionTok{Reduce}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x, k) (x }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ a }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ x) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{iters, }\AttributeTok{init =}\NormalTok{ x0, }\AttributeTok{accumulate =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{traces }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{), babylonian)}
\FunctionTok{dimnames}\NormalTok{(traces) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{paste}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"iter"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{), }\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"sqrt(\%d)"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{)))}

\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(traces, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

iter 0 1.00000000000  1.00000000000  1.00000000000
iter 1 5.50000000000 10.50000000000 25.50000000000
iter 2 3.65909090909  6.20238095238 13.73039215686
iter 3 3.19600508187  4.71347454529  8.68597437190
iter 4 3.16245562280  4.47831444547  7.22119047433
iter 5 3.16227766518  4.47214021707  7.07262827574

       sqrt(10) sqrt(20) sqrt(50)
iter 0 2.16e+00 3.47e+00 6.07e+00
iter 1 2.34e+00 6.03e+00 1.84e+01
iter 2 4.97e-01 1.73e+00 6.66e+00
