Starting from \(x_0 = 1\) in every case, the first two steps do the
coarse work --- the larger \(a\) is, the further the opening leap ---
and from the third step on the error squares: roughly \(10^{-1}\), then
\(10^{-3}\), then \(10^{-6}\), then machine precision. Six iterations
suffice for all three, though \(\sqrt{50}\) spends one more step than
\(\sqrt{10}\) recovering from the poor start.

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Multivariate Optimization Methods for Finding
MLE}\label{multivariate-optimization-methods-for-finding-mle}

In the previous chapter the parameter was a scalar, and the
log-likelihood could be inspected by plotting it. Once
\(\theta \in \mathbb{R}^p\) neither is true: we cannot see the surface,
and the notion of ``bracketing a root'' that underlies bisection and
golden section has no direct multivariate analogue. What survives is the
local quadratic approximation, and the Newton--Raphson algorithm is its
direct consequence.

This chapter develops the multivariate Newton--Raphson method and its
statistical variant, Fisher scoring, applies both to logistic
regression, examines interactively how and why the iteration can
diverge, and closes with a survey of the derivative-free and
gradient-only alternatives.

\section{The Newton--Raphson Algorithm in Several
Dimensions}\label{the-newtonraphson-algorithm-in-several-dimensions}

\subsection{Derivation from a quadratic
approximation}\label{derivation-from-a-quadratic-approximation}

Write the log-likelihood as \(\ell(\theta)\) for
\(\theta \in \mathbb{R}^p\), and define

\[
S(\theta) = \frac{\partial \ell(\theta)}{\partial \theta}
\quad (p \times 1, \text{ the score}),
\qquad
H(\theta) = \frac{\partial^2 \ell(\theta)}{\partial \theta \, \partial \theta^{\top}}
\quad (p \times p, \text{ the Hessian}).
\]

The negative Hessian \(J(\theta) = -H(\theta)\) is the \textbf{observed
information matrix}.

Expand \(\ell\) to second order about the current iterate
\(\theta^{(k)}\):

\[
\ell(\theta) \;\approx\; \ell(\theta^{(k)})
 + S(\theta^{(k)})^{\top}(\theta - \theta^{(k)})
 - \tfrac{1}{2}\,(\theta - \theta^{(k)})^{\top} J(\theta^{(k)}) \,(\theta - \theta^{(k)}).
\]

This is a quadratic in \(\theta\). If \(J(\theta^{(k)})\) is positive
definite the quadratic has a unique maximum, found by setting its
derivative to zero:

\[
S(\theta^{(k)}) - J(\theta^{(k)})(\theta - \theta^{(k)}) = 0 .
\]

Solving for \(\theta\) gives the \textbf{Newton--Raphson update}:

\[
\boxed{\;\theta^{(k+1)} = \theta^{(k)} + J(\theta^{(k)})^{-1} S(\theta^{(k)}) \;}
\]

The algorithm therefore replaces the log-likelihood by the quadratic
that matches its value, slope and curvature at the current point, and
jumps to the maximum of that quadratic. Everything that follows --- the
fast convergence, and the modes of failure --- comes from how good or
bad that replacement is.

Three remarks are worth making explicit.

\emph{The inverse is never formed.} Write the update as
\(\theta^{(k+1)} = \theta^{(k)} + \delta^{(k)}\) where \(\delta^{(k)}\)
solves the linear system \(J(\theta^{(k)})\,\delta = S(\theta^{(k)})\).
In R this is \texttt{solve(J,\ S)}, not \texttt{solve(J)\ \%*\%\ S}. The
former is faster and numerically more stable; the latter computes
\(p^2\) numbers in order to use \(p\) of them.

\emph{The step is not the gradient direction.}
\(\delta^{(k)} = J^{-1}S\) points along the gradient only when \(J\) is
a multiple of the identity. The information matrix rescales and rotates
the ascent direction to account for differing curvature and correlation
among the parameters, which is exactly why Newton--Raphson is
insensitive to the scaling of the parameters while gradient descent is
not.

\emph{Positive definiteness is an assumption, not a guarantee.} Away
from the maximum \(J(\theta^{(k)})\) can be indefinite or singular, in
which case the ``maximum of the quadratic'' is a saddle point or does
not exist, and \(\delta^{(k)}\) may point downhill.
Section~\ref{sec-divergence} examines this.

\subsubsection{Fisher scoring}\label{fisher-scoring}

The observed information \(J(\theta)\) depends on the data. Its
expectation under the model,

\[
I(\theta) = E_{\theta}\{J(\theta)\} = -E_{\theta}\left\{ \frac{\partial^2 \ell(\theta)}{\partial \theta \, \partial \theta^{\top}} \right\},
\]

is the \textbf{expected (Fisher) information matrix}. Replacing \(J\) by
\(I\) in the update gives the \textbf{method of scoring}:

\[
\theta^{(k+1)} = \theta^{(k)} + I(\theta^{(k)})^{-1} S(\theta^{(k)}).
\]

The two algorithms have different iterates but the same fixed point,
since both stop where \(S(\theta) = 0\). The practical differences:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\caption{Newton--Raphson compared with Fisher
scoring}\label{tbl-nr-scoring}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Newton--Raphson
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Fisher scoring
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Newton--Raphson
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Fisher scoring
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Curvature matrix & Observed information \(J(\theta)\) & Expected
information \(I(\theta)\) \\
Derivation & Second derivative of \(\ell\) & Expectation of that second
derivative \\
Positive definite? & Only guaranteed near the maximum & Always, wherever
the model is identified \\
Algebra & Can be messy & Often simpler, as data-dependent terms average
out \\
Local convergence & Quadratic & Linear, but typically with a very small
rate constant \\
Stability from poor starts & Less stable & More stable \\
\end{longtable}

The guaranteed positive definiteness of \(I(\theta)\) is the main
practical argument for scoring: the update direction is always an ascent
direction, so the iteration cannot be sent uphill-seeking in a downhill
direction by an ill-behaved Hessian.

For generalised linear models with the \textbf{canonical link} the two
coincide exactly, because the second derivative of the log-likelihood
does not involve the response. Logistic regression uses the canonical
logit link, so the example below is simultaneously Newton--Raphson,
Fisher scoring, and --- as we note at the end of the section ---
iteratively reweighted least squares.

\subsection{Convergence properties}\label{convergence-properties}

Near a maximum \(\hat\theta\) at which \(J(\hat\theta)\) is positive
definite, Newton--Raphson converges \textbf{quadratically}: the error
satisfies

\[
\|\theta^{(k+1)} - \hat\theta\| \;\le\; C \,\|\theta^{(k)} - \hat\theta\|^{2}
\]

for some constant \(C\) depending on the third derivatives. Informally,
the number of correct digits doubles at each iteration, which is why
five or six iterations are usually enough and why a well-started run
appears to converge instantly.

The qualification ``near'' is essential. The theorem is local: it
guarantees nothing about a start far from \(\hat\theta\), and nothing at
all if \(J\) fails to be positive definite along the way.

\subsection{Standard errors as a
by-product}\label{standard-errors-as-a-by-product}

At convergence the same matrix that drove the iteration supplies the
asymptotic variance. Under the usual regularity conditions,

\[
\hat\theta \;\dot\sim\; N\!\left(\theta, \; J(\hat\theta)^{-1}\right),
\]

so
\(\operatorname{SE}(\hat\theta_j) = \sqrt{[J(\hat\theta)^{-1}]_{jj}}\).
This is a genuine practical advantage of Newton-type methods over
derivative-free ones: the curvature information needed for inference has
already been computed. Methods that never form a Hessian require a
separate numerical differentiation step, which is what
\texttt{hessian\ =\ TRUE} requests from \texttt{nlm} and \texttt{optim}.

\section{Worked Example: Logistic
Regression}\label{worked-example-logistic-regression}

\subsection{The model and its
derivatives}\label{the-model-and-its-derivatives}

With a single covariate \(z_i\) and binary response \(r_i\), let
\(u_i = \beta_0 + \beta_1 z_i\) and \(p_i = e^{u_i}/(1 + e^{u_i})\). The
log-likelihood is

\[
\ell(\beta) = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{ r_i u_i - \log\left(1 + e^{u_i}\right) \right\}.
\]

Writing \(X\) for the \(n \times 2\) design matrix with rows
\((1, z_i)\), the score and information are

\[
S(\beta) = X^{\top}(r - p), \qquad
J(\beta) = X^{\top} W X, \qquad W = \operatorname{diag}\{p_i(1 - p_i)\}.
\]

Note that \(J\) does not involve \(r\). This is the canonical-link
property mentioned above, and it means \(J(\beta) = I(\beta)\)
identically.

Two numerical points, both carried over from the chapter on computer
arithmetic. First, \(\log(1 + e^{u})\) overflows for \(u \gtrsim 710\);
the stable form is \(\log(1+e^u) = \max(u,0) + \log(1 + e^{-|u|})\).
Second, \(p_i(1-p_i) \to 0\) when \(|u_i|\) is large, so \(W\) --- and
hence \(J\) --- degenerates toward singularity when the fitted
probabilities are extreme. That observation is the whole explanation of
the divergence we are about to see.

\subsection{Implementation}\label{implementation}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# numerically stable log(1 + exp(u))}
\NormalTok{log1pexp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(u) }\FunctionTok{pmax}\NormalTok{(u, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{log1p}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{abs}\NormalTok{(u)))}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# negative log{-}likelihood, negative score, and information matrix}
\NormalTok{lik\_score\_info }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(b, r, z) \{}
\NormalTok{    u }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ b[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ b[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ z}
\NormalTok{    p }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{plogis}\NormalTok{(u)}
\NormalTok{    w }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ p }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ p)}
    \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}
        \AttributeTok{neg\_loglik =} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(u }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ r }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log1pexp}\NormalTok{(u)),}
        \AttributeTok{neg\_score  =} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(r }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ p), }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(z }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (r }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ p))),}
        \AttributeTok{inf        =} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(w), }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(z }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ w),}
                              \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(z }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ w), }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(z }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ z }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ w)), }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    )}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{neg\_logp\_logistic }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(b, z, r) \{}
\NormalTok{    u }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ b[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ b[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ z}
    \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(u }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ r }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log1pexp}\NormalTok{(u))}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Because we minimise the \emph{negative} log-likelihood, the update
\(\beta^{(k+1)} = \beta^{(k)} + J^{-1}S\) becomes
\(\beta^{(k+1)} = \beta^{(k)} - J^{-1} \times (\text{negative score})\),
which is the form coded below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mle\_logistic\_nr }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(b0, no\_iter, r, z, }\AttributeTok{debug =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NA\_real\_}\NormalTok{, no\_iter }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{,}
                  \AttributeTok{dimnames =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"beta0"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"beta1"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"neg\_loglike"}\NormalTok{)))}
\NormalTok{    b }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ b0}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(no\_iter }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)) \{}
\NormalTok{        q }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{lik\_score\_info}\NormalTok{(b, r, z)}
\NormalTok{        out[i, ] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(b, q}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{neg\_loglik)}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (debug) }\FunctionTok{print}\NormalTok{(out[i, ])}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (i }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ no\_iter) }\ControlFlowTok{break}
\NormalTok{        step }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{tryCatch}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{solve}\NormalTok{(q}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{inf, q}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{neg\_score),}
                         \AttributeTok{error =} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(e) }\FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NA\_real\_}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{any}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.finite}\NormalTok{(step))) }\ControlFlowTok{break}
\NormalTok{        b }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ b }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ step}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    out}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{gen\_logistic\_data }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(b, n) \{}
\NormalTok{    z }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sort}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(n, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{    p }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{plogis}\NormalTok{(b[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ b[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ z)}
\NormalTok{    r }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rbinom}\NormalTok{(n, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, p)}
    \FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(z, p, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"P(r = 1)"}\NormalTok{)}
    \FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(z, r, }\AttributeTok{col =}\NormalTok{ r }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
    \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{z =}\NormalTok{ z, }\AttributeTok{r =}\NormalTok{ r)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{data }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{gen\_logistic\_data}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{), }\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

}

A start close to the truth converges in a handful of iterations:

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Comparison with \texttt{nlm} and
\texttt{glm}}{Comparison with nlm and glm}}\label{comparison-with-nlm-and-glm}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{logit\_nlm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{nlm}\NormalTok{(neg\_logp\_logistic, }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{),}
                 \AttributeTok{z =}\NormalTok{ data}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{z, }\AttributeTok{r =}\NormalTok{ data}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{r, }\AttributeTok{hessian =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{logit\_nlm}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{estimate}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] -0.2725325  1.5034237
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{diag}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{solve}\NormalTok{(logit\_nlm}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{hessian)))   }\CommentTok{\# asymptotic standard errors}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 0.1797391 0.2089673
\end{verbatim}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{glm}\NormalTok{(r }\SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ z, }\AttributeTok{family =} \FunctionTok{binomial}\NormalTok{(), }\AttributeTok{data =}\NormalTok{ data))}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{coefficients}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
              Estimate Std. Error   z value     Pr(>|z|)
(Intercept) -0.2725322  0.1797371 -1.516283 1.294479e-01
z            1.5034244  0.2089473  7.195234 6.235374e-13
\end{verbatim}

The three routes agree to printing accuracy. \texttt{glm} is doing the
same arithmetic as \texttt{mle\_logistic\_nr}: for a GLM the
Newton--Raphson step can be rearranged as a weighted least-squares fit
of the working response \(u_i + (r_i - p_i)/\{p_i(1-p_i)\}\) on \(X\)
with weights \(p_i(1-p_i)\), giving \textbf{iteratively reweighted least
squares (IRLS)}. This is not a different algorithm but a different
arrangement of the same one, and it explains why \texttt{glm} reports
``Fisher Scoring iterations'' in its output.

\section{Divergence}\label{sec-divergence}

\subsection{What goes wrong}\label{what-goes-wrong}

The quadratic approximation is trustworthy only in a neighbourhood of
the current iterate. Starting from a large \(|\beta_1|\), nearly all
fitted probabilities are pushed to \(0\) or \(1\), so the weights
\(p_i(1-p_i)\) are essentially zero, \(J\) is nearly singular, and
\(J^{-1}\) is enormous. The resulting step is huge, the new iterate
lands far outside the region where the quadratic was a reasonable fit,
and the process can oscillate or run off to infinity --- even though the
logistic log-likelihood is globally concave and has a unique maximum.

The interactive figure below lets you choose a starting value and step
through the iterations. Press the play button on the iteration slider to
animate the path over the contours of the negative log-likelihood. This
app runs live only in the HTML edition of this book; readers of other
formats can follow the link below it.

\protect\phantomsection\label{sec-nr-divergence-app}{}

This section contains a live, in-browser Shiny app that only runs in the
HTML edition of this book. View it at
\url{https://longhaisk.github.io/compstat/chapters/unit06_MLEm.html\#sec-nr-divergence-app}.

\end{Highlighting}

and add \texttt{filters:\ {[}shinylive{]}} to the document or project

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Safeguards}\label{safeguards-1}

The divergence is not a defect of the Newton direction but of the Newton
\emph{step length}. The standard remedies all keep the direction and
control the distance.

\textbf{Step halving (backtracking).} Take \(\delta^{(k)}\) as usual but
move \(\theta^{(k+1)} = \theta^{(k)} + s\,\delta^{(k)}\), starting from
\(s = 1\) and halving until the log-likelihood actually increases. This
costs a few extra function evaluations, never damages quadratic
convergence near the maximum (where \(s = 1\) is accepted on the first
try), and rescues most divergent runs. Toggle it in the app above to see
the effect.

\textbf{Ridging (Levenberg--Marquardt).} Replace \(J\) by
\(J + \lambda \mathbb{I}\) with \(\lambda > 0\). Large \(\lambda\)
shrinks the step toward a small gradient step; \(\lambda \to 0\)
recovers Newton. This also repairs indefiniteness, and is the approach
\texttt{nlm} takes internally.

\textbf{Trust regions.} Maximise the quadratic approximation subject to
\(\|\theta - \theta^{(k)}\| \le \Delta_k\), adapting \(\Delta_k\)
according to how well the quadratic predicted the realised change. This
is \texttt{nlminb} and the \texttt{PORT} routines.

\textbf{Reparameterisation and good starts.} Often the cheapest fix.
Centring and scaling covariates improves conditioning of \(J\)
substantially, and for GLMs the null-model fit is a reliable starting
point --- which is what \texttt{glm} uses when \texttt{start} is not
supplied.

\section{Other Multivariate Optimization
Methods}\label{other-multivariate-optimization-methods}

Newton--Raphson is the method of choice when the second derivatives are
available and \(p\) is modest. Outside that regime, three considerations
push toward alternatives: derivatives may be unavailable or painful to
code; the \(O(p^3)\) cost of solving the Newton system and the
\(O(p^2)\) storage of \(J\) become prohibitive for large \(p\); and the
objective may be non-smooth, noisy or multimodal.

\subsection{Classification by derivative
information}\label{classification-by-derivative-information}

Methods are most usefully organised by what they require of the
objective.

\subsection{The main alternatives}\label{the-main-alternatives}

\textbf{Nelder--Mead (simplex).} Maintains \(p+1\) points forming a
simplex and moves it downhill by reflecting, expanding, contracting and
shrinking. It needs no derivatives and tolerates mild non-smoothness,
but converges slowly, degrades badly as \(p\) grows past roughly 10--20,
and can stall at non-stationary points. It is \texttt{optim}'s default,
which makes it by far the most-used and most-overused method in R.
Reasonable as a first look at an unfamiliar objective; a poor choice for
a likelihood you will fit thousands of times.

\textbf{Steepest descent with line search.} Move along \(-\nabla f\),
choosing the distance by a one-dimensional search. Conceptually the
simplest gradient method and the one that best illustrates the role of a
line search, but the convergence is only linear, and the rate
deteriorates with the condition number of the Hessian: on an elongated
valley the iterates zig-zag across it rather than travelling along it.
Rarely used unmodified.

\textbf{Conjugate gradient.} Corrects the zig-zag by choosing each
direction to be conjugate to the previous ones with respect to the local
curvature, without ever forming or storing a Hessian. Storage is
\(O(p)\), which is its defining advantage. Fletcher--Reeves and
Polak--Ribière differ only in the formula for the correction
coefficient. The method is sensitive to the accuracy of the line search
and usually needs periodic restarting.

\textbf{Quasi-Newton (BFGS and L-BFGS).} Builds an approximation to
\(J^{-1}\) from successive gradient differences, so it attains
superlinear convergence with only first derivatives. BFGS stores a full
\(p \times p\) approximation; L-BFGS keeps only the last few update
vectors, reducing storage to \(O(mp)\) and making it the default choice
for high-dimensional smooth problems. For most likelihood problems where
the Hessian is awkward to derive, BFGS with an analytic gradient is the
best practical compromise.

\textbf{Stochastic gradient descent.} For an objective that is a sum
over \(n\) observations, estimate the gradient from a small random
subset (``minibatch'') at each step. The per-iteration cost becomes
independent of \(n\), at the price of a noisy trajectory requiring a
decreasing step-size sequence (\(\sum_k \alpha_k = \infty\),
\(\sum_k \alpha_k^2 < \infty\)). Modern variants --- momentum, AdaGrad,
RMSProp, Adam --- adapt the step size per coordinate. SGD is the
foundation of neural-network fitting and is treated in its own right in
a later chapter; for classical likelihood problems with \(n\) in the
thousands it offers nothing over BFGS.

Suggested approach

\midrule\noalign{}

\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

Hessian derivable, \(p\) small, standard errors wanted & Newton--Raphson

GLM or a model expressible as one & \texttt{glm} (IRLS) \\
Gradient available, \(p\) moderate & BFGS via
\texttt{optim(method\ =\ "BFGS")} \\
Gradient available, \(p\) large & L-BFGS \\
Derivatives unavailable, \(p < 10\) & Nelder--Mead as a first pass, then
refine \\
Objective noisy or non-smooth & Nelder--Mead or simulated annealing \\
Divergence or poor conditioning suspected & \texttt{nlm} or
\texttt{nlminb}; rescale the parameters \\
Objective a sum over very many observations & Stochastic gradient
descent and variants \\
Multiple local maxima suspected & Multiple random starts with any of the
above \\
\end{longtable}

The last row deserves emphasis. Every method in this chapter is a
\emph{local} method: each converges to a stationary point near where it
started, and none can certify that the point found is the global
maximum. Restarting from a dispersed set of initial values and comparing
the attained log-likelihoods is the only general safeguard, and it
should be routine for any likelihood that is not known to be concave.

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{The EM Algorithm}\label{the-em-algorithm}

\section{Overview of EM}\label{overview-of-em}

\subsection{Setup and motivation}\label{setup-and-motivation}

Let \(Y\) denote the observed data and \(\theta\) the parameter, with
likelihood \(L(\theta) = P(Y \mid \theta)\). In many models this
quantity is itself an integral (or sum) over latent or missing data
\(Z\):

\[
P(Y \mid \theta) = \int P(Y, Z \mid \theta) \, dZ .
\]

The integral is what makes direct maximization awkward: it destroys the
product structure that would otherwise make the log-likelihood a sum of
simple terms. The EM algorithm avoids it by working with the
complete-data likelihood, which retains that structure, and correcting
for the fact that \(Z\) is unobserved.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\caption{Observed and complete
likelihoods}\label{tbl-terminology}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Likelihood
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Log-likelihood
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Likelihood
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Log-likelihood
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Observed & \(L_{\text{obs}}(\theta) = P(Y \mid \theta)\) &
\(\ell_{\text{obs}}(\theta) = \log P(Y \mid \theta)\) \\
Complete & \(L_{\text{comp}}(\theta) = P(Y, Z \mid \theta)\) &
\(\ell_{\text{comp}}(\theta; Y, Z) = \log P(Y, Z \mid \theta)\) \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{The algorithm}\label{the-algorithm}

Start from \(\hat\theta^{(0)}\). For \(t = 0, 1, 2, \ldots\):

\textbf{E-step.} Replace \(\ell_{\text{obs}}(\theta)\) by the expected
complete log-likelihood,

\[
Q\!\left(\theta \mid \hat\theta^{(t)}\right)
= E_{Z \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)}}\!\left[\ell_{\text{comp}}(\theta; Y, Z)\right].
\]

\textbf{M-step.} Maximize it,

\[
\hat\theta^{(t+1)} = \arg\max_{\theta} \; Q\!\left(\theta \mid \hat\theta^{(t)}\right).
\]

Iterate until \(\hat\theta^{(t)}\) converges.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{A common misreading}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

\(Q\) is an \emph{average of} \(\ell_{\text{comp}}(\theta; Y, Z)\) over
\(P(Z \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)})\). It is \textbf{not}
\(\ell_{\text{comp}}\!\left(\theta; Y, E(Z \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)})\right)\)
--- that is, EM is not ``fill in the missing values with their
expectations and maximize''. The two coincide only when
\(\ell_{\text{comp}}\) happens to be linear in \(Z\), which is true in
both examples of this chapter and false in general.

\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(Q\) as an average of complete
log-likelihoods}{Q as an average of complete log-likelihoods}}\label{q-as-an-average-of-complete-log-likelihoods}

Fix \(\hat\theta^{(t)}\). Each draw
\(Z^{(s)} \sim P(Z \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)})\) gives one curve
\(\ell_{\text{comp}}(\theta; Y, Z^{(s)})\), and
\(Q(\theta \mid \hat\theta^{(t)})\) is their expectation. The figure
uses the censored Poisson model of Section~\ref{sec-poisson} with
\(\lambda^{(t)} = 1.5\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{\(Q\) functions as tangent lower
bounds}{Q functions as tangent lower bounds}}\label{q-functions-as-tangent-lower-bounds}

The geometric picture that explains why EM works: each
\(Q(\cdot \mid \hat\theta^{(t)})\), shifted by a constant, lies
\textbf{below} \(\ell_{\text{obs}}\) and \textbf{touches} it at
\(\hat\theta^{(t)}\). Maximizing the bound therefore moves uphill on
\(\ell_{\text{obs}}\) itself.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}

\NormalTok{lt }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{; cols }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"darkorange"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"forestgreen"}\NormalTok{)}

\subsection{The ascending property}\label{the-ascending-property}

Write \(h(Y, Z \mid \theta) = P(Y, Z \mid \theta)\),
\(g(Y \mid \theta) = \int h(Y, Z \mid \theta) \, dZ\), and
\(K(Z \mid Y, \theta) = h / g\).

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Theorem}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

\(\ell_{\text{obs}}\!\left(\hat\theta^{(t+1)}\right) \ \ge\ \ell_{\text{obs}}\!\left(\hat\theta^{(t)}\right)\)
at every iteration.

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Key identity.} Since
\(\log g(Y \mid \theta) = \log h(Y, Z \mid \theta) - \log K(Z \mid Y, \theta)\)
holds for every \(Z\), taking \(E_{Z \mid Y, \theta_0}\) of both sides
gives, for any \(\theta_0\),

\[
\ell_{\text{obs}}(\theta)
= \underbrace{E_{Z \mid Y, \theta_0}\!\left[\log h(Y, Z \mid \theta)\right]}_{Q(\theta \mid \theta_0)}
- \underbrace{E_{Z \mid Y, \theta_0}\!\left[\log K(Z \mid Y, \theta)\right]}_{H(\theta \mid \theta_0)} .
\]

The left side does not depend on \(Z\), so the expectation leaves it
unchanged; this is the whole content of the step.

\textbf{Proof.} Set \(\theta_0 = \hat\theta^{(t)}\) and subtract the
identity evaluated at \(\theta = \hat\theta^{(t)}\) from the one
evaluated at \(\theta = \hat\theta^{(t+1)}\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\ell_{\text{obs}}\!\left(\hat\theta^{(t+1)}\right) - \ell_{\text{obs}}\!\left(\hat\theta^{(t)}\right)
&= \underbrace{\left[Q\!\left(\hat\theta^{(t+1)} \mid \hat\theta^{(t)}\right) - Q\!\left(\hat\theta^{(t)} \mid \hat\theta^{(t)}\right)\right]}_{\ge\, 0 \text{ by the M-step}} \\
&\quad - \underbrace{\left[H\!\left(\hat\theta^{(t+1)} \mid \hat\theta^{(t)}\right) - H\!\left(\hat\theta^{(t)} \mid \hat\theta^{(t)}\right)\right]}_{\le\, 0 \text{ by Jensen, below}} .
\end{aligned}
\]

\textbf{The \(H\) term.} We need
\(H(\theta \mid \hat\theta^{(t)}) \le H(\hat\theta^{(t)} \mid \hat\theta^{(t)})\)
for every \(\theta\), that is

\[
E_{Z \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)}}\!\left[\log \frac{K(Z \mid Y, \theta)}{K(Z \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)})}\right] \le 0 .
\]

Because \(\log\) is concave, Jensen's inequality gives
\(E[\log W] \le \log E[W]\), so

\[
\begin{aligned}
E_{Z \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)}}\!\left[\log \frac{K(Z \mid Y, \theta)}{K(Z \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)})}\right]
&\le \log \int \frac{K(Z \mid Y, \theta)}{K(Z \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)})} \, K(Z \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)}) \, dZ \\
&= \log \int K(Z \mid Y, \theta) \, dZ = \log 1 = 0 . \qquad \blacksquare
\end{aligned}
\]

The quantity on the left is
\(-\mathrm{KL}\!\left(K(\cdot \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)}) \,\|\, K(\cdot \mid Y, \theta)\right)\).
Note carefully what the theorem does and does not say: the observed
likelihood never decreases, but that guarantees convergence only to a
stationary point, which may be a local maximum or even a saddle point.

\subsection{EM as alternating
optimization}\label{em-as-alternating-optimization}

There is a second formulation that makes the algorithm's structure
clearer and generalizes better. Let \(\tilde P(\cdot)\) be any
distribution on the hidden data \(Z\), and define

\[
F\!\left(\tilde P, \theta\right)
= \int \tilde P(z) \log P(Y, z \mid \theta) \, dz
- \int \tilde P(z) \log \tilde P(z) \, dz .
\]

Using \(P(Y, z \mid \theta) = P(Y \mid \theta) \, P(z \mid Y, \theta)\),

\[
F\!\left(\tilde P, \theta\right)
= \ell_{\text{obs}}(\theta) - \mathrm{KL}\!\left(\tilde P \,\|\, P(\cdot \mid Y, \theta)\right),
\qquad
\mathrm{KL}(P \| q) = \int P(z) \log \frac{P(z)}{q(z)} \, dz \; \ge\; 0 .
\]

Both steps of EM are now coordinate ascent on the single function \(F\):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{E-step} maximizes \(F\) over \(\tilde P\) with
  \(\theta = \hat\theta^{(t)}\) fixed. Since \(\mathrm{KL} \ge 0\) with
  equality if and only if
  \(\tilde P = P(\cdot \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)})\), the E-step sets
  \(\tilde P\) to the conditional distribution of \(Z\).
\item
  \textbf{M-step} maximizes \(F\) over \(\theta\) with \(\tilde P\)
  fixed. The entropy term is free of \(\theta\), so this is
  \(\arg\max_\theta Q(\theta \mid \hat\theta^{(t)})\).
\end{itemize}

The ascending property is then immediate: alternating maximization of a
single objective cannot decrease it. This view also explains the
variational methods used when the E-step is intractable --- restrict
\(\tilde P\) to a manageable family and accept a bound rather than an
equality.

For completeness, \(\mathrm{KL} \ge 0\) follows from
\(\log x \le x - 1\):

\[
\mathrm{KL}(P \| q) = -\int P(z) \log \frac{q(z)}{P(z)} \, dz
\;\ge\; -\int P(z) \left(\frac{q(z)}{P(z)} - 1\right) dz
= -\int \left[q(z) - P(z)\right] dz = 0 .
\]

\section{Example 1: Two-Component Normal Mixture}\label{sec-mixture}

\subsection{Model and likelihoods}\label{model-and-likelihoods}

\[
Z_i \sim \text{Bern}(p), \qquad
Y_i \mid Z_i = 1 \sim N(\mu_1, 1), \qquad
Y_i \mid Z_i = 0 \sim N(\mu_0, 1) .
\]

Observed: \(y_1, \ldots, y_n\). Hidden: \(z_1, \ldots, z_n\). Parameter
\(\theta = (\mu_0, \mu_1, p)\).

\textbf{Complete likelihood}

\[
P(y, z \mid \theta) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} P(y_i \mid z_i, \theta) \, P(z_i \mid \theta)
= \prod_{i=1}^{n} \varphi(y_i \mid \mu_{z_i}, 1) \, p^{z_i} (1-p)^{1-z_i} .
\]

\textbf{Observed likelihood}, summing out each \(z_i\),

\[
P(y \mid \theta) = \prod_{i=1}^{n}\left[\varphi(y_i \mid \mu_1, 1)\, p + \varphi(y_i \mid \mu_0, 1)(1 - p)\right] .
\]

The sum inside the product is what makes direct maximization awkward.

\subsection{Complete log-likelihood}\label{complete-log-likelihood}

With
\(\log \varphi(y \mid \mu, 1) = -\tfrac{1}{2}(y - \mu)^2 - \log\sqrt{2\pi}\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
\ell_{\text{comp}}(\theta; y, z)
&= \sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[z_i \log p + (1 - z_i)\log(1-p) - \tfrac{1}{2}(y_i - \mu_{z_i})^2\right] + C \\
&= \left(\sum_{i=1}^{n} z_i\right) \log \frac{p}{1-p} + n \log(1-p) \\
&\quad - \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[z_i (y_i - \mu_1)^2 + (1 - z_i)(y_i - \mu_0)^2\right] + C .
\end{aligned}
\]

Two features drive everything that follows. The expression is
\textbf{linear in each \(z_i\)}, so the E-step needs only
\(E(Z_i \mid y_i, \hat\theta^{(t)})\) rather than the full conditional
distribution. And given \(z\), the parameters \textbf{separate}: \(p\)
is a Bernoulli proportion while \(\mu_1\) and \(\mu_0\) are group means.

\subsection{E-step: soft
classification}\label{e-step-soft-classification}

Given
\(\hat\theta^{(t)} = (\hat\mu_0^{(t)}, \hat\mu_1^{(t)}, \hat p^{(t)})\),
Bayes' rule gives

\[
\hat p_i^{(t)} \equiv P\!\left(Z_i = 1 \mid y_i, \hat\theta^{(t)}\right)
= \frac{\varphi\!\left(y_i \mid \hat\mu_1^{(t)}, 1\right) \hat p^{(t)}}
       {\varphi\!\left(y_i \mid \hat\mu_0^{(t)}, 1\right)\!\left(1 - \hat p^{(t)}\right)
        + \varphi\!\left(y_i \mid \hat\mu_1^{(t)}, 1\right) \hat p^{(t)}} ,
\]

and therefore

\[
\begin{aligned}
Q\!\left(\theta \mid \hat\theta^{(t)}\right)
&= \left(\sum_i \hat p_i^{(t)}\right) \log \frac{p}{1-p} + n \log(1-p) \\
&\quad - \frac{1}{2}\sum_i \left[\hat p_i^{(t)} (y_i - \mu_1)^2 + \left(1 - \hat p_i^{(t)}\right)(y_i - \mu_0)^2\right] .
\end{aligned}
\]

Computationally, \(\hat p_i^{(t)}\) must be formed on the log scale.
Writing
\(a_i = \log \varphi(y_i \mid \hat\mu_1^{(t)}, 1) + \log \hat p^{(t)}\)
and
\(b_i = \log \varphi(y_i \mid \hat\mu_0^{(t)}, 1) + \log(1 - \hat p^{(t)})\),
we have \(\hat p_i^{(t)} = \exp\{a_i - \operatorname{lse}(a_i, b_i)\}\)
where \(\operatorname{lse}\) is the log-sum-exp function of the chapter
on computer arithmetic. Forming the ratio directly underflows as soon as
an observation sits far from both means.

\subsection{M-step: weighted proportions and
means}\label{m-step-weighted-proportions-and-means}

\(Q\) separates into one term in \(p\) and two in \(\mu_1\), \(\mu_0\),
each maximized in closed form.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\caption{M-step updates for the two-component
mixture}\label{tbl-mstep-mixture}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Update
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Stationary equation \(\Longrightarrow\) solution
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Update
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Stationary equation \(\Longrightarrow\) solution
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(p\) &
\(\dfrac{\partial Q}{\partial p} = \dfrac{\sum_i \hat p_i^{(t)}}{p(1-p)} - \dfrac{n}{1-p} = 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \hat p^{(t+1)} = \dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} \hat p_i^{(t)}\) \\
\(\mu_1\) &
\(\dfrac{\partial Q}{\partial \mu_1} = \sum_i \hat p_i^{(t)}(y_i - \mu_1) = 0 \;\Longrightarrow\; \hat\mu_1^{(t+1)} = \dfrac{\sum_i \hat p_i^{(t)} y_i}{\sum_i \hat p_i^{(t)}}\) \\
\(\mu_0\) & weights \(1 - \hat p_i^{(t)}\):
\(\;\hat\mu_0^{(t+1)} = \dfrac{\sum_i \left(1 - \hat p_i^{(t)}\right) y_i}{\sum_i \left(1 - \hat p_i^{(t)}\right)}\) \\
\end{longtable}

In words: the M-step is the complete-data MLE with each observation
split between the two groups in proportion to its soft classification.
Return to the E-step with \(\hat\theta^{(t+1)}\) and recompute the
responsibilities.

\subsection{Implementation}\label{implementation-1}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{log\_sum\_exp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(log\_x) \{}
\NormalTok{    max\_log\_x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(log\_x)}
\NormalTok{    max\_log\_x }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(log\_x }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ max\_log\_x)))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# observed log{-}likelihood, theta = (p, mu1, mu0)}
\NormalTok{log\_like\_obs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(theta, Y) \{}
\NormalTok{    log\_joint }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(theta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])     }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(Y, theta[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{], }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{),}
                       \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ theta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(Y, theta[}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{], }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}
    \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{apply}\NormalTok{(log\_joint, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, log\_sum\_exp))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# E{-}step: responsibilities P(Z\_i = 1 | y\_i, theta)}
\NormalTok{e\_step }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(theta, Y) \{}
\NormalTok{    l1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(Y, theta[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{], }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(theta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
\NormalTok{    l0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(Y, theta[}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{], }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ theta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
    \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(l1 }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{apply}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(l1, l0), }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, log\_sum\_exp))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# M{-}step: closed{-}form weighted updates}
\NormalTok{m\_step }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(w, Y) \{}
    \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(w),}
      \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(Y }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ w) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(w),}
      \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(Y }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ w)) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ w))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{em\_mixnorm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(theta0, Y, no\_iters) \{}
\NormalTok{    out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NA\_real\_}\NormalTok{, no\_iters }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}
                  \AttributeTok{dimnames =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"p"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"mu1"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"mu0"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"log\_lik"}\NormalTok{)))}
\NormalTok{    theta }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ theta0}
\NormalTok{    out[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, ] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(theta, }\FunctionTok{log\_like\_obs}\NormalTok{(theta, Y))}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(no\_iters)) \{}
\NormalTok{        theta }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{m\_step}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{e\_step}\NormalTok{(theta, Y), Y)}
\NormalTok{        out[i }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, ] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(theta, }\FunctionTok{log\_like\_obs}\NormalTok{(theta, Y))}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    out}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{gen\_mixnorm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(theta, n) \{}
\NormalTok{    Z }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rbinom}\NormalTok{(n, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, theta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
\NormalTok{    Y }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(n, }\AttributeTok{mean =} \FunctionTok{ifelse}\NormalTok{(Z }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, theta[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{], theta[}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{]), }\AttributeTok{sd =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
    \FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(Y, Z, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{cm.colors}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)[Z }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{,}
         \AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"component label Z"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    Y}
\NormalTok{\}}

\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{812}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{data }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{gen\_mixnorm}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
           p    mu1    mu0   log_lik
 [1,] 0.5000 0.0000 3.0000 -375.3490
 [2,] 0.3377 0.2695 3.0137 -361.6709
 [3,] 0.3242 0.2319 2.9768 -361.3077
 [4,] 0.3154 0.1956 2.9584 -361.1388
 [5,] 0.3094 0.1694 2.9463 -361.0581
 [6,] 0.3054 0.1513 2.9380 -361.0202
 [7,] 0.3026 0.1389 2.9323 -361.0026
 [8,] 0.3007 0.1306 2.9285 -360.9945
 [9,] 0.2995 0.1249 2.9258 -360.9908
[10,] 0.2986 0.1211 2.9240 -360.9891
[11,] 0.2980 0.1185 2.9228 -360.9884
\end{verbatim}

The monotone increase of the last column is the ascending property in
action; it is the single most useful diagnostic when debugging an EM
implementation, since any decrease indicates an error in the E- or
M-step.

\subsection{Interactive demonstration}\label{sec-mixture-app}

The figure below replaces the frame-by-frame animation of earlier
versions of this chapter. Choose a data set and a starting value ---
either from the presets in the left panel or by typing your own --- then
step through the fit with \textbf{Prev} and \textbf{Next}, or press
\textbf{Play} to run it automatically. \textbf{Regenerate data} draws a
fresh sample from the same model, which is the quickest way to see how
much of the algorithm's behaviour is a property of the model and how
much is an accident of one data set. Changing the data, the sample size
or any starting value resets the display to iteration 0.

Upper panel: the two weighted component densities, with each observation
coloured by its current responsibility \(\hat p_i^{(t)}\) and arrows
showing where the M-step moves the two means. Lower panel: the observed
log-likelihood, which must never decrease.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{\#| label: em{-}mixture{-}app}
\NormalTok{\#| standalone: true}
\NormalTok{\#| viewerHeight: 760}

\NormalTok{library(shiny)}

\NormalTok{\#\#\# {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-} model functions {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{log\_sum\_exp \textless{}{-} function(lx) \{ mx \textless{}{-} max(lx); mx + log(sum(exp(lx {-} mx))) \}}

\NormalTok{log\_like\_obs \textless{}{-} function(theta, Y) \{}
\NormalTok{    A \textless{}{-} cbind(log(theta[1])     + dnorm(Y, theta[2], 1, log = TRUE),}
\NormalTok{               log(1 {-} theta[1]) + dnorm(Y, theta[3], 1, log = TRUE))}
\NormalTok{    sum(apply(A, 1, log\_sum\_exp))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{e\_step \textless{}{-} function(theta, Y) \{}
\NormalTok{    l1 \textless{}{-} dnorm(Y, theta[2], 1, log = TRUE) + log(theta[1])}
\NormalTok{    l0 \textless{}{-} dnorm(Y, theta[3], 1, log = TRUE) + log(1 {-} theta[1])}
\NormalTok{    exp(l1 {-} apply(cbind(l1, l0), 1, log\_sum\_exp))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{m\_step \textless{}{-} function(w, Y) \{}
\NormalTok{    c(mean(w), sum(Y * w) / sum(w), sum(Y * (1 {-} w)) / sum(1 {-} w))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{em\_path \textless{}{-} function(theta0, Y, no\_iters) \{}
\NormalTok{    out \textless{}{-} matrix(NA\_real\_, no\_iters + 1, 4,}
\NormalTok{                  dimnames = list(NULL, c("p", "mu1", "mu0", "log\_lik")))}
\NormalTok{    th \textless{}{-} theta0}
\NormalTok{    out[1, ] \textless{}{-} c(th, log\_like\_obs(th, Y))}
\NormalTok{    for (i in seq\_len(no\_iters)) \{}
\NormalTok{        th \textless{}{-} m\_step(e\_step(th, Y), Y)}
\NormalTok{        if (any(!is.finite(th))) break}
\NormalTok{        out[i + 1, ] \textless{}{-} c(th, log\_like\_obs(th, Y))}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    out}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{\#\#\# {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-} preset starting values {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{presets \textless{}{-} list(}
\NormalTok{    "near the truth: (0.5, 0, 3)"                 = c(0.5,  0.0,  3.0),}
\NormalTok{    "far outside the data: (0.5, {-}10, 5)"         = c(0.5, {-}10.0, 5.0),}
\NormalTok{    "both means too high: (0.5, 5, 10)"           = c(0.5,  5.0, 10.0),}
\NormalTok{    "means nearly equal: (0.5, 0.1, 0.2)"         = c(0.5,  0.1,  0.2),}
\NormalTok{    "label{-}switched: (0.5, 3, 0)"                 = c(0.5,  3.0,  0.0),}
\NormalTok{    "random labels"                               = NA,}
\NormalTok{    "manual entry"                                = NULL}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{datasets \textless{}{-} list(}
\NormalTok{    "well separated: p = 0.3, mu = (3, 0)"  = c(0.3, 3, 0),}
\NormalTok{    "widely separated: p = 0.3, mu = (5, 0)" = c(0.3, 5, 0),}
\NormalTok{    "overlapping: p = 0.3, mu = (1.5, 0)"    = c(0.3, 1.5, 0),}
\NormalTok{    "balanced and overlapping: p = 0.5, mu = (1.5, 0)" = c(0.5, 1.5, 0)}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{\#\#\# {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-} UI {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{ui \textless{}{-} fluidPage(}
\NormalTok{    sidebarLayout(}
\NormalTok{        sidebarPanel(}
\NormalTok{            width = 4,}
\NormalTok{            h5("Data"),}
\NormalTok{            selectInput("dataset", NULL, choices = names(datasets)),}
\NormalTok{            sliderInput("n", "sample size", 50, 400, 200, step = 50),}
\NormalTok{            actionButton("newdata", "Regenerate data", width = "100\%"),}
\NormalTok{            tags$hr(),}
\NormalTok{            h5("Starting value"),}
\NormalTok{            selectInput("preset", NULL, choices = names(presets),}
\NormalTok{                        selected = "far outside the data: (0.5, {-}10, 5)"),}
\NormalTok{            fluidRow(}
\NormalTok{                column(4, numericInput("p0",   "p",       0.5, min = 0.01,}
\NormalTok{                                       max = 0.99, step = 0.05)),}
\NormalTok{                column(4, numericInput("mu10", "mu1",   {-}10,   step = 0.5)),}
\NormalTok{                column(4, numericInput("mu00", "mu0",     5,   step = 0.5))}
\NormalTok{            ),}
\NormalTok{            helpText("Selecting a preset fills the three boxes. The boxes are ",}
\NormalTok{                     "always live: edit them to use your own values."),}
\NormalTok{            tags$hr(),}
\NormalTok{            sliderInput("maxit", "iterations to run", 5, 60, 30, step = 5),}
\NormalTok{            h5(textOutput("stepLabel", inline = TRUE)),}
\NormalTok{            fluidRow(}
\NormalTok{                column(4, actionButton("prv",  "\textbackslash{}u25c0 Prev", width = "100\%")),}
\NormalTok{                column(4, actionButton("nxt",  "Next \textbackslash{}u25b6", width = "100\%")),}
\NormalTok{                column(4, actionButton("play", "Play",         width = "100\%"))}
\NormalTok{            ),}
\NormalTok{            tags$br(),}
\NormalTok{            tableOutput("tab")}
\NormalTok{        ),}
\NormalTok{        mainPanel(}
\NormalTok{            width = 8,}
\NormalTok{            plotOutput("fit",   height = "400px"),}
\NormalTok{            plotOutput("trace", height = "260px")}
\NormalTok{        )}
\NormalTok{    )}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{\#\#\# {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-} server {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{server \textless{}{-} function(input, output, session) \{}

\NormalTok{    seed \textless{}{-} reactiveVal(812)}
\NormalTok{    observeEvent(input$newdata, seed(seed() + 1))}

\NormalTok{    Y \textless{}{-} reactive(\{}
\NormalTok{        s \textless{}{-} datasets[[input$dataset]]}
\NormalTok{        set.seed(seed())}
\NormalTok{        Z \textless{}{-} rbinom(input$n, 1, s[1])}
\NormalTok{        rnorm(input$n, mean = ifelse(Z == 1, s[2], s[3]), sd = 1)}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    \#\# preset {-}\textgreater{} fill the three boxes}
\NormalTok{    observeEvent(list(input$preset, input$dataset, input$n, seed()), \{}
\NormalTok{        pr \textless{}{-} presets[[input$preset]]}
\NormalTok{        if (is.null(pr)) return(invisible(NULL))          \# manual entry}
\NormalTok{        if (length(pr) == 1 \&\& is.na(pr)) \{               \# random labels}
\NormalTok{            y \textless{}{-} Y()}
\NormalTok{            lab \textless{}{-} rbinom(length(y), 1, 0.5)}
\NormalTok{            pr \textless{}{-} c(0.5, mean(y[lab == 1]), mean(y[lab == 0]))}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{        updateNumericInput(session, "p0",   value = round(pr[1], 3))}
\NormalTok{        updateNumericInput(session, "mu10", value = round(pr[2], 3))}
\NormalTok{        updateNumericInput(session, "mu00", value = round(pr[3], 3))}
\NormalTok{    \}, ignoreInit = FALSE)}

\NormalTok{    \#\# {-}{-}{-}{-} iteration state driven by the three buttons {-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{    t\_cur   \textless{}{-} reactiveVal(0)      \# iteration currently displayed}
\NormalTok{    playing \textless{}{-} reactiveVal(FALSE)}

\NormalTok{    stop\_play \textless{}{-} function() \{}
\NormalTok{        playing(FALSE)}
\NormalTok{        updateActionButton(session, "play", label = "Play")}
\NormalTok{    \}}

\NormalTok{    \#\# any change to the data or the starting value restarts the fit}
\NormalTok{    observeEvent(list(input$dataset, input$n, seed(), input$maxit,}
\NormalTok{                      input$p0, input$mu10, input$mu00), \{}
\NormalTok{        t\_cur(0)}
\NormalTok{        stop\_play()}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    observeEvent(input$nxt, \{}
\NormalTok{        stop\_play()}
\NormalTok{        t\_cur(min(t\_cur() + 1, input$maxit))}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    observeEvent(input$prv, \{}
\NormalTok{        stop\_play()}
\NormalTok{        t\_cur(max(t\_cur() {-} 1, 0))}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    observeEvent(input$play, \{}
\NormalTok{        if (playing()) \{}
\NormalTok{            stop\_play()}
\NormalTok{        \} else \{}
\NormalTok{            if (t\_cur() \textgreater{}= input$maxit) t\_cur(0)   \# replay from the start}
\NormalTok{            playing(TRUE)}
\NormalTok{            updateActionButton(session, "play", label = "Pause")}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    \#\# the timer: advances one iteration per tick while playing}
\NormalTok{    observe(\{}
\NormalTok{        if (!playing()) return(NULL)}
\NormalTok{        invalidateLater(700, session)}
\NormalTok{        isolate(\{}
\NormalTok{            if (t\_cur() \textgreater{}= input$maxit) stop\_play() else t\_cur(t\_cur() + 1)}
\NormalTok{        \})}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$stepLabel \textless{}{-} renderText(}
\NormalTok{        sprintf("Iteration \%d of \%d", t\_cur(), input$maxit)}
\NormalTok{    )}

\NormalTok{    theta0 \textless{}{-} reactive(\{}
\NormalTok{        p \textless{}{-} input$p0}
\NormalTok{        validate(need(is.finite(p) \&\& p \textgreater{} 0 \&\& p \textless{} 1, "p must lie strictly between 0 and 1"),}
\NormalTok{                 need(is.finite(input$mu10) \&\& is.finite(input$mu00),}
\NormalTok{                      "both means must be numeric"))}
\NormalTok{        c(p, input$mu10, input$mu00)}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    path \textless{}{-} reactive(em\_path(theta0(), Y(), input$maxit))}

\NormalTok{    output$fit \textless{}{-} renderPlot(\{}
\NormalTok{        y \textless{}{-} Y(); P \textless{}{-} path()}
\NormalTok{        k  \textless{}{-} min(t\_cur() + 1, nrow(P))}
\NormalTok{        th \textless{}{-} P[k, 1:3]}
\NormalTok{        validate(need(all(is.finite(th)), "the iteration broke down numerically"))}
\NormalTok{        w  \textless{}{-} e\_step(th, y)}
\NormalTok{        cw \textless{}{-} cm.colors(100)}

\NormalTok{        xr \textless{}{-} range(y, th[2:3]) + c({-}2, 2)}
\NormalTok{        xg \textless{}{-} seq(xr[1], xr[2], length.out = 400)}
\NormalTok{        d1 \textless{}{-} dnorm(xg, th[2]) * th[1]}
\NormalTok{        d0 \textless{}{-} dnorm(xg, th[3]) * (1 {-} th[1])}

\NormalTok{        par(mar = c(4, 4, 3, 1))}
\NormalTok{        plot(xg, d1, type = "l", lwd = 2, col = cw[100],}
\NormalTok{             ylim = c(0, max(0.42, d1, d0)), xlab = "y", ylab = "weighted density",}
\NormalTok{             main = sprintf("step \%d:  p = \%.3f,  mu1 = \%.3f,  mu0 = \%.3f,  loglik = \%.2f",}
\NormalTok{                            k {-} 1, th[1], th[2], th[3], P[k, 4]))}
\NormalTok{        lines(xg, d0, lwd = 2, col = cw[1])}
\NormalTok{        points(y, rep(0, length(y)), col = cw[pmax(1, ceiling(w * 100))], pch = 19,}
\NormalTok{               cex = 0.7)}
\NormalTok{        if (k \textless{} nrow(P) \&\& all(is.finite(P[k + 1, 1:3]))) \{}
\NormalTok{            arrows(th[2], 0.02, P[k + 1, 2], 0.02, length = 0.08, lwd = 2, col = cw[100])}
\NormalTok{            arrows(th[3], 0.02, P[k + 1, 3], 0.02, length = 0.08, lwd = 2, col = cw[1])}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{        legend("topright", bty = "n", lwd = 2, col = c(cw[100], cw[1]),}
\NormalTok{               legend = c(expression(hat(p) * phi(y * "|" * hat(mu)[1])),}
\NormalTok{                          expression((1 {-} hat(p)) * phi(y * "|" * hat(mu)[0]))))}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$trace \textless{}{-} renderPlot(\{}
\NormalTok{        P \textless{}{-} path(); k \textless{}{-} min(t\_cur() + 1, nrow(P))}
\NormalTok{        par(mfrow = c(1, 2), mar = c(4, 4, 2, 1))}
\NormalTok{        it \textless{}{-} 0:(nrow(P) {-} 1)}
\NormalTok{        plot(it, P[, 4], type = "n", xlab = "iteration",}
\NormalTok{             ylab = "observed log{-}likelihood", main = "must never decrease")}
\NormalTok{        lines(it[1:k], P[1:k, 4], type = "b", pch = 19, col = "firebrick")}
\NormalTok{        yl \textless{}{-} range(P[, 2:3], na.rm = TRUE)}
\NormalTok{        plot(it, P[, 2], type = "n", ylim = yl, xlab = "iteration",}
\NormalTok{             ylab = "component means", main = "parameter paths")}
\NormalTok{        lines(it[1:k], P[1:k, 2], type = "b", pch = 19, col = "firebrick")}
\NormalTok{        lines(it[1:k], P[1:k, 3], type = "b", pch = 19, col = "steelblue")}
\NormalTok{        legend("right", bty = "n", lwd = 2, col = c("firebrick", "steelblue"),}
\NormalTok{               legend = c(expression(hat(mu)[1]), expression(hat(mu)[0])))}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$tab \textless{}{-} renderTable(\{}
\NormalTok{        P \textless{}{-} path(); k \textless{}{-} min(t\_cur() + 1, nrow(P))}
\NormalTok{        d \textless{}{-} as.data.frame(P[seq\_len(k), , drop = FALSE])}
\NormalTok{        d$iter \textless{}{-} seq\_len(k) {-} 1}
\NormalTok{        tail(d[, c("iter", "p", "mu1", "mu0", "log\_lik")], 5)}
\NormalTok{    \}, digits = 4, rownames = FALSE)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{shinyApp(ui, server)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Four behaviours are worth producing deliberately.

\textbf{Slow drift from a distant start.} With \((0.5, -10, 5)\) both
means begin far outside the data. The early iterations move them very
little, because every observation is assigned almost the same
responsibility; progress accelerates only once one mean reaches the
data.

\textbf{Label switching.} The starts \((0.5, 0, 3)\) and \((0.5, 3, 0)\)
converge to the same fitted mixture with the roles of the two components
exchanged. The likelihood is invariant to this relabelling, so the
fitted density is identical while the parameter estimates differ --- a
genuine identifiability problem for the model, not a defect of the
algorithm.

\textbf{Stalling at a symmetric point.} With \(\mu_1^{(0)} = 0.1\) and
\(\mu_0^{(0)} = 0.2\) the two components are nearly indistinguishable,
every responsibility is close to \(\hat p^{(0)}\), and the algorithm
creeps away from the one-component solution very slowly. That
configuration is close to a saddle point of \(\ell_{\text{obs}}\).

\textbf{Separation and speed.} Switch the data set from
\(\mu = (1.5, 0)\) to \(\mu = (5, 0)\). Well-separated components make
the responsibilities nearly \(0\) or \(1\), the E-step becomes almost a
hard classification, and convergence takes a handful of iterations
rather than dozens. This is the practical face of the general result
that EM's linear convergence rate is governed by the fraction of missing
information.

\subsection{Comparison with general-purpose
optimizers}\label{comparison-with-general-purpose-optimizers}

EM is not the only way to maximize the observed likelihood. Applying a
general-purpose optimizer directly requires attention to the parameter
space: \(p\) must lie in \((0,1)\), so we reparameterize with the logit.

\NormalTok{    theta }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ ttheta}
\NormalTok{    theta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ttheta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]))}
    \FunctionTok{neg\_loglike\_obs}\NormalTok{(theta, Y)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{lp0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.3} \SpecialCharTok{/} \FloatTok{0.7}\NormalTok{)}

\end{verbatim}

Applied to the observed log-likelihood, these algorithms are more
fragile than EM when the initial values are poorly chosen: the
untransformed runs can step outside \(p \in (0,1)\) and produce
\texttt{NaN}, and the gradient-based methods can stall in the flat
region that a distant start creates. Nelder--Mead is the most stable of
the three here, at the cost of many more function evaluations.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\caption{EM compared with direct numerical
maximization}\label{tbl-em-vs-optim}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
EM
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
General optimizer on \(\ell_{\text{obs}}\)
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\endlastfoot
Constraints & Respected automatically by the M-step & Require
reparameterization \\
Monotonicity & Guaranteed; a decrease signals a coding error & None; a
bad step can be accepted \\
Derivatives & None needed & Gradient helpful, Hessian better \\
Convergence rate & Linear, rate set by the missing information &
Superlinear or quadratic near the optimum \\
Standard errors & Not produced; need Louis' method or a bootstrap &
Hessian available directly \\
Behaviour from poor starts & Slow but stable & Can fail outright \\
\end{longtable}

The last row is the practical trade. EM rarely fails and rarely needs
tuning, which is why it dominates in mixture and latent-variable
modelling; it is also slow near the optimum and gives no standard
errors, which is why a hybrid --- EM to get close, then a Newton step to
polish and to obtain the Hessian --- is common in production code.

\section{Example 2: Censored Poisson Data}\label{sec-poisson}

\subsection{Truncated distributions in
brief}\label{truncated-distributions-in-brief}

Let \(X \sim f(x)\) and \(Y = I(X > c)\). Then
\(Y \sim \text{Bern}\{P(X > c)\}\), and the conditional distribution of
\(X\) given \(Y\) is \(f\) restricted to one side of \(c\) and
renormalized:

\[
f(x \mid Y = 1) = \frac{f(x)\, I(x > c)}{\int_c^{\infty} f(t)\,dt},
\qquad
f(x \mid Y = 0) = \frac{f(x)\, I(x \le c)}{\int_{-\infty}^{c} f(t)\,dt} .
\]

For discrete \(X\) the integrals are sums. This is exactly the
distribution the E-step needs when an observation is known only to lie
on one side of \(c\).

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit07_em_files/figure-pdf/fig-truncation-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-truncation}A density split at a cut point, and the
two renormalized conditional densities.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Data and likelihoods}\label{data-and-likelihoods}

Let \(y_i \overset{iid}{\sim} \text{Pois}(\lambda)\) for
\(i = 1, \ldots, n\). The first \(m\) values are observed exactly; for
\(i > m\) we know only that \(y_i < 2\), recorded as
\(x_i = I(y_i < 2) = 1\).

\textbf{Observed data}: \(y_1, \ldots, y_m\) together with
\(x_{m+1} = \cdots = x_n = 1\). \textbf{Missing}:
\(y_{m+1}, \ldots, y_n\).

\textbf{Complete likelihood}

\[
\prod_{i=1}^{n} f^{\text{Pois}}(y_i \mid \lambda) \cdot \prod_{i=m+1}^{n} I(y_i < 2) .
\]

\textbf{Observed likelihood}

\[
\prod_{i=1}^{m} f^{\text{Pois}}(y_i \mid \lambda) \cdot \prod_{i=m+1}^{n} P(y_i < 2 \mid \lambda),
\qquad P(y_i < 2 \mid \lambda) = e^{-\lambda}(1 + \lambda) .
\]

Taking logarithms and dropping constants,

\[
\ell_{\text{obs}}(\lambda) = m \bar y_{1:m} \log \lambda - n \lambda + (n - m)\log(1 + \lambda) .
\]

The second factor makes the MLE nonlinear in \(\lambda\); EM sidesteps
it.

\subsection{E-step}\label{e-step}

\[
\ell_{\text{comp}}(\lambda) = -n\lambda + (\log \lambda) \sum_{i=1}^{m} y_i
+ (\log \lambda) \underbrace{\sum_{i=m+1}^{n} y_i}_{\text{missing}}
- \sum_{i=1}^{n} \log(y_i!) .
\]

For \(i > m\) we have \(y_i \in \{0, 1\}\), and the Poisson pmf
truncated to that set given \(\lambda^{(t)}\) is

\[
P\!\left(y_i = 0 \mid x_i = 1\right) = \frac{1}{1 + \lambda^{(t)}},
\qquad
P\!\left(y_i = 1 \mid x_i = 1\right) = \frac{\lambda^{(t)}}{1 + \lambda^{(t)}} ,
\]

so that

\[
\hat y^{(t)} \equiv E\!\left(y_i \mid x_i = 1, \lambda^{(t)}\right) = \frac{\lambda^{(t)}}{1 + \lambda^{(t)}} .
\]

Since \(y_i! = 1\) for \(y_i \in \{0, 1\}\), the \(\log(y_i!)\) term is
a known constant and no further expectation is needed.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)); lt }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{; k }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{0}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{6}
\FunctionTok{barplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{dpois}\NormalTok{(k, lt), }\AttributeTok{names.arg =}\NormalTok{ k,}
        \AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"gray80"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)),}
        \AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"y"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"probability"}\NormalTok{,}
        \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{bquote}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Pois("} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ lambda}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{(t) }\SpecialCharTok{==}\NormalTok{ .(lt) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \StringTok{")"}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{barplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, lt) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ lt), }\AttributeTok{names.arg =} \DecValTok{0}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{,}
        \AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"y"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"probability"}\NormalTok{,}
        \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"truncated to \{0, 1\}: "} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{P}\NormalTok{(y[i]}\SpecialCharTok{*}\StringTok{"|"}\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{x[i] }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

}

\caption{\label{fig-poisson-estep}The conditional distribution of a
censored count is the Poisson pmf restricted to \(\{0,1\}\) and
renormalized.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{M-step and the EM update}\label{m-step-and-the-em-update}

\[
Q\!\left(\lambda \mid \lambda^{(t)}\right)
= -n\lambda + (\log \lambda)\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m} y_i + (n - m)\,\hat y^{(t)}\right] + C^{(t)} ,
\]

\[
\frac{\partial Q}{\partial \lambda}
= -n + \frac{1}{\lambda}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m} y_i + (n - m)\,\hat y^{(t)}\right] = 0 ,
\]

\[
\Longrightarrow \quad
\lambda^{(t+1)} = \frac{m\, \bar y_{1:m} + (n - m)\dfrac{\lambda^{(t)}}{1 + \lambda^{(t)}}}{n} .
\]

The update is the ordinary Poisson MLE --- the sample mean --- with each
censored value replaced by its conditional expectation. As warned in the
overview, this works \emph{only} because \(\ell_{\text{comp}}\) is
linear in the missing \(y_i\).

\subsection{Implementation}\label{implementation-2}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# observed log{-}likelihood, up to an additive constant}
\DocumentationTok{\#\#   m    : number of exactly observed counts}
\DocumentationTok{\#\#   ybar : their mean}
\DocumentationTok{\#\#   ncen : number of counts known only to be \textless{} 2}
\NormalTok{loglik\_obs\_pois }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(m, ybar, ncen, lambda) \{}
\NormalTok{    m }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ ybar }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(lambda) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ (m }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ ncen) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ lambda }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ ncen }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ lambda)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{em\_censored\_poisson }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(m, ybar, ncen,}
                                \AttributeTok{lambda0 =}\NormalTok{ (m }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ ybar }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ ncen) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (m }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ ncen),}
                                \AttributeTok{iterations =} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NA\_real\_}\NormalTok{, iterations }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{,}
                  \AttributeTok{dimnames =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"lambda"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"log\_lik"}\NormalTok{)))}
\NormalTok{    lambda }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ lambda0}
\NormalTok{    out[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, ] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(lambda, }\FunctionTok{loglik\_obs\_pois}\NormalTok{(m, ybar, ncen, lambda))}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(iterations)) \{}
\NormalTok{        y\_mis  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ lambda }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ lambda)                       }\CommentTok{\# E{-}step}
\NormalTok{        lambda }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ (m }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ ybar }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ ncen }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ y\_mis) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (m }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ ncen)       }\CommentTok{\# M{-}step}
\NormalTok{        out[i }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, ] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(lambda, }\FunctionTok{loglik\_obs\_pois}\NormalTok{(m, ybar, ncen, lambda))}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    out}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{812}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{y    }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rpois}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ncen }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(y }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{m    }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(y) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ ncen}
\NormalTok{ybar }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(y[y }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{])}
\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{m =}\NormalTok{ m, }\AttributeTok{ncen =}\NormalTok{ ncen, }\AttributeTok{ybar\_observed =}\NormalTok{ ybar)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
            m          ncen ybar_observed 
   157.000000     43.000000      3.528662 
\end{verbatim}

Discarding the censored observations and averaging the rest gives an
estimate clearly above the true mean of \(3\): conditioning on
\(y \ge 2\) removes the small counts and biases the mean upward. EM
recovers them.

Both starting values reach the same limit, and the log-likelihood
increases monotonically from each. Note how slowly the run from
\(\lambda^{(0)} = 100\) descends at first --- a single-parameter
illustration of the same slow-drift behaviour seen in the mixture app.

With one parameter and a smooth concave objective this is an easy
problem for any method; the contrast with the mixture example is
instructive, since there the multimodality and the constraint on \(p\)
were what made the general optimizers fragile.

\section{More Complex Applications}\label{more-complex-applications}

\subsection{Model-based clustering}\label{model-based-clustering}

\[
Z_i \sim \text{Categorical}(\pi_1, \ldots, \pi_K),
\qquad Y_i \mid Z_i = k \sim N_d(\mu_k, \Sigma_k) .
\]

This is the multivariate, \(K\)-component generalization of
Section~\ref{sec-mixture}. The E-step computes soft cluster memberships
\(P(Z_i = k \mid y_i, \hat\theta^{(t)})\); the M-step returns weighted
means, covariances and proportions. The \texttt{mclust} package
implements this with a family of constrained covariance structures.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}

\subsection{Hidden Markov models}\label{hidden-markov-models}

Hidden states \(h_1 \to h_2 \to \cdots \to h_n\) form a Markov chain,
and each observation depends only on its own state:

\[
P(y_{1:n} \mid h_{1:n}, \theta) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} P(y_i \mid h_i, \theta) .
\]

The E-step is the forward--backward algorithm, which computes
\(P(h_i \mid y_{1:n}, \hat\theta^{(t)})\) and
\(P(h_i, h_{i+1} \mid y_{1:n}, \hat\theta^{(t)})\) without enumerating
all \(K^n\) state sequences; the M-step produces weighted transition and
emission estimates. The combination is known as the Baum--Welch
algorithm, and it is the clearest example of an E-step that is itself a
nontrivial computation.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)); N }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{120}
\NormalTok{Pm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.95}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.9}\NormalTok{), }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{byrow =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{h }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{integer}\NormalTok{(N); h[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{N) h[i] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sample}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{prob =}\NormalTok{ Pm[h[i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], ])}
\NormalTok{yy }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(N, }\AttributeTok{mean =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)[h])}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(yy, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"i"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(y[i]))}
\NormalTok{r }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rle}\NormalTok{(h); e }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{cumsum}\NormalTok{(r}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{lengths); s }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{head}\NormalTok{(e, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{rect}\NormalTok{(s }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"usr"}\NormalTok{)[}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{], e }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"usr"}\NormalTok{)[}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{],}
     \AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{adjustcolor}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{)[r}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{values], }\FloatTok{0.15}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{border =} \ConstantTok{NA}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{lines}\NormalTok{(yy, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"gray30"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(yy, }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{)[h], }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{0.7}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{legend}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"topright"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{bty =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{),}
       \AttributeTok{legend =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(h[i] }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(h[i] }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)), }\AttributeTok{horiz =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}
