\subsection{Factor analysis and the common
pattern}\label{factor-analysis-and-the-common-pattern}

\[
y_i = \Gamma x_i + \varepsilon_i,
\qquad x_i \sim N_q(0, I) \text{ hidden},
\qquad \varepsilon_i \sim N_d(0, \Psi) .
\]

The E-step uses the fact that \(x_i \mid y_i, \hat\theta^{(t)}\) is
normal with closed-form mean and covariance; the M-step is a regression
of \(y\) on the expected factors.

Across all these applications the recipe is the same:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Introduce a hidden variable that makes the complete-data model
  tractable.
\item
  \textbf{E-step}: compute the conditional distribution of the hidden
  data, or just the expected sufficient statistics if
  \(\ell_{\text{comp}}\) is linear in them.
\item
  \textbf{M-step}: compute the complete-data MLE with those expected
  sufficient statistics in place of the unobserved ones.
\end{enumerate}

The art is entirely in step 1. The same observed-data model often admits
several choices of \(Z\), and they lead to EM algorithms with quite
different convergence rates --- faster when the augmentation adds little
information, slower when the missing information is a large fraction of
the total.

\section{Summary}\label{summary-1}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  EM maximizes \(\ell_{\text{obs}}\) by alternating between forming
  \(Q(\theta \mid \hat\theta^{(t)}) = E_{Z \mid Y, \hat\theta^{(t)}}[\ell_{\text{comp}}]\)
  and maximizing it.
\item
  Every iteration weakly increases \(\ell_{\text{obs}}\). Equivalently,
  EM is coordinate ascent on
  \(F(\tilde P, \theta) = \ell_{\text{obs}}(\theta) - \mathrm{KL}(\tilde P \,\|\, P(Z \mid Y, \theta))\).
\item
  When \(\ell_{\text{comp}}\) is linear in sufficient statistics of
  \(Z\), the E-step reduces to computing their conditional expectations.
  Otherwise the full conditional distribution is required, and ``plug in
  the expectation'' is wrong.
\item
  Convergence is linear, often slow, and only to a stationary point. Run
  from several starting values and compare the attained log-likelihoods.
\item
  Standard errors are not a by-product. Use Louis' identity, the SEM
  algorithm, a bootstrap, or a final Newton step.
\end{itemize}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Numerical Quadrature}\label{numerical-quadrature}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(metRology)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The previous two chapters found the mode of a log-likelihood or
log-posterior by optimization. Many Bayesian calculations instead need
an \emph{area}: a tail probability, a posterior expectation, or the
normalizing constant of a posterior density. This chapter develops
\textbf{numerical quadrature} --- approximating an integral by a
weighted sum of function values at a finite set of points --- and
applies it to the central example of the rest of the book, the marginal
likelihood of a Bayesian model. The next chapter (Laplace's method)
revisits the same example with a method based on the mode instead of a
grid.

\section{The Problem and
Transformations}\label{the-problem-and-transformations}

\subsection{The general setup}\label{the-general-setup}

The goal is to approximate \[
A = \int_a^b f(x)\,dx
\] for finite \(a,b\) by evaluating \(f\) at \(n\) points
\(x_1,\ldots,x_n\) and forming a weighted sum \[
\tilde A_n = \sum_{i=1}^n w_i\, f(x_i).
\] The quadrature rules below differ only in how the points and weights
are chosen. Typical statistical uses are probabilities \(P(a < X < b)\),
expectations \(E[h(X)]\), and normalizing constants such as the marginal
likelihood \(P(y) = \int L(\theta)\pi(\theta)\,d\theta\) in Bayesian
inference.

\subsection{Infinite ranges: the logistic
transformation}\label{infinite-ranges-the-logistic-transformation}

A grid needs a finite interval, so when the natural range of integration
is \((-\infty,\infty)\) we first change variables. The logistic function
maps \(x \in (-\infty,\infty)\) to \(y \in (0,1)\), \[
y = \frac{1}{1+e^{-x}}, \qquad x = t(y) = \log\frac{y}{1-y}, \qquad
t'(y) = \frac{1}{y(1-y)},
\] and the standard change-of-variables identity gives \[
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} f(x)\,dx
 = \int_0^1 f\big(t(y)\big)\,t'(y)\,dy
 = \int_0^1 g(y)\,dy, \qquad
g(y) = \frac{f\big(\log\frac{y}{1-y}\big)}{y(1-y)}.
\] Any quadrature rule for a finite interval now applies to \(g\) on
\((0,1)\). Two consequences matter later in the chapter:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Points equally spaced in \(y\) correspond to points in \(x\) that are
  dense near \(x=0\) and increasingly sparse in the tails --- the grid
  automatically concentrates effort where the logistic curve is steep.
\item
  The factor \(1/\{y(1-y)\}\) blows up at \(y=0\) and \(y=1\), so \(g\)
  is typically undefined at the endpoints even when \(f\) is perfectly
  well behaved. This is the reason the midpoint rule, which never
  evaluates the integrand at the endpoints of a panel, is the natural
  choice here.
\end{itemize}

For a half-line \((0,\infty)\) the same idea applies with
\(x = -\log(1-y)\) or \(x = y/(1-y)\) in place of the logistic map.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}

\FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{x)), }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"x"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"y"}\NormalTok{,}
      \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(y }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ e}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}{-}}\NormalTok{x)))}
\NormalTok{ys }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.95}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =} \FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{xs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(ys }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ ys))}
\FunctionTok{segments}\NormalTok{(xs, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, xs, ys, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"gray50"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{segments}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, ys, xs, ys, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"gray50"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(xs, ys, }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{f }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\FloatTok{0.6} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FloatTok{0.4} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(f, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"x"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"f(x)"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"original integrand"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{xs2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{400}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{polygon}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(xs2, }\FunctionTok{rev}\NormalTok{(xs2)), }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{f}\NormalTok{(xs2), }\FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{400}\NormalTok{)), }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{adjustcolor}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{border =} \ConstantTok{NA}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{rug}\NormalTok{(xs, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(g, }\FloatTok{0.001}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.999}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{501}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"y"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"g(y)"}\NormalTok{,}
      \AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"transformed integrand on (0, 1)"}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{polygon}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(ys2, }\FunctionTok{rev}\NormalTok{(ys2)), }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{g}\NormalTok{(ys2), }\FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{400}\NormalTok{)), }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{adjustcolor}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{border =} \ConstantTok{NA}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{rug}\NormalTok{(ys, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}

\caption{\label{fig-logistic-transform}Equally spaced points in \(y\)

\subsection{Probabilities as a special
case}\label{probabilities-as-a-special-case}

For \(X\) with density \(\varphi\),
e.g.~\(\varphi(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^2/2}\), \[
P(a < X < b) = \int_a^b \varphi(x)\,dx = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \varphi^{(c)}(x)\,dx,
\qquad \varphi^{(c)}(x) = \varphi(x)\,I(a < x < b).
\] Either integrate \(\varphi\) directly over the finite interval
\([a,b]\), or integrate the \emph{truncated} function \(\varphi^{(c)}\)
over the whole line, letting the indicator do the cutting. The second
view is the more useful one: the same machinery --- transform to
\((0,1)\), apply a quadrature rule --- computes \(P(X \in S)\) for an
arbitrary set \(S\) and, more generally, \(E[h(X)] = \int
h(x)\varphi(x)\,dx\) for an arbitrary \(h\).

\section{Quadrature Rules on a Finite
Interval}\label{quadrature-rules-on-a-finite-interval}

All three rules below partition \([a,b]\) into \(n\) panels of width
\(h_n =
(b-a)/n\) and differ only in how \(f\) is approximated \emph{within}
each panel.

\subsection{Midpoint rule}\label{midpoint-rule}

Approximate \(f\) on each panel by its value at the panel's midpoint (a
constant, i.e.~a rectangle): \[
\tilde A_n = h_n \sum_{i=1}^n f\!\Big(a + \big(i - \tfrac12\big)h_n\Big).
\] For smooth \(f\) the error is
\(A - \tilde A_n = \dfrac{(b-a)h_n^2}{24}f''(\xi) =
O(h_n^2)\) for some \(\xi \in (a,b)\). The rule never evaluates \(f\) at
the panel edges, so in particular it never touches \(a\) or \(b\) ---
convenient when \(f\) is singular or undefined at the endpoints, exactly
the situation created by the logistic transform above.

\subsection{Trapezoidal rule}\label{trapezoidal-rule}

Approximate \(f\) on \([x_{i-1},x_i]\) by the straight line through the
two endpoint values, so each panel contributes a trapezoid,
\(\tfrac12[f(x_{i-1})+f(x_i)](x_i-x_{i-1})\). Summing over panels,
interior points are shared by two neighbouring panels and each
contributes once (not twice) to the total: \[
\tilde A_n = \tfrac12 h_n\big[f(a) + f(b)\big] + h_n \sum_{i=1}^{n-1} f(a+ih_n).
\] The error is
\(A - \tilde A_n = -\dfrac{(b-a)h_n^2}{12}f''(\xi) = O(h_n^2)\) --- the
same order as the midpoint rule, but with twice the constant and the
opposite sign. It is exact for linear \(f\).

\subsection{Simpson's rule}\label{simpsons-rule}

Take \(n = 2m\) even. On each \emph{pair} of panels \([x_0,x_1]\) with
\(x_1-x_0=2h_n\), replace \(f\) by the quadratic through the two
endpoints and the midpoint; integrating that quadratic exactly gives \[
\int_{x_0}^{x_1} f(x)\,dx \approx \frac{x_1-x_0}{6}
\Big[f(x_0) + 4f\big(\tfrac{x_0+x_1}{2}\big) + f(x_1)\Big].
\] Summing over the \(m\) panel-pairs, with each even interior point
shared by two pairs, \[
\tilde A_{2m} = \frac{h_n}{3}\left[f(a) + f(b)
 + 4\sum_{i=1}^{m} f\big(a+(2i-1)h_n\big)
 + 2\sum_{i=1}^{m-1} f\big(a+2ih_n\big)\right].
\] The error is
\(A - \tilde A_{2m} = -\dfrac{(b-a)h_n^4}{180}f^{(4)}(\xi) =
O(h_n^4)\), two orders faster than the other two rules, and Simpson's
rule is exact for cubics. It can also be written as a weighted average
of the other two rules on the same panel pair:
\(\text{Simpson} = \tfrac23\,\text{midpoint} +
\tfrac13\,\text{trapezoid}\).

Table~\ref{tbl-rules-compared} summarizes the three rules.

accuracy}\label{implementation-and-accuracy}

All three rules are one line of vectorized R each --- no explicit loop
over panels is needed:

}

\caption{\label{fig-convergence}Absolute error versus number of panels,

rounding error dominates near \(10^{-15}\).}

\end{figure}%

For a smooth, unimodal integrand like a normal density, Simpson's rule
dominates. The rest of this chapter nonetheless uses the
\textbf{midpoint rule}, because the application of interest --- Bayesian
marginal likelihood after a logistic transform --- produces an integrand
that is undefined exactly at the endpoints \(0\) and \(1\), which only
the midpoint rule avoids evaluating.

\section{Application: The Marginal
Likelihood}\label{application-the-marginal-likelihood}

\subsection{Setup}\label{setup}

With likelihood \(L(\theta) = \prod_{i=1}^n P(y_i \mid \theta)\) and
prior \(\pi(\theta)\), Bayes' rule is \[
P(\theta \mid y) = \frac{L(\theta)\pi(\theta)}{P(y)}, \qquad
P(y) = \int L(\theta)\pi(\theta)\,d\theta = E_\pi[L(\theta)].
\] \(P(y)\), the \textbf{marginal likelihood} (the likelihood averaged
over the prior), is the normalizing constant of the posterior and the
basis of Bayes factors for comparing models. It has no closed form
outside conjugate settings, so we approximate it by quadrature. Because
it depends on the \emph{whole} prior --- not just where the likelihood
happens to be large --- its value is far more sensitive to prior choice
than a posterior mean is, as the next figure shows.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{L }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(t) }\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(t, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{priors }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"prior near likelihood"}\NormalTok{),}
               \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"prior far away"}\NormalTok{),}
               \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"vague prior N(0, 100)"}\NormalTok{))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (pr }\ControlFlowTok{in}\NormalTok{ priors) \{}
\NormalTok{    m }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{as.numeric}\NormalTok{(pr[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]); s }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{as.numeric}\NormalTok{(pr[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{])}
\NormalTok{    pi\_fn }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(t) }\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(t, m, s)}
\NormalTok{    ml }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{integrate}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(t) }\FunctionTok{L}\NormalTok{(t) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{pi\_fn}\NormalTok{(t), }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{, }\ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{value}
    \FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(L, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.42}\NormalTok{),}
          \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{""}\NormalTok{,}
          \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"\%s}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{P(y) = \%.4f"}\NormalTok{, pr[}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{], ml))}
    \FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(pi\_fn, }\AttributeTok{add =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ts }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{)}
    \FunctionTok{polygon}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(ts, }\FunctionTok{rev}\NormalTok{(ts)), }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{L}\NormalTok{(ts) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{pi\_fn}\NormalTok{(ts), }\FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{)),}
            \AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{adjustcolor}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"gray30"}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.4}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{border =} \ConstantTok{NA}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit08_integral_files/figure-pdf/fig-prior-sensitivity-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-prior-sensitivity}The same likelihood (red) combined
with three priors (blue). \(P(y)\) is the area under
\(L(\theta)\pi(\theta)\) (shaded), computed here by \texttt{integrate}
for a single parameter as a sanity check.}

\end{figure}%

A prior centred where the likelihood is large gives a much bigger
marginal likelihood than one that is far away or merely diffuse ---
which is exactly why \(P(y)\) is useful for comparing models, and why we
must be able to compute it even when the parameter is multivariate and
there is no \texttt{integrate}.

\subsection{A two-parameter example: mean and variance
unknown}\label{a-two-parameter-example-mean-and-variance-unknown}

Let
\(y_1,\ldots,y_n \mid \mu, \sigma \overset{iid}{\sim} t_\nu(\mu,\sigma)\),
a location-scale \(t\) distribution with \(\nu\) degrees of freedom (the
special case \(\nu=\infty\) is the normal). Reparametrize the scale as
\(w = \log \sigma\) so that both parameters range over
\((-\infty,\infty)\), and put independent normal priors on them: \[
\mu \sim N(\mu_0, \sigma_\mu^2), \qquad w \sim N(w_0, \sigma_w^2).
\] The marginal likelihood is the two-dimensional integral \[
P(y) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}
 L(\mu, w)\,\pi(\mu, w)\, d\mu\, dw,
\qquad L(\mu,w) = \prod_{i=1}^n t_\nu\!\big(y_i;\ \mu,\ e^w\big),
\] which has no closed form because the normal prior on \(w\) is not
conjugate to the likelihood. We handle it with a \textbf{product
midpoint rule}: apply the logistic transform to each of \(\mu\) and
\(w\) separately, \[
\mu = \log\frac{u}{1-u}, \qquad w = \log\frac{v}{1-v}, \qquad
d\mu\,dw = \frac{du\,dv}{u(1-u)\,v(1-v)},
\] giving \[
P(y) = \int_0^1\!\int_0^1 g(u,v)\,du\,dv, \qquad
g(u,v) = \frac{L(\mu(u),w(v))\,\pi(\mu(u),w(v))}{u(1-u)\,v(1-v)},
\] and evaluate \(g\) on an \(n \times n\) grid \(u_j = (j-\tfrac12)h\),
\(v_k =
(k-\tfrac12)h\) with \(h=1/n\): \[
P(y) \approx h^2 \sum_{j=1}^n \sum_{k=1}^n g(u_j, v_k).
\] The midpoint rule again earns its place here:
\(1/\{u(1-u)v(1-v)\} \to \infty\) at the boundary of the square, and the
midpoint grid never touches it.

\subsection{Computing on the log
scale}\label{computing-on-the-log-scale}

\(L(\theta)\) is a product of \(n\) densities and underflows to exactly
\(0\) in floating point for even moderate \(n\), so every calculation
below is carried out on the log scale, and sums of exponentials are
combined with \textbf{log-sum-exp}: \[
\log \sum_i e^{\ell_i} = M + \log \sum_i e^{\ell_i - M}, \qquad M = \max_i \ell_i.
\] Subtracting the maximum before exponentiating makes the largest term
exactly \(e^0=1\), so nothing overflows; terms far below \(M\) underflow
harmlessly to zero instead of corrupting the sum.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{log\_sum\_exp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(lx) \{}
\NormalTok{    m }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(lx)}
\NormalTok{    m }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(lx }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ m)))}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{An efficient
implementation}\label{an-efficient-implementation}

The functions below compute \(\log g(u,v)\) on the whole grid at once
with \texttt{outer()}, rather than looping in R or nesting two calls to
a generic 1-D integrator (the nested approach used in earlier drafts of
this material is correct but re-evaluates the logistic transform and
re-dispatches a function call \(n^2\) times through two layers of
\texttt{sapply}; building the grid directly is both clearer and several
times faster for the grid sizes used here).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# logistic link and its log{-}derivative, both vectorized}
\NormalTok{logit\_inv     }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(u) }\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(u }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ u))}
\NormalTok{log\_der\_logit }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(u) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(u) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ u)}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# log likelihood of a location{-}scale t sample (df = Inf is the normal)}
\NormalTok{log\_lik }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x, mu, w, }\AttributeTok{df =} \ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{) }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{dt.scaled}\NormalTok{(x, df, mu, }\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(w), }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# log joint prior on (mu, w)}
\NormalTok{log\_prior }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(mu, w, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w) \{}
    \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(mu, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(w, w\_0, sigma\_w, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# log of the transformed integrand g(u, v) at one grid point}
\NormalTok{log\_g }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(u, v, x, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w, }\AttributeTok{df =} \ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    mu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{logit\_inv}\NormalTok{(u); w }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{logit\_inv}\NormalTok{(v)}
    \FunctionTok{log\_lik}\NormalTok{(x, mu, w, df) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{log\_prior}\NormalTok{(mu, w, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
        \FunctionTok{log\_der\_logit}\NormalTok{(u) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{log\_der\_logit}\NormalTok{(v)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# log marginal likelihood by the product midpoint rule on an n x n grid}
\NormalTok{log\_marlik\_mid }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w, n, }\AttributeTok{df =} \ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    h }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ n}
\NormalTok{    grid }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(n) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ h}
\NormalTok{    G }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{outer}\NormalTok{(grid, grid, }\FunctionTok{Vectorize}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(u, v)}
        \FunctionTok{log\_g}\NormalTok{(u, v, x, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w, df)))}
    \FunctionTok{log\_sum\_exp}\NormalTok{(G) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(h)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# log marginal likelihood by naive Monte Carlo (for validating the above)}
\NormalTok{log\_marlik\_mc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w, iters\_mc, }\AttributeTok{df =} \ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    mus }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(iters\_mc, mu\_0, sigma\_mu)}
\NormalTok{    ws  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(iters\_mc, w\_0, sigma\_w)}
\NormalTok{    v\_log\_lik }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(iters\_mc), }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(i) }\FunctionTok{log\_lik}\NormalTok{(x, mus[i], ws[i], df), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
    \FunctionTok{log\_sum\_exp}\NormalTok{(v\_log\_lik) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(iters\_mc)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{log\_marlik\_mc} draws \(\theta\) from the prior and averages
the likelihood --- a direct Monte Carlo estimate of \(E_\pi[L(\theta)]\)
--- and serves purely as an independent check on the quadrature answer,
since it makes no use of a grid or a transformation and so cannot share
their failure modes.

\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{v =} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =}\NormalTok{ h), }\AttributeTok{h =} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =}\NormalTok{ h), }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{adjustcolor}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"gray30"}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.4}\NormalTok{))}

\subsection{Checking the quadrature against Monte
Carlo}\label{checking-the-quadrature-against-monte-carlo}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rt.scaled}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mean =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rt.scaled}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mean =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{comparison }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{dataset =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"n=50, t (df=2) data"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"n=100, normal data"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{midpoint\_n100 =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log\_marlik\_mid}\NormalTok{(x1, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{),}

\NormalTok{)}

\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrr@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-quad-vs-mc}Estimated log marginal likelihood:
product midpoint rule versus naive Monte Carlo, for two simulated data
sets.}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
dataset & midpoint\_n100 & monte\_carlo \\
\midrule\noalign{}

\bottomrule\noalign{}

The two independent methods agree to well within a tenth of a log-unit,
which is reassuring: they share no code path, so agreement is evidence
that neither the grid nor the Monte Carlo sample is too coarse for this
posterior.

\subsection{Comparing priors}\label{comparing-priors}

Holding the data fixed, the marginal likelihood falls as the prior on
\(\mu\) either narrows around the wrong value or spreads out
indefinitely:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{prior\_grid }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{expand.grid}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mu\_0 =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{sigma\_mu =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.01}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{prior\_grid}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{log\_P\_y }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{mapply}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(m, s) }\FunctionTok{log\_marlik\_mid}\NormalTok{(x, m, s, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{                              prior\_grid}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{mu\_0, prior\_grid}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sigma\_mu)}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(prior\_grid, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{,}
             \AttributeTok{col.names =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"prior mean $}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}}\StringTok{mu\_0$"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"prior sd $}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}}\StringTok{sigma\_}\SpecialCharTok{\textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}}\StringTok{mu$"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"log P(y)"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}rrr@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-compare-priors}Log marginal likelihood for data
centred at 0, under priors on mu with the stated mean and standard
deviation (w-prior fixed at N(0,1)).}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
prior mean \(\mu_0\) & prior sd \(\sigma_\mu\) & log P(y) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
0 & 1e-02 & -129.39 \\
-5 & 1e-02 & -739.84 \\
0 & 1e-01 & -128.95 \\
-5 & 1e-01 & -316.30 \\
0 & 1e+00 & -130.97 \\
-5 & 1e+00 & -143.43 \\
0 & 1e+01 & -133.27 \\
-5 & 1e+01 & -133.40 \\
0 & 1e+02 & -135.57 \\
-5 & 1e+02 & -135.57 \\
0 & 1e+03 & -137.88 \\
-5 & 1e+03 & -137.88 \\

\end{longtable}

}

With \(\mu_0 = 0\) (the right neighbourhood for data centred at \(0\)),
the marginal likelihood is highest for a moderately informative prior
and degrades slowly as the prior widens. With \(\mu_0 = -5\) (the wrong
neighbourhood), a \emph{narrow} prior is actively harmful --- it insists
on a region the data reject --- while a wide prior recovers because it
eventually puts enough mass back near the truth. This is the general
shape of Bayesian model comparison: strong, correct priors win; strong,
wrong priors lose; vague priors are safe but not optimal.

\subsection{Comparing models for the
data}\label{comparing-models-for-the-data}

The marginal likelihood also compares different likelihoods for the same
data --- here, whether \(\nu\) (the degrees of freedom of the \(t\)
model) matches how the data were actually generated:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{dfs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x\_normal }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rt.scaled}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mean =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x\_heavy  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rt.scaled}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mean =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{model\_compare }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{df\_fitted        =}\NormalTok{ dfs,}
    \AttributeTok{normal\_data       =} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(dfs, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(d) }\FunctionTok{log\_marlik\_mid}\NormalTok{(x\_normal, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =}\NormalTok{ d), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)),}
    \AttributeTok{heavy\_tailed\_data =} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(dfs, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(d) }\FunctionTok{log\_marlik\_mid}\NormalTok{(x\_heavy,  }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =}\NormalTok{ d), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(model\_compare, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{,}
             \AttributeTok{col.names =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"fitted df"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"data simulated as normal"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"data simulated as t(df=2)"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}rrr@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-compare-models}Log marginal likelihood under four
values of the t-model degrees of freedom nu, fit to data simulated from
a Gaussian and from a heavy-tailed t(df=2).}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
fitted df & data simulated as normal & data simulated as t(df=2) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Inf & -210.675 & -300.951 \\
2.0 & -220.056 & -261.663 \\
1.0 & -231.060 & -265.646 \\
0.5 & -251.042 & -281.555 \\

\end{longtable}

}

Each column favours the \(\nu\) that actually generated it: normal data
are best explained by \(\nu=\infty\), and \(t(\text{df}=2)\) data are
best explained by \(\nu=2\), with the wrong tail weight costing several
log-likelihood units either way. As the earlier prior comparison showed,
the prior also shifts these numbers --- but the \emph{ranking} of the
correct model over the incorrect ones survives even under a badly
misspecified prior on \((\mu,w)\), which is why marginal likelihoods
remain useful for model comparison despite their prior-sensitivity.

\section{Difficulties of Numerical
Quadrature}\label{difficulties-of-numerical-quadrature}

\subsection{Peaked integrands can be missed by a fixed
grid}\label{peaked-integrands-can-be-missed-by-a-fixed-grid}

A grid of fixed width \(h_n\) can straddle a narrow spike without ever
landing a point near its peak, badly under- or over-estimating the area
it represents.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{a }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{; b }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{; n\_panels }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{; h }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ (b }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ a) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ n\_panels}
\NormalTok{xm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ a }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(n\_panels) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ h}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (s }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.03}\NormalTok{)) \{}
\NormalTok{    f }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x, }\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{, s)}
    \FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(f, a, b, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{2001}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"x"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"f(x)"}\NormalTok{,}
          \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"sd = \%g: midpoint = \%.3f (exact = 1)"}\NormalTok{, s, h }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{f}\NormalTok{(xm))))}
    \FunctionTok{rect}\NormalTok{(xm }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ h }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, xm }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ h }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{f}\NormalTok{(xm), }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{adjustcolor}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{border =} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{)}
    \FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(xm, }\FunctionTok{f}\NormalTok{(xm), }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit08_integral_files/figure-pdf/fig-peaked-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-peaked}Same rule, same 8-panel grid: the wide
density (left) integrates almost exactly, while the narrow spike (right)
is nearly invisible to the grid because no panel midpoint falls near its
mode.}

\end{figure}%

This is exactly the situation a Bayesian posterior creates as \(n\)
grows: its width shrinks like \(O(1/\sqrt n)\), so a grid tuned for
\(n=10\) observations can become useless for \(n=10{,}000\). The usual
remedy is to find the mode first (e.g.~by the optimization methods of
the previous two chapters), then centre and scale the grid around it ---
which is also the starting point for the Laplace approximation in the
next chapter.

\subsection{Transformed integrands can be squeezed against the
boundary}\label{transformed-integrands-can-be-squeezed-against-the-boundary}

The logistic transform has its own failure mode: if \(\theta\)'s
posterior sits at a large \(|\theta|\), it gets squeezed against \(y=0\)
or \(y=1\). For \(\theta \approx 50\),
\(y = 1/(1+e^{-50}) \approx 1 - 2\times 10^{-22}\) --- the entire
posterior mass occupies a sliver of \((0,1)\) far too thin for any
practical uniform grid to resolve.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{f }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(f, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{""}\NormalTok{,}
      \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"posterior at"} \SpecialCharTok{\textasciitilde{}}\NormalTok{ theta }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{g }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(y) }\FunctionTok{f}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(y }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ y))) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (y }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ y))}
\FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(g, }\FloatTok{0.001}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.9999}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{5001}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"y"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{""}\NormalTok{,}
      \AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"after logistic transform"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{v =} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.025}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.975}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =} \FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{adjustcolor}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.6}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit08_integral_files/figure-pdf/fig-boundary-squeeze-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-boundary-squeeze}A posterior located at theta=8
already occupies a visibly asymmetric region after the logistic
transform (right); the grid lines (n=20) are far more informative on the
left than the right.}

\end{figure}%

We already met the extreme version of this problem at the start of this
chapter's development: with data simulated far from where the
untransformed grid was designed to look, the midpoint rule and Monte
Carlo disagree badly.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x\_far }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rt.scaled}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mean =} \DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{df =} \ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{method =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"midpoint (n = 100)"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Monte Carlo (n = 10,000)"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{log\_P\_y =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log\_marlik\_mid}\NormalTok{(x\_far, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{),}
                \FunctionTok{log\_marlik\_mc}\NormalTok{(x\_far, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1e4}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lr@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-quad-failure}Quadrature failure when the posterior
is squeezed against the boundary: data centred at 50, but the logistic
transform (with no recentring) puts almost the whole posterior mass
within about 1e-20 of y = 1.}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
method & log\_P\_y \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
midpoint (n = 100) & -535.484 \\
Monte Carlo (n = 10,000) & -251.749 \\

\end{longtable}

}

The two estimates disagree by many orders of magnitude on the log scale
--- the grid is simply not looking where the posterior mass is. The
general remedy is the same as for the peaked-integrand problem:
\textbf{recentre the transform} on a preliminary location estimate
\(\hat\theta\) (e.g.~the MLE), using
\(y = 1/(1 + e^{-(\theta-\hat\theta)/s})\) for a suitable scale \(s\),
so that the bulk of the posterior maps back to the middle of \((0,1)\)
where the grid is dense.

\subsection{Explore it interactively}\label{explore-it-interactively}

The app below lets you move the true mean of the simulated data and
watch the midpoint-rule estimate diverge from the Monte Carlo baseline
as the posterior is pushed toward the boundary of the transformed
square, and shows the \(g(u,v)\) heat map so you can see the mass
leaving the grid.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{\#| label: quadrature{-}failure{-}app}
\NormalTok{\#| standalone: true}
\NormalTok{\#| viewerHeight: 620}

\NormalTok{library(shiny)}

\NormalTok{logit\_inv     \textless{}{-} function(u) log(u / (1 {-} u))}
\NormalTok{log\_der\_logit \textless{}{-} function(u) {-}log(u) {-} log(1 {-} u)}
\NormalTok{log\_lik   \textless{}{-} function(x, mu, w) sum(dnorm(x, mu, exp(w), log = TRUE))}
\NormalTok{log\_prior \textless{}{-} function(mu, w, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w) \{}
\NormalTok{    dnorm(mu, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, log = TRUE) + dnorm(w, w\_0, sigma\_w, log = TRUE)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{log\_g \textless{}{-} function(u, v, x, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w) \{}
\NormalTok{    mu \textless{}{-} logit\_inv(u); w \textless{}{-} logit\_inv(v)}
\NormalTok{    log\_lik(x, mu, w) + log\_prior(mu, w, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w) +}
\NormalTok{        log\_der\_logit(u) + log\_der\_logit(v)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{log\_sum\_exp \textless{}{-} function(lx) \{ m \textless{}{-} max(lx); m + log(sum(exp(lx {-} m))) \}}

\NormalTok{log\_marlik\_mid \textless{}{-} function(x, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w, n) \{}
\NormalTok{    h \textless{}{-} 1 / n}
\NormalTok{    grid \textless{}{-} (seq\_len(n) {-} 0.5) * h}
\NormalTok{    G \textless{}{-} outer(grid, grid, Vectorize(function(u, v) log\_g(u, v, x, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w)))}
\NormalTok{    list(log\_ml = log\_sum\_exp(G) + 2 * log(h), grid = grid, G = G)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{log\_marlik\_mc \textless{}{-} function(x, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w, iters\_mc) \{}
\NormalTok{    mus \textless{}{-} rnorm(iters\_mc, mu\_0, sigma\_mu)}
\NormalTok{    ws  \textless{}{-} rnorm(iters\_mc, w\_0, sigma\_w)}
\NormalTok{    v\_log\_lik \textless{}{-} vapply(seq\_len(iters\_mc), function(i) log\_lik(x, mus[i], ws[i]), numeric(1))}
\NormalTok{    log\_sum\_exp(v\_log\_lik) {-} log(iters\_mc)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{ui \textless{}{-} fluidPage(}
\NormalTok{    titlePanel(NULL),}
\NormalTok{    sidebarLayout(}
\NormalTok{        sidebarPanel(}
\NormalTok{            width = 4,}
\NormalTok{            sliderInput("true\_mean", "true mean of the simulated data",}
\NormalTok{                        min = 0, max = 60, value = 0, step = 2),}
\NormalTok{            sliderInput("n\_grid", "grid points per axis (midpoint rule)",}
\NormalTok{                        min = 10, max = 60, value = 20, step = 5),}
\NormalTok{            helpText("The prior on mu is N(0, 100), i.e. it is not recentred ",}
\NormalTok{                     "on the data. As the true mean moves past about 15{-}20, the ",}
\NormalTok{                     "posterior for mu is squeezed against y = 1 after the ",}
\NormalTok{                     "logistic transform, and the midpoint estimate breaks away ",}
\NormalTok{                     "from the Monte Carlo baseline."),}
\NormalTok{            tableOutput("tab")}
\NormalTok{        ),}
\NormalTok{        mainPanel(}
\NormalTok{            width = 8,}
\NormalTok{            plotOutput("heat", height = "320px"),}
\NormalTok{            plotOutput("compare", height = "260px")}
\NormalTok{        )}
\NormalTok{    )}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{server \textless{}{-} function(input, output, session) \{}

\NormalTok{    dat \textless{}{-} reactive(\{}
\NormalTok{        set.seed(1)}
\NormalTok{        rnorm(60, input$true\_mean, 2)}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    fit \textless{}{-} reactive(\{}
\NormalTok{        log\_marlik\_mid(dat(), 0, 100, 0, 100, input$n\_grid)}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    mc \textless{}{-} reactive(\{}
\NormalTok{        set.seed(2)}
\NormalTok{        log\_marlik\_mc(dat(), 0, 100, 0, 100, 20000)}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$heat \textless{}{-} renderPlot(\{}
\NormalTok{        f \textless{}{-} fit()}
\NormalTok{        par(mar = c(4, 4, 2, 1))}
\NormalTok{        image(f$grid, f$grid, exp(f$G {-} max(f$G)),}
\NormalTok{              col = hcl.colors(30, "YlOrRd", rev = TRUE),}
\NormalTok{              xlab = "u", ylab = "v",}
\NormalTok{              main = "g(u, v) on the unit square: where is the mass?")}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$compare \textless{}{-} renderPlot(\{}
\NormalTok{        par(mar = c(4, 4, 1, 1))}
\NormalTok{        vals \textless{}{-} c(midpoint = fit()$log\_ml, monte\_carlo = mc())}
\NormalTok{        bp \textless{}{-} barplot(vals, col = c("steelblue", "firebrick"),}
\NormalTok{                      ylab = "estimated log P(y)",}
\NormalTok{                      ylim = range(vals) + c({-}1, 1) * 0.15 * diff(range(vals) + 1e{-}6))}
\NormalTok{        text(bp, vals, sprintf("\%.2f", vals), pos = 3)}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$tab \textless{}{-} renderTable(\{}
\NormalTok{        data.frame(method = c("midpoint rule", "Monte Carlo"),}
\NormalTok{                   \textasciigrave{}log P(y)\textasciigrave{} = c(fit()$log\_ml, mc()), check.names = FALSE)}
\NormalTok{    \}, digits = 3, rownames = FALSE)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{shinyApp(ui, server)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Reproducing this figure}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

\href{https://github.com/quarto-ext/shinylive}{shinylive} extension,

\end{Highlighting}

\subsection{The curse of
dimensionality}\label{the-curse-of-dimensionality}

A product grid with \(n\) points per axis needs \(n^d\) evaluations in
\(d\) dimensions:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}rrr@{}}
\caption{Grid size grows exponentially with
dimension}\label{tbl-curse}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
\(d\) & \(n=20\) & \(n=50\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
\(d\) & \(n=20\) & \(n=50\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
1 & 20 & 50 \\
2 & 400 & 2,500 \\
5 & \(3.2\times10^6\) & \(3.1\times10^8\) \\
10 & \(1.0\times10^{13}\) & \(9.8\times10^{16}\) \\
\end{longtable}

Meanwhile the posterior occupies only a tiny, often tilted, region
inside that grid --- most evaluations are wasted on points where
\(L(\theta)\pi(\theta)
\approx 0\) --- and the error of a product rule, expressed in terms of
the total number of evaluations \(N=n^d\), degrades to \(O(N^{-2/d})\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{S }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.85}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.85}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FloatTok{0.15}
\NormalTok{Si }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{solve}\NormalTok{(S)}
\NormalTok{grd }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{150}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{Z }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{outer}\NormalTok{(grd, grd, }\FunctionTok{Vectorize}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(a, b) }\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.5} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(a, b) }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%}\NormalTok{ Si }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(a, b))))}
\FunctionTok{contour}\NormalTok{(grd, grd, Z, }\AttributeTok{nlevels =} \DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]),}
        \AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"posterior inside a 12 x 12 grid"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{drawlabels =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{gr }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{v =}\NormalTok{ gr, }\AttributeTok{h =}\NormalTok{ gr, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{adjustcolor}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"gray30"}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{pts }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{expand.grid}\NormalTok{(gr, gr)}
\FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(pts, }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{inside }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{apply}\NormalTok{(pts, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(p) }\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.5} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ p }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%}\NormalTok{ Si }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%}\NormalTok{ p)) }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.01}
\FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(pts[inside, ], }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{0.9}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{mtext}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"\%d of \%d grid points carry non{-}negligible mass"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(inside), }\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(pts)),}
      \AttributeTok{side =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{line =} \FloatTok{2.6}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{0.85}\NormalTok{)}

\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(d) }\DecValTok{20}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{d), }\AttributeTok{log =} \StringTok{"y"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"b"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{,}
     \AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"dimension d"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"evaluations with 20 points per axis"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \FloatTok{1e12}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{text}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{3e12}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"one trillion"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{adj =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{0.9}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit08_integral_files/figure-pdf/fig-curse-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-curse}Left: a correlated 2-D posterior inside a
12x12 grid; only the highlighted points carry non-negligible mass.
Right: evaluations needed for a 20-point-per-axis grid as dimension
increases.}

\end{figure}%

\textbf{Conclusion.} Quadrature is a reliable, cheap tool for
\(d \lesssim 3\), and it is what the rest of this book uses whenever a
low-dimensional integral needs checking against another method. Beyond
that, Monte Carlo methods --- whose error is \(O(N^{-1/2})\)
\emph{regardless of dimension} --- take over, at the cost of losing the
very fast convergence rates of Simpson's rule; this trade-off motivates
the sampling methods developed from the next few chapters onward.

\section{Summary}\label{summary-2}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Quadrature approximates \(\int_a^b f\,dx\) by a weighted sum of \(f\)
  at grid points; infinite ranges are handled by a change of variable
  such as the logistic transform, always carrying along the Jacobian
  \(t'(y)\).
\item
  The midpoint and trapezoidal rules have \(O(h_n^2)\) error; Simpson's
  rule has \(O(h_n^4)\). The midpoint rule's extra virtue --- never
  evaluating the integrand at a panel edge --- matters whenever the
  transformed integrand is singular at the boundary, as it is here.
\item
  The marginal likelihood \(P(y) = \int L(\theta)\pi(\theta)\,d\theta\)
  is the key statistical use case in this book: always compute it on the
  log scale with log-sum-exp, and validate a quadrature answer against
  an independent Monte Carlo estimate.
\item
  Quadrature fails when the integrand is peaked relative to the grid, or
  when a transform squeezes the mass of interest into a region the grid
  cannot resolve; both problems are cured by locating the mass first
  (optimization) and centring the grid or transform on it --- the idea
  taken up systematically by the Laplace approximation in the next
  chapter.
\item
  Product grids cost \(n^d\) evaluations, so quadrature is practical
  only for small \(d\); higher-dimensional problems require Monte Carlo
  methods.
\end{itemize}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Laplace Approximation}\label{laplace-approximation}

The previous chapter approximated an integral by a weighted sum of
function values on a grid. A grid gets expensive fast: an
\(n\)-point-per-axis product grid costs \(n^d\) evaluations, so
quadrature is only practical for \(d \lesssim
3\). The \textbf{Laplace approximation} takes a completely different
route: instead of evaluating the integrand everywhere, it looks only at
the single most important point --- the mode --- and replaces the whole
integrand there by the Gaussian that matches its height and curvature.
No grid is needed at any dimension, at the price of assuming the
integrand is roughly bell-shaped.

\section{The Goal and the Idea}\label{the-goal-and-the-idea}

Many Bayesian calculations are posterior expectations \[
E_f\big[a(\theta)\big] = \frac{\int a(\theta)\,f(\theta)\,d\theta}{\int f(\theta)\,d\theta},
\] where \(\theta \in \mathbb{R}^p\), \(a(\theta)\) is some quantity of
interest (a parameter, a prediction, an indicator), and \(f(\theta)\) is
an \textbf{unnormalized} posterior, typically
\(f(\theta) = L(\theta)\pi(\theta)\). Both numerator and denominator are
\(p\)-dimensional integrals.

Write \(h(\theta) = -\log f(\theta)\), so
\(f(\theta) = e^{-h(\theta)}\). For independent data, \(h\) is (up to an
additive constant) minus the log-likelihood plus minus the log-prior ---
a sum of \(n\) terms --- so as \(n\) grows it becomes large and sharply
curved at its minimum. This is the same large-sample regularity that
makes the MLE asymptotically normal (seen in the maximum-likelihood
chapters); here it makes the \emph{posterior} asymptotically normal too.
The Laplace approximation makes this precise by expanding \(h\) to
second order at its minimum and integrating the resulting Gaussian
exactly --- no grid, no sampling, just one optimization and one Hessian.

\section{Derivation}\label{derivation-1}

Let \(\theta_0 = \arg\min_\theta h(\theta)\), the posterior mode, found
numerically (\texttt{optim(...,\ hessian\ =\ TRUE)} or
\texttt{nlm(...,\ hessian\ =\ TRUE)} in R --- exactly the optimizers
used to find the MLE in earlier chapters). Expand \(h\) around
\(\theta_0\): \[
h(\theta) = h(\theta_0) + \underbrace{\nabla h(\theta_0)^\top}_{=\,0\text{ at the minimum}}(\theta - \theta_0)
 + \tfrac12(\theta-\theta_0)^\top H (\theta - \theta_0) + O\big(\|\theta-\theta_0\|^3\big),
\qquad H = \nabla^2 h(\theta_0).
\] \(H\), the Hessian of \(-\log f\) at the mode, is positive definite
at a genuine minimum; for \(f = L\pi\) it is the observed information
plus the curvature of the log-prior. Dropping the cubic remainder and
exponentiating, \[
f(\theta) \approx \tilde f(\theta) = f(\theta_0)\exp\Big\{-\tfrac12(\theta-\theta_0)^\top H(\theta-\theta_0)\Big\}
 = \underbrace{f(\theta_0)(2\pi)^{p/2}|H|^{-1/2}}_{\text{constant}}
   \cdot \underbrace{(2\pi)^{-p/2}|H|^{1/2}e^{-\frac12(\theta-\theta_0)^\top H(\theta-\theta_0)}}_{N_p(\theta_0,\,H^{-1})\text{ density}}.
\] Two results follow immediately, since the bracketed factor is a
properly normalized \(N_p(\theta_0, H^{-1})\) density:

\[
\boxed{\ \theta \mid \text{data} \ \approx\ N_p\big(\theta_0,\ H^{-1}\big) \ }
\qquad\qquad
\boxed{\ \int f(\theta)\,d\theta \ \approx\ f(\theta_0)\,(2\pi)^{p/2}\,|H|^{-1/2} \ }
\]

Only the mode and the Hessian of \(-\log f\) at the mode are needed ---
both come for free from any optimizer that returns a Hessian. The
relative error of the second result is \(O(1/n)\) for regular models;
the error of the first is governed by how skewed the true posterior
actually is.

\section{Worked Example: A Poisson Rate with a Gamma
Prior}\label{worked-example-a-poisson-rate-with-a-gamma-prior}

To see the approximation against ground truth, use a model with a known
exact answer. For
\(y_1,\ldots,y_n \mid \lambda \sim \text{Poisson}(\lambda)\) and
\(\lambda \sim \text{Gamma}(\alpha,\beta)\), the posterior is exactly
\(\text{Gamma}(\alpha + \sum y_i,\ \beta+n)\), so both the exact log
marginal likelihood and the exact posterior are available in closed form
and the Laplace error can be measured directly.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]

\subsection{Linear functionals: posterior mean and credible
intervals}\label{linear-functionals-posterior-mean-and-credible-intervals}

If \(a(\theta) = A^\top\theta + a_0\) is linear, replacing \(f\) by
\(\tilde f\) gives \(E_f[a(\theta)] \approx A^\top\theta_0 + a_0\): the
posterior mean is approximated by the posterior mode, and
\(\text{Var}(A^\top\theta) \approx
A^\top H^{-1}A\) gives Wald-style credible intervals \(\theta_{0j} \pm
z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{(H^{-1})_{jj}}\) --- exactly the Bayesian counterpart
of an MLE plus its Wald standard error from the maximum-likelihood
chapters.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{42}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{n }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{; y }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rpois}\NormalTok{(n, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{); lap }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{laplace\_poisson}\NormalTok{(y)}
\NormalTok{a1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ alpha }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(y); b1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ beta }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ n}
\NormalTok{comp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}
  \AttributeTok{quantity =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Laplace (mode / se)"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"exact posterior (mean / sd)"}\NormalTok{),}

  \AttributeTok{upper95 =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(lap[}\StringTok{"mode"}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FloatTok{1.96} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ lap[}\StringTok{"se"}\NormalTok{], }\FunctionTok{qgamma}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.975}\NormalTok{, a1, b1))}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(comp, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{BIC as a large-\(n\)
simplification}{BIC as a large-n simplification}}\label{bic-as-a-large-n-simplification}

With \(f(\theta) = L(\theta;D)\pi(\theta)\), the Laplace approximation
to the log marginal likelihood is \[
\log P(D) \approx \log\big[L(\theta_0;D)\,\pi(\theta_0)\big] + \frac p2\log(2\pi) - \frac12\log|H|.
\] For large \(n\) the Hessian is dominated by the likelihood,
\(H \approx
n\,I(\theta_0)\) with \(I\) the per-observation Fisher information, so
\(|H|
\approx n^p|I(\theta_0)|\) and \[
\log P(D) \approx \log L(\theta_0;D) - \frac p2\log n
 + \underbrace{\log\pi(\theta_0) + \frac p2\log(2\pi) - \frac12\log|I(\theta_0)|}_{O(1),\text{ dropped}}.
\] Multiplying by \(-2\) and replacing the posterior mode by the MLE
(the two coincide to \(O(1/n)\)) gives the \textbf{Bayesian information
criterion}, \(\text{BIC} = -2\log L(\hat\theta_{\text{MLE}}) + p\log n\)
--- a model-comparison score that needs no prior at all, because the
dropped \(O(1)\) terms do not grow with \(n\) and so wash out of
\emph{differences} between models fit to the same data. For the \(n=20\)
Poisson example from the credible-interval check above,
\(-2\times(\text{Laplace log }P(y))\) and the MLE-based BIC differ by
about 1.68 --- a constant offset consistent with the dropped \(O(1)\)
terms, not something that grows with \(n\).

\section{When Laplace Works Well, and When It Does
Not}\label{when-laplace-works-well-and-when-it-does-not}

\textbf{Works well} when \(n\) is large relative to \(p\) and the
posterior is unimodal and roughly symmetric, as in the \(n=100\) panel
above. Parameters should live on an unbounded scale --- transform first
(\(\log\sigma\), \(\text{logit}\,p\)) so the matching Gaussian is not
truncated at a boundary, remembering to include the Jacobian of the
transform in \(f\) (exactly the \(w=\log\sigma^2\) reparametrization
used throughout this book since the multivariate optimization chapter).

\textbf{Struggles} when the posterior is skewed (small \(n\), a
parameter near a boundary) --- the mode is then a poor stand-in for the
mean and the Gaussian tails are wrong, as the \(n=5\) panel above
already shows mildly; when the posterior is multimodal, since the
approximation only sees the one mode an optimizer happened to find; and
in hierarchical models where \(p\) grows with \(n\), so the \(O(1/n)\)
error does not vanish (nested Laplace approximation, INLA, handles this
by integrating out the high-dimensional part analytically).

\section{Comparing Laplace, Quadrature, and Monte Carlo on the Same
Problem}\label{comparing-laplace-quadrature-and-monte-carlo-on-the-same-problem}

The previous chapter (Numerical Quadrature) computed the marginal
likelihood of a normal sample with unknown mean \(\mu\) and log-variance
\(w=\log\sigma^2\), \[
P(y) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} L(\mu,w)\,\pi(\mu,w)\,d\mu\,dw,
\qquad \mu\sim N(\mu_0,\sigma_\mu^2),\ w\sim N(w_0,\sigma_w^2),
\] by a product midpoint rule on a logistic-transformed \((0,1)^2\)
grid, checked against naive Monte Carlo. The Laplace approximation
attacks the same integral by optimization instead of a grid: find the
joint mode of \((\mu,w)\) and its \(2\times2\) Hessian, then apply the
boxed formula with \(p=2\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# log likelihood and log prior for the normal/log{-}variance model of Unit08}
\NormalTok{log\_lik\_gauss  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x, mu, w) }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x, mu, }\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(w), }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{log\_prior\_gauss }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(mu, w, mu0, sigma\_mu, w0, sigma\_w) \{}
  \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(mu, mu0, sigma\_mu, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(w, w0, sigma\_w, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{three\_way }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{t}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(scenarios, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(s) \{}
\NormalTok{  x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mean =} \FunctionTok{as.numeric}\NormalTok{(s[}\StringTok{"mean"}\NormalTok{]), }\AttributeTok{sd =} \FunctionTok{as.numeric}\NormalTok{(s[}\StringTok{"sd"}\NormalTok{]))}
\NormalTok{  lap }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{laplace\_gaussian}\NormalTok{(x, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
  \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{Laplace =}\NormalTok{ lap}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{log\_ml,}
    \AttributeTok{Monte\_Carlo =} \FunctionTok{log\_mar\_mc}\NormalTok{(x, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1e6}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{Quadrature =} \FunctionTok{log\_mar\_mid}\NormalTok{(x, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{\}))}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{scenario =} \FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(scenarios, }\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\AttributeTok{[}\StringTok{\textasciigrave{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"desc"}\NormalTok{), }\FunctionTok{round}\NormalTok{(three\_way, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)),}
             \AttributeTok{row.names =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col.names =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"scenario"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Laplace"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Monte Carlo"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Quadrature (n=100 grid)"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}

well-centred: mean 5 & -81.378 & -81.502 & -79.713 \\

\end{longtable}

}

The pattern is striking. For data centred near the origin, all three
methods agree to within a unit or two on the log scale. But once the
data are far from the origin (mean \(-50\)), \textbf{Laplace and Monte
Carlo still agree closely with each other, while the fixed-grid
quadrature is off by well over 100 log-units} --- the same
boundary-squeeze failure diagnosed at the end of Unit08: the logistic
transform used there is centred at \(0\), so a posterior sitting at
\(\mu\approx-50\) gets squeezed into a sliver of the unit square that a
\(100\times100\) grid cannot resolve. Laplace approximation has no such
failure mode here, and the reason is structural, not a matter of tuning:
\textbf{it has no fixed grid to be squeezed against}. It optimizes to
find \(\theta_0\) wherever the mode actually is, then builds its
approximation \emph{around that point} --- so it automatically
``recentres'' itself for every dataset, which is exactly the manual fix
(recentre the transform on a location estimate) that Unit08 proposed for
quadrature. This is Laplace's core practical advantage over quadrature:
robustness to \emph{where} the posterior happens to sit, at the cost of
assuming it looks Gaussian once you get there.

\subsection{Explore it interactively}\label{explore-it-interactively-1}

The app below lets you move the true mean of the simulated data and
compare the three log marginal likelihood estimates live, so you can
watch quadrature's error grow while Laplace and Monte Carlo continue to
track each other.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{\#| label: laplace{-}vs{-}quadrature{-}app}
\NormalTok{\#| standalone: true}
\NormalTok{\#| viewerHeight: 560}

\NormalTok{library(shiny)}

\NormalTok{log\_lik\_gauss  \textless{}{-} function(x, mu, w) sum(dnorm(x, mu, exp(w), log = TRUE))}
\NormalTok{log\_prior\_gauss \textless{}{-} function(mu, w, mu0, sigma\_mu, w0, sigma\_w) \{}
\NormalTok{  dnorm(mu, mu0, sigma\_mu, log = TRUE) + dnorm(w, w0, sigma\_w, log = TRUE)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{neg\_log\_post \textless{}{-} function(theta, x, mu0, sigma\_mu, w0, sigma\_w) \{}
\NormalTok{  {-}log\_lik\_gauss(x, theta[1], theta[2]) {-} log\_prior\_gauss(theta[1], theta[2], mu0, sigma\_mu, w0, sigma\_w)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{laplace\_gaussian \textless{}{-} function(x, mu0, sigma\_mu, w0, sigma\_w) \{}
\NormalTok{  op \textless{}{-} nlm(neg\_log\_post, p = c(mean(x), log(sd(x))), hessian = TRUE,}
\NormalTok{            x = x, mu0 = mu0, sigma\_mu = sigma\_mu, w0 = w0, sigma\_w = sigma\_w)}
\NormalTok{  H \textless{}{-} op$hessian; p \textless{}{-} length(op$estimate)}
\NormalTok{  list(mode = op$estimate, log\_ml = {-}op$minimum + p / 2 * log(2 * pi) {-} 0.5 * log(det(H)))}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{log\_sum\_exp \textless{}{-} function(lx) \{ m \textless{}{-} max(lx); m + log(sum(exp(lx {-} m))) \}}
\NormalTok{log\_mar\_mc \textless{}{-} function(x, mu0, sigma\_mu, w0, sigma\_w, iters) \{}
\NormalTok{  mus \textless{}{-} rnorm(iters, mu0, sigma\_mu); ws \textless{}{-} rnorm(iters, w0, sigma\_w)}
\NormalTok{  ll \textless{}{-} vapply(seq\_len(iters), function(i) log\_lik\_gauss(x, mus[i], ws[i]), numeric(1))}
\NormalTok{  log\_sum\_exp(ll) {-} log(iters)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{logit\_inv     \textless{}{-} function(u) log(u / (1 {-} u))}
\NormalTok{log\_der\_logit \textless{}{-} function(u) {-}log(u) {-} log(1 {-} u)}
\NormalTok{log\_g \textless{}{-} function(u, v, x, mu0, sigma\_mu, w0, sigma\_w) \{}
\NormalTok{  mu \textless{}{-} logit\_inv(u); w \textless{}{-} logit\_inv(v)}
\NormalTok{  log\_lik\_gauss(x, mu, w) + log\_prior\_gauss(mu, w, mu0, sigma\_mu, w0, sigma\_w) +}
\NormalTok{    log\_der\_logit(u) + log\_der\_logit(v)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{log\_mar\_mid \textless{}{-} function(x, mu0, sigma\_mu, w0, sigma\_w, n) \{}
\NormalTok{  h \textless{}{-} 1 / n; grid \textless{}{-} (seq\_len(n) {-} 0.5) * h}
\NormalTok{  G \textless{}{-} outer(grid, grid, Vectorize(function(u, v) log\_g(u, v, x, mu0, sigma\_mu, w0, sigma\_w)))}
\NormalTok{  log\_sum\_exp(G) + 2 * log(h)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{ui \textless{}{-} fluidPage(}
\NormalTok{  titlePanel(NULL),}
\NormalTok{  sidebarLayout(}
\NormalTok{    sidebarPanel(}
\NormalTok{      width = 4,}
\NormalTok{      sliderInput("true\_mean", "true mean of the simulated data", min = {-}60, max = 10, value = 5, step = 2),}
\NormalTok{      sliderInput("n\_grid", "quadrature grid points per axis", min = 10, max = 100, value = 100, step = 10),}
\NormalTok{      helpText("Prior: N(0, 100) on mu, N(0, 5) on w = log(sd). As the true mean ",}
\NormalTok{               "moves away from 0, the fixed logistic{-}transform grid squeezes the ",}
\NormalTok{               "posterior into a corner and the quadrature estimate breaks away, ",}
\NormalTok{               "while Laplace (which re{-}optimizes for every dataset) tracks Monte Carlo."),}
\NormalTok{      tableOutput("tab")}
\NormalTok{    ),}
\NormalTok{    mainPanel(width = 8, plotOutput("bars", height = "420px"))}
\NormalTok{  )}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{server \textless{}{-} function(input, output, session) \{}
\NormalTok{  dat \textless{}{-} reactive(\{ set.seed(1); rnorm(50, input$true\_mean, 2) \})}
\NormalTok{  vals \textless{}{-} reactive(\{}
\NormalTok{    x \textless{}{-} dat()}
\NormalTok{    c(Laplace = laplace\_gaussian(x, 0, 100, 0, 5)$log\_ml,}
\NormalTok{      Monte\_Carlo = \{ set.seed(2); log\_mar\_mc(x, 0, 100, 0, 5, 2e5) \},}
\NormalTok{      Quadrature = log\_mar\_mid(x, 0, 100, 0, 5, input$n\_grid))}
\NormalTok{  \})}
\NormalTok{  output$bars \textless{}{-} renderPlot(\{}
\NormalTok{    v \textless{}{-} vals()}
\NormalTok{    par(mar = c(4, 4, 2, 1))}
\NormalTok{    bp \textless{}{-} barplot(v, col = c("forestgreen", "steelblue", "firebrick"),}
\NormalTok{                  ylab = "estimated log P(y)", main = "three methods, same data")}
\NormalTok{    text(bp, v, sprintf("\%.1f", v), pos = ifelse(v \textgreater{}= 0, 3, 1))}
\NormalTok{  \})}
\NormalTok{  output$tab \textless{}{-} renderTable(\{}
\NormalTok{    v \textless{}{-} vals()}
\NormalTok{    data.frame(method = names(v), \textasciigrave{}log P(y)\textasciigrave{} = as.numeric(v), check.names = FALSE)}
\NormalTok{  \}, digits = 2, rownames = FALSE)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{shinyApp(ui, server)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Summary}\label{summary-3}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The Laplace approximation expands \(h=-\log f\) to second order at its
  minimum \(\theta_0\) and integrates the resulting Gaussian exactly:
  \(\theta\mid\text{data} \approx N_p(\theta_0, H^{-1})\) and
  \(\int f(\theta)\,d\theta \approx f(\theta_0)(2\pi)^{p/2}|H|^{-1/2}\).
\item
  It needs only an optimizer and a Hessian --- the same machinery
  already used to find the MLE --- and its cost does not grow with
  dimension the way a quadrature grid's does.
\item
  BIC is the large-\(n\) simplification of the Laplace marginal
  likelihood, dropping \(O(1)\) terms that do not depend on the sample
  size.
\item
  On the running Gaussian marginal-likelihood example, Laplace matched
  Monte Carlo everywhere quadrature was tested, and remained accurate
  exactly where quadrature's fixed grid broke down --- because Laplace
  re-optimizes for every dataset instead of committing to one transform
  in advance.
\item
  It is deterministic, fast, and accurate to \(O(1/n)\), but blind to
  skewness and multimodality; the Monte Carlo methods of the next
  chapters remove these limitations at higher computational cost.
\end{itemize}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Rejection Sampling}\label{rejection-sampling}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(ars)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Monte Carlo integration estimates
\(E_f[a(\theta)] = \int a(\theta) f(\theta)\,d\theta\) from iid draws
\(\theta_1,\ldots,\theta_n \sim f\). The catch is the sampling step
itself: R has built-in samplers for standard families, but a posterior
\(f(\theta) \propto L(\theta)\pi(\theta)\) is typically known only up to
a constant, and there is no generic way to draw from it directly.
\textbf{Rejection sampling} solves this by producing \emph{exact} iid
draws from \(f\) using draws from an easier distribution \(g\),
following the recipe \emph{reject, reweight, repeat}.

\section{The Envelope and the
Algorithm}\label{the-envelope-and-the-algorithm}

\subsection{The envelope condition}\label{the-envelope-condition}

Suppose we can find a function \(g(x)\) such that

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(g(x) \ge f(x)\) for every \(x\) --- an \textbf{envelope} of \(f\),
  and
\item
  we can sample from the normalized density \(g(x)/\!\int g(x)\,dx\).
\end{itemize}

Neither \(f\) nor \(g\) needs to be normalized: \(f\) may be an
unnormalized posterior and \(g\) a scaled density \(M\,g_0(x)\) for a
sampler \(g_0\) we already have. The overall acceptance rate of the
algorithm below turns out to be the ratio of the two areas, \[
P(\text{accept}) = \frac{\int f(x)\,dx}{\int g(x)\,dx},
\] so a tight envelope wastes few draws and a loose one wastes many ---
the entire design problem is finding a \(g\) that is both easy to sample
and close to \(f\).

\subsection{The algorithm}\label{the-algorithm-1}

\textbf{Input}: target \(f\), envelope \(g \ge f\) with a sampler for
\(g(x)/\!\int g(x)\,dx\). For each draw needed, repeat until acceptance:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Draw \(x \sim g(x)/\!\int g(x)\,dx\).
\item
  Compute \(r = f(x)/g(x)\).
\item
  Draw \(U \sim \text{Unif}(0,1)\).
\item
  If \(U < r\), accept \(x\); otherwise return to step 1.
\end{enumerate}

Only the ratio \(f/g\) is ever evaluated, so unknown normalizing
constants in \(f\) (and in \(g\)) cancel automatically as long as the
\emph{versions actually used} satisfy \(g \ge f\). The number of
proposals needed per accepted draw is geometric with mean
\(1/P(\text{accept})\).

\subsection{An equivalent geometric
picture}\label{an-equivalent-geometric-picture}

An illuminating restatement: draw \(X \sim g\), then draw a height
\(V \sim \text{Unif}(0, g(X))\) (equivalently \(V = U\,g(X)\)), and
accept \(X\) if \(V < f(X)\). This is the same algorithm, since
\(V < f(X) \iff U < f(X)/g(X)\), but it exposes \emph{why} rejection
sampling works:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Steps ``draw \(X\sim g\), then \(V\sim\text{Unif}(0,g(X))\)'' place
  \((X,V)\) \textbf{uniformly} in the region under \(g\),
  \(G = \{(x,v): 0<v<g(x)\}\).
\item
  Keeping only points with \(v < f(x)\) restricts to the sub-region
  under \(f\), \(F = \{(x,v): 0<v<f(x)\} \subset G\).
\item
  A uniform point on \(G\), conditioned on landing in \(F\), is uniform
  on \(F\). The \(x\)-coordinate of a uniform point on \(F\) has density
  proportional to the \emph{height} of \(F\) at \(x\), which is
  \(f(x)\). That is exactly a draw from \(f/\!\int f\).
\end{itemize}
