}

\caption{\label{fig-envelope-scatter}Left: target \(f(x)=e^{-x^2/2}\)
(unnormalized standard normal) under its Laplace envelope
\(g(x) = e^{1/2-|x|}\), with the two areas shaded. Right: 600 candidate
points drawn uniformly under \(g\); the accepted ones (red, under \(f\)
too) are uniformly distributed under \(f\), so their \(x\)-coordinates
are draws from the normalized \(f\).}

\end{figure}%

\section{Example: Sampling a Normal from a Laplace
Envelope}\label{example-sampling-a-normal-from-a-laplace-envelope}

The figure above already uses a concrete, closed-form example worth
stating in full, because it is simple enough to check every claim
exactly. The target is the unnormalized standard normal density
\(f(x) = e^{-x^2/2}\). Take \(g_0(x) = \tfrac12 e^{-|x|}\), the standard
Laplace (double-exponential) density, which is trivial to sample by
inversion. Since \(e^{-x^2/2} \le e^{1/2-|x|}\) for all \(x\) (equality
exactly at \(|x| = 1\), where the two curves in the figure touch), the
envelope \[
g(x) = 2e^{1/2}\,g_0(x) = e^{1/2 - |x|} \ \ge\ f(x)
\] is valid, and the exact acceptance rate is \[
P(\text{accept}) = \frac{\int f(x)\,dx}{\int g(x)\,dx}
 = \frac{\sqrt{2\pi}}{2e^{1/2}} \approx 0.760.
\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{rnorm\_reject }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n) \{}
\NormalTok{    out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{); tries }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{0}
    \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(out) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ n) \{}
\NormalTok{        m }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(out)}
\NormalTok{        x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rlaplace}\NormalTok{(m); tries }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ tries }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ m}
\NormalTok{        out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(out, x[}\FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(m) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \FunctionTok{f\_demo}\NormalTok{(x) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{g\_demo}\NormalTok{(x)])}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ out, }\AttributeTok{acceptance =}\NormalTok{ n }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ tries)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{res }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm\_reject}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{2e5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{quantity =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"acceptance rate"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"sample mean"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"sample sd"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{theoretical =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ pi) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))), }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{simulated =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(res}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{acceptance, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(res}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{x), }\FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(res}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{x))}
\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrr@{}}
\caption{Theoretical vs.~simulated acceptance rate and sample moments
for the Laplace-envelope normal sampler.}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
quantity & theoretical & simulated \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
quantity & theoretical & simulated \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
acceptance rate & 0.7602 & 0.7595 \\
sample mean & 0.0000 & 0.0031 \\
sample sd & 1.0000 & 0.9991 \\
\end{longtable}

Roughly 3 in 4 proposals are kept --- a tight, efficient envelope, and
the benchmark against which the next example's Cauchy envelope should be
judged.

\subsection{Trying it interactively: tight vs.~loose
envelopes}\label{trying-it-interactively-tight-vs.-loose-envelopes}

The app below scales the envelope by a factor \(c \ge 1\),
\(g_c(x) = c\, e^{1/2-|x|}\): larger \(c\) keeps the envelope valid but
wastes more draws (acceptance rate \(= 0.760/c\)). Dragging \(c\) below
\(1\) shows what an \emph{invalid} envelope looks like --- the target
pokes through it, and rejection sampling would silently under-represent
that region.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{\#| label: envelope{-}tightness{-}app}
\NormalTok{\#| standalone: true}
\NormalTok{\#| viewerHeight: 560}

\NormalTok{library(shiny)}

\NormalTok{f\_demo \textless{}{-} function(x) exp({-}x\^{}2 / 2)}
\NormalTok{g\_demo \textless{}{-} function(x, c) c * exp(0.5 {-} abs(x))}
\NormalTok{rlaplace \textless{}{-} function(n) \{ u \textless{}{-} runif(n); ifelse(u \textless{} 0.5, log(2 * u), {-}log(2 * (1 {-} u))) \}}

\NormalTok{ui \textless{}{-} fluidPage(}
\NormalTok{    titlePanel(NULL),}
\NormalTok{    sidebarLayout(}
\NormalTok{        sidebarPanel(}
\NormalTok{            width = 4,}
\NormalTok{            sliderInput("c", "envelope scale c (g = c * e\^{}(1/2 {-} |x|))",}
\NormalTok{                        min = 0.6, max = 3, value = 1.2, step = 0.05),}
\NormalTok{            helpText("c \textgreater{}= 1 keeps g above f everywhere: a valid, if looser, ",}
\NormalTok{                     "envelope. c \textless{} 1 breaks the envelope condition near ",}
\NormalTok{                     "|x| = 1, where the target actually exceeds it."),}
\NormalTok{            actionButton("resample", "draw new points"),}
\NormalTok{            tableOutput("tab")}
\NormalTok{        ),}
\NormalTok{        mainPanel(}
\NormalTok{            width = 8,}
\NormalTok{            plotOutput("scatter", height = "440px")}
\NormalTok{        )}
\NormalTok{    )}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{server \textless{}{-} function(input, output, session) \{}

\NormalTok{    pts \textless{}{-} eventReactive(list(input$c, input$resample), \{}
\NormalTok{        set.seed(input$resample + 1)}
\NormalTok{        N \textless{}{-} 500}
\NormalTok{        x \textless{}{-} rlaplace(N)}
\NormalTok{        v \textless{}{-} runif(N) * g\_demo(x, input$c)}
\NormalTok{        list(x = x, v = v, acc = v \textless{} f\_demo(x))}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$scatter \textless{}{-} renderPlot(\{}
\NormalTok{        p \textless{}{-} pts()}
\NormalTok{        xs \textless{}{-} seq({-}6, 6, length.out = 500)}
\NormalTok{        par(mar = c(4, 4, 2, 1))}
\NormalTok{        plot(xs, g\_demo(xs, input$c), type = "l", lwd = 3, col = "steelblue",}
\NormalTok{             ylim = c(0, max(2.2, 1.05 * input$c * exp(0.5))),}
\NormalTok{             xlab = "x", ylab = "v", main = sprintf("c = \%.2f", input$c))}
\NormalTok{        lines(xs, f\_demo(xs), lwd = 3, col = "firebrick")}
\NormalTok{        bad \textless{}{-} f\_demo(xs) \textgreater{} g\_demo(xs, input$c)}
\NormalTok{        if (any(bad)) lines(xs[bad], f\_demo(xs)[bad], col = "black", lwd = 5)}
\NormalTok{        points(p$x[!p$acc], p$v[!p$acc], pch = 4, col = adjustcolor("gray40", 0.6), cex = 0.7)}
\NormalTok{        points(p$x[p$acc], p$v[p$acc], pch = 19, col = adjustcolor("firebrick", 0.7), cex = 0.7)}
\NormalTok{        legend("topright", bty = "n", lwd = c(3, 3, 5), col = c("firebrick", "steelblue", "black"),}
\NormalTok{               legend = c("target f", "envelope g", "f exceeds g here: envelope invalid"))}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$tab \textless{}{-} renderTable(\{}
\NormalTok{        p \textless{}{-} pts()}
\NormalTok{        data.frame(quantity = c("theoretical acceptance", "observed acceptance", "envelope valid?"),}
\NormalTok{                   value = c(sprintf("\%.3f", 0.7602 / input$c), sprintf("\%.3f", mean(p$acc)),}
\NormalTok{                             ifelse(input$c \textgreater{}= 1, "yes", "NO (c \textless{} 1)")))}
\NormalTok{    \}, colnames = FALSE)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{shinyApp(ui, server)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Reproducing this figure}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

The chunk above uses the
\href{https://github.com/quarto-ext/shinylive}{shinylive} extension,
compiled to WebAssembly so it runs in the reader's browser with no Shiny
server (\texttt{quarto\ add\ quarto-ext/shinylive}, then
\texttt{filters:\ {[}shinylive{]}} in the document or project YAML).
Only base R and \texttt{shiny} are available inside the app.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Example: Sampling a Gamma
Distribution}\label{example-sampling-a-gamma-distribution}

The Laplace envelope above was designed to match a normal target. A
different target needs a different envelope. Here is one built for
\(\text{Gamma}(\alpha, 1)\), \(\alpha > 1\), whose unnormalized
log-density is \((\alpha-1)\log x - x\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# log of a function that is (for alpha \textgreater{} 2) always above the Gamma(alpha, 1) log{-}density}
\NormalTok{log\_g\_gamma }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x, alpha) \{}
\NormalTok{    (alpha }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(alpha }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ (x }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ (alpha }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ alpha }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Where the envelope comes
from}\label{where-the-envelope-comes-from}

\texttt{log\_g\_gamma} is not an arbitrary formula. Up to an additive
constant that depends only on \(\alpha\), it is the log-density of a
\textbf{rescaled, shifted Cauchy} distribution: writing
\(Y = (\alpha-1) + \sqrt{2\alpha-1}\,C\) for a standard Cauchy \(C\), a
change of variables gives
\(\log f_Y(y) = -\log\pi - \tfrac12\log(2\alpha-1) - \log\!\big(1 + (y-(\alpha-1))^2/(2\alpha-1)\big)\),
which differs from \texttt{log\_g\_gamma(y,\ alpha)} only by a term that
does not depend on \(y\). We can check this numerically rather than take
it on faith:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{x\_check }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{by =} \FloatTok{0.37}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{alpha\_check }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{3.3}
\NormalTok{log\_f\_Y }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{dcauchy}\NormalTok{((x\_check }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ (alpha\_check }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ alpha\_check }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}
    \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ alpha\_check }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log\_g\_gamma}\NormalTok{(x\_check, alpha\_check) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ log\_f\_Y)   }\CommentTok{\# constant across x confirms the claim}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] 1.621804 1.621804
\end{verbatim}

So sampling the envelope is just sampling a (rescaled) Cauchy, and the
constant in \texttt{log\_g\_gamma} was chosen so that \(g\) touches the
Gamma density exactly at the shared mode \(x = \alpha - 1\). Whether
\(g\) stays \emph{above} the Gamma density everywhere else is a separate
question, and the docstring's claim ``alpha must be \textgreater{} 2''
is a claim about exactly that:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{alphas\_check }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{1.1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.9}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.99}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.01}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{xv }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{1e{-}6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \FloatTok{2e4}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{gaps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(alphas\_check, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(a) }\FunctionTok{min}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log\_g\_gamma}\NormalTok{(xv, a) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{dgamma}\NormalTok{(xv, a, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{alpha =}\NormalTok{ alphas\_check, }\AttributeTok{min\_log\_gap =}\NormalTok{ gaps, }\AttributeTok{envelope\_valid =}\NormalTok{ gaps }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}rrl@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-envelope-threshold}Minimum of log g(x) - log f(x)
over x \textgreater{} 0, for several alpha. Negative means the envelope
is violated somewhere.}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
alpha & min\_log\_gap & envelope\_valid \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
1.10 & -0.04987 & FALSE \\
1.50 & -0.12078 & FALSE \\
1.90 & -0.03898 & FALSE \\
1.99 & -0.00420 & FALSE \\
2.00 & 0.00000 & TRUE \\
2.01 & 0.00426 & TRUE \\
2.10 & 0.04544 & TRUE \\
2.50 & 0.28468 & TRUE \\
3.00 & 0.69315 & TRUE \\
4.50 & 2.45374 & TRUE \\

\end{longtable}

}

The gap is exactly \(0\) at \(\alpha = 2\) and negative below it: the
Cauchy-based envelope is tangent to the Gamma density at \(\alpha = 2\)
and crosses below it for smaller \(\alpha\), so \(\alpha > 2\) is
precisely the validity condition, not a conservative rule of thumb.

\subsection{Sampling function}\label{sampling-function}

A batch-and-refill implementation is both clearer and much faster than
drawing one candidate at a time in an R loop: generate a vector of
candidates, vectorize the accept/reject test, keep the survivors, and
top up only the shortfall.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sample\_gamma\_rej }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n, alpha, }\AttributeTok{max\_tries =} \FloatTok{2e6}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{); tries }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{0}
    \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(out) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ n) \{}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (tries }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ max\_tries) }\FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"acceptance rate too low for this alpha: envelope is too loose"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        m }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(out)}
\NormalTok{        x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rcauchy}\NormalTok{(m) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ alpha }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ (alpha }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        tries }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ tries }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ m}
\NormalTok{        keep }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(m)) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \FunctionTok{dgamma}\NormalTok{(x, }\AttributeTok{shape =}\NormalTok{ alpha, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log\_g\_gamma}\NormalTok{(x, alpha)}
\NormalTok{        out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(out, x[keep])}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \FunctionTok{structure}\NormalTok{(out[}\FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(n)], }\AttributeTok{accept.rate =}\NormalTok{ n }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ tries)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Test cases and efficiency}\label{test-cases-and-efficiency}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{alphas\_test }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{2.1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{gamma\_tests }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{lapply}\NormalTok{(alphas\_test, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(a) }\FunctionTok{sample\_gamma\_rej}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{, a))}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{alpha =}\NormalTok{ alphas\_test,}
    \AttributeTok{accept\_rate =} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(gamma\_tests, attr, }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\StringTok{"accept.rate"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{ks\_stat =} \FunctionTok{mapply}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x, a) }\FunctionTok{suppressWarnings}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{ks.test}\NormalTok{(x, }\StringTok{"pgamma"}\NormalTok{, a))}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{statistic,}
\NormalTok{                      gamma\_tests, alphas\_test)}
\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col.names =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"alpha"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"acceptance rate"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"KS statistic vs true Gamma"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    a }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ alphas\_test[i]}
    \FunctionTok{qqplot}\NormalTok{(gamma\_tests[[i]], }\FunctionTok{rgamma}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{, a), }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"rejection sample"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"rgamma sample"}\NormalTok{,}
           \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"alpha = \%g (accept \%.1f\%\%)"}\NormalTok{, a, }\DecValTok{100} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{attr}\NormalTok{(gamma\_tests[[i]], }\StringTok{"accept.rate"}\NormalTok{)))}
    \FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit10_rejection_sampling_files/figure-pdf/fig-gamma-tests-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-gamma-tests}Q-Q plots of rejection-sampled draws
against the true Gamma(alpha,1) quantiles, for three of the test cases:
the envelope produces exact draws regardless of alpha, only the cost per
draw changes.}

\end{figure}%

The Q-Q plots confirm exactness at every \(\alpha\): rejection sampling
never trades accuracy for speed, only speed changes. Efficient
random-number generators for the Gamma distribution (as used inside
\texttt{rgamma} itself) use envelopes that stay tight across a much
wider range of \(\alpha\); see Marsaglia \& Tsang (2000, \emph{ACM
TOMS}) for the algorithm R actually uses.

\section{Adaptive Rejection Sampling for Log-Concave
Targets}\label{adaptive-rejection-sampling-for-log-concave-targets}

Designing an envelope by hand, as above, is the hard part of rejection
sampling. For a broad and important class of targets it can be
automated.

\subsection{Log-concavity and the tangent
hull}\label{log-concavity-and-the-tangent-hull}

A density \(f\) is \textbf{log-concave} if \(\log f(x)\) is concave.
Many posteriors are: the normal, the gamma with shape \(\ge 1\), the
beta with both parameters \(\ge 1\), logistic-regression posteriors
under normal priors, and any product of log-concave factors (so most
conjugate and many non-conjugate exponential- family posteriors
qualify).

A concave function lies below every one of its tangent lines. So for
tangent lines \(\ell_1,\ldots,\ell_k\) to \(\log f\) at points
\(x_1 < \cdots < x_k\), the piecewise-linear \textbf{upper hull} \[
\log g(x) = \min_j \ell_j(x)
\] satisfies \(\log g \ge \log f\) everywhere, hence
\(g = e^{\log g} \ge f\): a valid envelope, built automatically from a
handful of tangent points and their slopes (the log-density's
derivative), with no hand-crafted formula. Because \(g\) is
piecewise-exponential, both its normalizing integral and its inverse CDF
are closed-form on each piece, so sampling from \(g\) is cheap.

\subsection{The adaptive algorithm}\label{the-adaptive-algorithm}

\textbf{Adaptive rejection sampling} (Gilks \& Wild, 1992) refines the
hull as it goes:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Start with a few tangent points, at least one on each side of the
  mode.
\item
  Draw \(x \sim g\), \(U \sim \text{Unif}(0,1)\); accept if
  \(U < f(x)/g(x)\).
\item
  Whenever a point is \textbf{rejected}, add it to the tangent set and
  rebuild the hull.
\end{enumerate}

Each rejection adds a tangent exactly where the envelope was loose, so
the acceptance rate rises toward \(1\) as sampling proceeds --- the
envelope adapts itself to the target instead of being fixed in advance.
Only \(\log f\) and its derivative are required (a derivative-free
variant uses secants in place of tangents). The \texttt{ars} package
implements this for any user-supplied log-concave \texttt{f}.

\subsection{Example: Truncated Normal}\label{example-truncated-normal}

The normal density is log-concave, so \texttt{ars} applies directly to a
normal truncated to \([l, u]\) --- useful whenever a parameter has a
hard bound (a variance, a probability, a physically constrained rate).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# direct rejection sampling for a truncated normal: draw from N(0,1),}
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# keep values inside [lb, ub]; vectorized batch{-}and{-}refill}
\NormalTok{sample\_tnorm\_drs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n, }\AttributeTok{lb =} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ub =} \ConstantTok{Inf}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{while}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(out) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ n) \{}
\NormalTok{        x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(out))}
\NormalTok{        out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(out, x[x }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=}\NormalTok{ lb }\SpecialCharTok{\&}\NormalTok{ x }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=}\NormalTok{ ub])}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    out}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# adaptive rejection sampling via the ars package}
\NormalTok{sample\_tnorm\_ars }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n, lb, ub) \{}

For \([-50,-40]\) or \([100,110]\) the naive sampler's acceptance
probability is astronomically small
(\(P(-50<Z<-40) \approx 10^{-221}\)), so it is left as \texttt{NA}
rather than run to timeout; \texttt{ars} samples these windows in a
fraction of a second because its envelope is built from the truncated
density itself, never from the untruncated normal.

\section{Summary}\label{summary-4}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Rejection sampling turns draws from an envelope \(g \ge f\) into
  \emph{exact} iid draws from \(f\), using only the ratio \(f/g\); no
  normalizing constants are needed. The geometric view --- uniform
  points under \(g\), keep those under \(f\) --- explains why this works
  and why efficiency is exactly the ratio of the two areas.
\item
  A good envelope is tight (high acceptance) and easy to sample; finding
  one by hand, as in the Cauchy envelope for the Gamma distribution
  above, requires matching the target's tails and curvature and can be
  delicate.
\item
  Adaptive rejection sampling automates envelope construction for any
  log-concave target via tangent lines to \(\log f\), tightening the
  envelope on every rejection.
\item
  Limitations: an envelope with a bounded ratio \(g/f\) may not exist
  when \(f\) has heavier tails than any convenient \(g\), and in high
  dimensions the acceptance rate of a product envelope typically decays
  exponentially with dimension, for the same curse-of-dimensionality
  reason quadrature grids fail (Unit 8). These limitations motivate
  \textbf{importance sampling}, which reweights instead of rejecting,
  and \textbf{Markov chain Monte Carlo}, which builds a chain that
  adapts to the target as it runs --- the subjects of the next two
  chapters.
\end{itemize}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Importance Sampling}\label{importance-sampling}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(ars)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Rejection sampling turns draws from an envelope \(g \ge f\) into exact
draws from \(f\), but every rejected proposal is wasted, and in the
tails of a poor envelope almost every proposal is rejected.
\textbf{Importance sampling} keeps every proposal instead of discarding
most of them: draw from an easier function \(g\) and attach a
\emph{weight} to each draw that corrects for sampling from the wrong
distribution. The price is that the resulting sample is no longer an
exact iid sample from \(f\) --- it is a \emph{weighted} sample --- but
nothing is ever thrown away, and no envelope condition \(g \ge f\) is
required.

\section{The Reweighting Idea}\label{the-reweighting-idea}

\subsection{The goal}\label{the-goal}

As in the last two chapters, \(f(\theta)\) is an \textbf{unnormalized}
function of interest --- typically \(f(\theta) = L(\theta)\pi(\theta)\)
for a likelihood \(L\) and prior \(\pi\) --- and we want two kinds of
integral: \[
A = E_f\big[a(\theta)\big] = \frac{\int a(\theta)\,f(\theta)\,d\theta}{\int f(\theta)\,d\theta},
\qquad\qquad
C_f = \int f(\theta)\,d\theta,
\] a posterior expectation and a normalizing constant (the marginal
likelihood when \(f = L\pi\)). Plain Monte Carlo needs draws from
\(f/C_f\), which we generally cannot produce.

Suppose instead we can draw from another function \(g(\theta)\) (also
possibly unnormalized, with \(C_g = \int g(\theta)\,d\theta\)). Wherever
\(f\) puts mass, \(g\) must too: \[
\{\theta : g(\theta) > 0\} \ \supseteq\ \{\theta : f(\theta) > 0\}.
\] Unlike rejection sampling, \(g\) need \textbf{not} dominate \(f\)
everywhere --- there is no requirement \(g \ge f\). This is what makes
importance sampling applicable in situations where no usable envelope
exists.

\subsection{Two estimators}\label{two-estimators}

Define the \textbf{importance weight}
\(w(\theta) = f(\theta)/g(\theta)\). For any \(a\), \[
E_f\big[a(\theta)\big]
= \int a(\theta)\,\frac{f(\theta)}{C_f}\,d\theta
= \frac{C_g}{C_f}\, E_g\big[a(\theta)\,w(\theta)\big],
\] and taking \(a \equiv 1\) gives \(C_f/C_g = E_g[w(\theta)]\).
Combining the two, \[
E_f\big[a(\theta)\big] = \frac{E_g\big[a(\theta)\,w(\theta)\big]}{E_g\big[w(\theta)\big]}.
\] Draw \(\theta_1,\ldots,\theta_S \overset{iid}{\sim} g/C_g\) and
compute \(w_s = w(\theta_s)\). Two estimators follow directly:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Ratio of normalizing constants}:
  \(\widehat{C_f/C_g} = \frac1S\sum_s w_s\), unbiased for \(C_f/C_g\);
  if \(g\) is already a normalized density (\(C_g=1\)) this estimates
  \(C_f\) itself.
\item
  \textbf{Self-normalized expectation}:
  \(\hat A = \sum_{s=1}^S a(\theta_s)\,\tilde w_s\), with normalized
  weights \(\tilde w_s = w_s / \sum_j w_j\) summing to \(1\). This turns
  the proposal sample into a \emph{weighted sample from \(f\)}: any
  unknown constants in \(f\) and \(g\) cancel in the ratio, so
  \(\hat A\) needs only \(f\) and \(g\) up to a proportionality
  constant. It is a ratio of two averages and so is biased with bias
  \(O(1/S)\), but consistent.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Effective sample size}\label{effective-sample-size}

\(\hat A\) is a weighted average of \(a(\theta_s)\): draws where
\(f \gg g\) count more, draws where \(f \ll g\) count less. How many
\emph{equally weighted} draws is the weighted sample worth? \[
S_{\text{eff}} = \frac{1}{\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^S \tilde w_s^2}.
\] If the weights are all equal, \(\tilde w_s = 1/S\) and
\(S_{\text{eff}} =
1/(S \cdot 1/S^2) = S\): the proposal is shaped just like the target,
and every draw counts fully. If one weight dominates all the others,
\(\tilde w_s
\approx 1\) for that draw and \(S_{\text{eff}} \approx 1\): only one
draw is doing any work. A variance heuristic makes this precise:
\(\text{Var}_g(\hat A) \approx \text{Var}_f(a)/S_{\text{eff}}\), the
same form as for an iid sample of size \(S_{\text{eff}}\) from \(f\)
itself.

The catch, central to this chapter, is that \(S_{\text{eff}}\) is
computed \textbf{only from the weights actually observed}. A proposal
that never visits the part of \(\theta\)-space where \(f\) and \(g\)
disagree most will report a reassuring \(S_{\text{eff}}\) while being
badly wrong --- this is exactly the subject of the ``bad stories''
below.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# effective sample size from raw (possibly unnormalized) weights}
\NormalTok{ess }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(w) }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(w)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(w}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# numerically stable log(sum(exp(lx)))}
\NormalTok{log\_sum\_exp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(lx) \{ m }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{max}\NormalTok{(lx); m }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(lx }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ m))) \}}
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# effective sample size computed from LOG weights (avoids under/overflow)}
\NormalTok{log\_ess }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(lw) }\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{log\_sum\_exp}\NormalTok{(lw) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log\_sum\_exp}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ lw))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{A Good Story: Estimating a Normal Tail
Probability}\label{a-good-story-estimating-a-normal-tail-probability}

\subsection{From plain Monte Carlo to importance
sampling}\label{from-plain-monte-carlo-to-importance-sampling}

For \(X \sim N(0,1)\), take \(f(x) = \varphi(x)\,I(a<x<b)\), the
standard normal density truncated to \([a,b]\), so that
\(C_f = P(a<X<b)\). Plain Monte Carlo draws \(X_i \sim N(0,1)\) and
averages the indicator \(I(a<X_i<b)\); this wastes every draw that lands
outside \([a,b]\); for a proposal \(g\) supported on \([a,b]\) itself,
every draw contributes.

The simplest such \(g\) is uniform on \([a,b]\):
\(g(x) = I(a\le x\le b)/(b-a)\), so \(w(x) = (b-a)\varphi(x)\) and
\(\widehat{C_f} = (b-a)\cdot\frac1S\sum_s
\varphi(x_s)\) --- the \emph{Monte Carlo} version of the midpoint rule
from the quadrature chapter: a random grid instead of an equally spaced
one, evaluating the same weighted sum.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{est\_normprob\_mc   }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(A, S) \{ X }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(S); }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{((X }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ A[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]) }\SpecialCharTok{\&}\NormalTok{ (X }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ A[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{])) \}}
\NormalTok{est\_normprob\_imps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(A, S) \{ X }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(S, A[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], A[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]); }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(X)) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (A[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ A[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]) \}}

\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{cases }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{mse\_tbl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{do.call}\NormalTok{(rbind, }\FunctionTok{lapply}\NormalTok{(cases, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(A) \{}
\NormalTok{    tp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{pnorm}\NormalTok{(A[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{pnorm}\NormalTok{(A[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
\NormalTok{    mc  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{est\_normprob\_mc}\NormalTok{(A, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{    imp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{est\_normprob\_imps}\NormalTok{(A, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{))}
    \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{interval =} \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"(\%g, \%g)"}\NormalTok{, A[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], A[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]),}
               \AttributeTok{true\_prob =} \FunctionTok{formatC}\NormalTok{(tp, }\AttributeTok{format =} \StringTok{"e"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{),}

               \AttributeTok{mse\_importance =} \FunctionTok{formatC}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{((imp }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ tp)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{format =} \StringTok{"e"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{\}))}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(mse\_tbl, }\AttributeTok{align =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"r"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"r"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"r"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

For the moderate interval \((-2,2)\) both methods work; for the rarer
event \((5,6)\), where plain Monte Carlo almost never lands an
observation inside the interval, the importance-sampling error is many
orders of magnitude smaller --- the uniform proposal \emph{guarantees}
every draw falls in \([a,b]\).

\subsection{An underflowing tail
probability}\label{an-underflowing-tail-probability}

Push the interval further out, to \(A = (50,100)\). Now even
\(\varphi(50)\) itself underflows to exactly \(0\) in double precision
(its log is about \(-1251\), far below the smallest representable log),
so every calculation must be carried out on the log scale.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{a }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{; b }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{100}
\NormalTok{log\_minus\_exp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(la, lb) la }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{log1p}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(lb }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ la))}
\NormalTok{log\_exact }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log\_minus\_exp}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{pnorm}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{a, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{), }\FunctionTok{pnorm}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{b, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{log\_exact}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
[1] -1254.831
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Matching the proposal to the tail: a better
envelope}\label{matching-the-proposal-to-the-tail-a-better-envelope}

A uniform proposal on \((50,100)\) still works in principle, but
\(\varphi\) decays so fast across the interval that the weights vary
enormously: nearly all of the probability mass under \(\varphi\)
restricted to \((50,100)\) sits within a sliver near \(x=50\), so a
uniform draw almost never lands where the weight is large. A
\textbf{shifted exponential} proposal with rate \(\lambda \approx a\)
matches that decay and keeps the weights nearly constant: \[
g(x) = \frac{\lambda e^{-\lambda(x-a)}}{1-e^{-\lambda(b-a)}}\,I(a\le x\le b).
\]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{S }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{1e4}\NormalTok{; lam }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ a}
\NormalTok{x\_u }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(S, a, b);  lw\_u }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(b }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ a) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x\_u, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{u\_e }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(S); x\_e }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ a }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ u\_e }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{lam }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (b }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ a)))) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ lam        }\CommentTok{\# inverse cdf}
\NormalTok{lg\_e }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(lam) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ lam }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (x\_e }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ a) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log1p}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{lam }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (b }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ a)))}
\NormalTok{lw\_e }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x\_e, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ lg\_e}

\NormalTok{tail\_tbl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{proposal   =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"uniform on (a, b)"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"shifted exponential"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{log\_C\_f    =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log\_sum\_exp}\NormalTok{(lw\_u) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(S), }\FunctionTok{log\_sum\_exp}\NormalTok{(lw\_e) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(S)),}
    \AttributeTok{S\_eff      =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log\_ess}\NormalTok{(lw\_u), }\FunctionTok{log\_ess}\NormalTok{(lw\_e))}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(tail\_tbl, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrr@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-tail-proposals}Two proposals for the same rare
event, S = 10,000 draws: log-scale weights avoid the underflow that
would occur if densities were multiplied directly.}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
proposal & log\_C\_f & S\_eff \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
uniform on (a, b) & -1254.489 & 7.8 \\
shifted exponential & -1254.831 & 10000.0 \\

\end{longtable}

}

Both proposals give a log-probability close to -1254.831, but their
effective sample sizes are worlds apart: the exponential proposal keeps
essentially all \ensuremath{10^{4}} draws informative, while the uniform
proposal's effective sample size collapses because almost all of its
weight concentrates on the handful of draws nearest \(a\). Shaping \(g\)
like \(f\) is not just elegant --- it is what makes the computation
efficient.

\section{Two Bad Stories: When Reweighting Fails
Silently}\label{two-bad-stories-when-reweighting-fails-silently}

\(S_{\text{eff}}\) diagnoses only \emph{one} kind of failure: weights
that vary wildly among the draws actually observed. It says nothing
about mass that \(g\) never visits at all, and a badly wrong estimate
can carry a perfectly healthy \(S_{\text{eff}}\).

\subsection{A proposal that misses a
mode}\label{a-proposal-that-misses-a-mode}

Let
\(f(\theta) = \tfrac12\varphi(\theta+5) + \tfrac12\varphi(\theta-5)\) (a
50/50 mixture, so \(C_f=1\) and \(E_f(\theta)=0\)) and
\(g(\theta) = \varphi(\theta+5)\). Both are positive everywhere, so the
support requirement holds. But \(g\) places essentially all of its mass
near \(\theta=-5\) and, for \(\theta\) near \(-5\), \[
w(\theta) = \tfrac12 + \tfrac12\,\frac{\varphi(\theta-5)}{\varphi(\theta+5)}
= \tfrac12 + \tfrac12\, e^{10\theta} \ \approx\ \tfrac12,
\] so every weight the sampler actually sees looks almost identical.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{S }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{5000}
\NormalTok{f\_mix }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(t) }\FloatTok{0.5} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(t }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FloatTok{0.5} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(t }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{g\_mix }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(t) }\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(t }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{th }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(S, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{w  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{f\_mix}\NormalTok{(th) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{g\_mix}\NormalTok{(th)}
\NormalTok{wt }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ w }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(w)}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(f\_mix, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{""}\NormalTok{,}
      \AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"target f and proposal g"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.42}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{curve}\NormalTok{(g\_mix, }\AttributeTok{add =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{rug}\NormalTok{(th[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{adjustcolor}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{legend}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"topright"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{bty =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{legend =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"f"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"g"}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{hist}\NormalTok{(wt }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ S, }\AttributeTok{breaks =} \DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"gray85"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{border =} \StringTok{"white"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(S }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{tilde}\NormalTok{(w)[s]),}
     \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"weights: S\_eff = \%.0f of \%d"}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{ess}\NormalTok{(w), S))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit11_importance_sampling_files/figure-pdf/fig-missed-mode-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-missed-mode}Left: target f (mixture of two normals)
and proposal g (only the left component). Right: normalized weights look
almost flat --- S\_eff is close to S --- even though the estimate is
badly wrong.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{missed\_mode\_tbl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{quantity =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"C\_f / C\_g (true 1)"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"E\_f[theta] (true 0)"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"S\_eff (of S = 5000)"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{estimate =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(w), }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(th }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ wt), }\FunctionTok{ess}\NormalTok{(w))}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(missed\_mode\_tbl, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lr@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-missed-mode}Missed-mode failure: the effective
sample size looks fine, but both quantities of interest are far from the
truth.}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
quantity & estimate \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
C\_f / C\_g (true 1) & 0.50 \\
E\_f{[}theta{]} (true 0) & -4.96 \\
S\_eff (of S = 5000) & 5000.00 \\

\end{longtable}

}

The estimate of \(C_f/C_g\) comes out near 0.5 instead of \(1\), and
\(E_f[\theta]\) near -4.96 instead of \(0\) --- both wrong by a wide
margin --- while \(S_{\text{eff}}\) reports essentially the full sample
size. The draws that would reveal the problem, near \(\theta=+5\), have
probability under \(g\) of order \(\Phi(-10) \approx 10^{-23}\): they
simply never appear, at any practical \(S\).

\subsection{A proposal narrower than the target's
support}\label{a-proposal-narrower-than-the-targets-support}

The same failure in its starkest form:
\(f(\theta) = I(0\le\theta\le2)\), \(g(\theta) = I(0\le\theta\le1)\).
The true ratio is \(C_f/C_g = 2\), but every draw from \(g\) lands in
\((0,1)\), where \(w(\theta)=f(\theta)/g(\theta)=1\) identically.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{f\_indicator }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\FunctionTok{as.numeric}\NormalTok{(x }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{0} \SpecialCharTok{\&}\NormalTok{ x }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)   }\CommentTok{\# f = I(0 \textless{}= theta \textless{}= 2)}
\NormalTok{g\_indicator }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\FunctionTok{as.numeric}\NormalTok{(x }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}=} \DecValTok{0} \SpecialCharTok{\&}\NormalTok{ x }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}=} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)   }\CommentTok{\# g = I(0 \textless{}= theta \textless{}= 1)}
\NormalTok{support\_tbl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{S =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{1e2}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1e3}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1e4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1e5}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{estimate =} \FunctionTok{sapply}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{1e2}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1e3}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1e4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1e5}\NormalTok{), }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(S) \{}
\NormalTok{        th }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(S, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)                              }\CommentTok{\# draws from g}
        \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{f\_indicator}\NormalTok{(th) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{g\_indicator}\NormalTok{(th))            }\CommentTok{\# weight is 1 for every draw}
\NormalTok{    \})}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(support\_tbl, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}rr@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-support-violation}Estimated C\_f / C\_g (true value
2) as the sample size grows: no amount of additional sampling helps,
because g never visits (1, 2{]}.}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
S & estimate \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
1e+02 & 1 \\
1e+03 & 1 \\
1e+04 & 1 \\
1e+05 & 1 \\

\end{longtable}

}

The estimate sits at \(1\) regardless of \(S\): this is a bias that more
sampling cannot fix, because it comes from where \(g\) looks, not from
how much it looks there. The reverse situation --- \(g\) wider than
\(f\) --- is harmless: draws outside \(\text{supp}(f)\) simply receive
weight \(0\) and are correctly down-weighted. Together with the
missed-mode example, the lesson is that \(S_{\text{eff}}\) alone cannot
certify a good proposal; the proposal must be checked against what is
actually known about where \(f\) puts its mass.

\subsection{Explore it interactively}\label{explore-it-interactively-2}

The app below reproduces the missed-mode example with an adjustable
proposal: move its mean and spread and watch the effective sample size
stay high while the estimated mean drifts away from the true value of
\(0\) whenever the proposal fails to reach both components of the
target.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{\#| label: missed{-}mode{-}app}
\NormalTok{\#| standalone: true}
\NormalTok{\#| viewerHeight: 620}

\NormalTok{library(shiny)}

\NormalTok{f\_mix \textless{}{-} function(t) 0.5 * dnorm(t + 5) + 0.5 * dnorm(t {-} 5)}
\NormalTok{ess   \textless{}{-} function(w) sum(w)\^{}2 / sum(w\^{}2)}

\NormalTok{ui \textless{}{-} fluidPage(}
\NormalTok{    titlePanel(NULL),}
\NormalTok{    sidebarLayout(}
\NormalTok{        sidebarPanel(}
\NormalTok{            width = 4,}
\NormalTok{            sliderInput("mean\_g", "proposal mean", min = {-}9, max = 9, value = {-}5, step = 0.5),}
\NormalTok{            sliderInput("sd\_g", "proposal sd", min = 0.5, max = 8, value = 1, step = 0.5),}
\NormalTok{            helpText("Target f is a 50/50 mixture of N({-}5, 1) and N(5, 1), so the ",}
\NormalTok{                     "truth is C\_f/C\_g = 1 and E\_f(theta) = 0 when g integrates to 1. ",}
\NormalTok{                     "A narrow proposal centred on one mode gives a deceptively healthy ",}
\NormalTok{                     "S\_eff while both estimates are wrong; only a proposal wide enough ",}
\NormalTok{                     "or centred between the modes recovers the truth."),}
\NormalTok{            tableOutput("tab")}
\NormalTok{        ),}
\NormalTok{        mainPanel(}
\NormalTok{            width = 8,}
\NormalTok{            plotOutput("curves", height = "260px"),}
\NormalTok{            plotOutput("weights", height = "260px")}
\NormalTok{        )}
\NormalTok{    )}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{server \textless{}{-} function(input, output, session) \{}

\NormalTok{    draws \textless{}{-} reactive(\{}
\NormalTok{        set.seed(1)}
\NormalTok{        th \textless{}{-} rnorm(5000, input$mean\_g, input$sd\_g)}
\NormalTok{        w  \textless{}{-} f\_mix(th) / dnorm(th, input$mean\_g, input$sd\_g)}
\NormalTok{        list(th = th, w = w)}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$curves \textless{}{-} renderPlot(\{}
\NormalTok{        d \textless{}{-} draws()}
\NormalTok{        par(mar = c(4, 4, 2, 1))}
\NormalTok{        curve(f\_mix, {-}12, 12, lwd = 3, col = "firebrick", xlab = expression(theta), ylab = "",}
\NormalTok{              main = "target f and proposal g", ylim = c(0, 0.45))}
\NormalTok{        curve(dnorm(x, input$mean\_g, input$sd\_g), add = TRUE, lwd = 3, col = "steelblue")}
\NormalTok{        rug(d$th[1:300], col = adjustcolor("steelblue", 0.5))}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$weights \textless{}{-} renderPlot(\{}
\NormalTok{        d \textless{}{-} draws(); wt \textless{}{-} d$w / sum(d$w)}
\NormalTok{        par(mar = c(4, 4, 2, 1))}
\NormalTok{        hist(wt * length(d$w), breaks = 40, col = "gray85", border = "white",}
\NormalTok{             xlab = expression(S * tilde(w)[s]),}
\NormalTok{             main = sprintf("S\_eff = \%.0f of \%d", ess(d$w), length(d$w)))}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$tab \textless{}{-} renderTable(\{}
\NormalTok{        d \textless{}{-} draws(); wt \textless{}{-} d$w / sum(d$w)}
\NormalTok{        data.frame(quantity = c("C\_f / C\_g (true 1)", "E\_f[theta] (true 0)", "S\_eff (of 5000)"),}
\NormalTok{                   estimate = c(mean(d$w), sum(d$th * wt), ess(d$w)))}
\NormalTok{    \}, digits = 3, rownames = FALSE)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{shinyApp(ui, server)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Reproducing this figure}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

See the callout in \hyperref[numerical-quadrature]{Numerical Quadrature}
for how to install the \texttt{shinylive} extension used above.

\section{Estimating a Truncated-Normal
Expectation}\label{estimating-a-truncated-normal-expectation}

Return to a truncated \(N(0,1)\) on \([l,u]\), and estimate
\(E\big[a(X)\mid l<X<u\big]\) for \(a(x)=x^2\). Two competing samplers:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Importance sampling} with \(g(x) = I(l\le x\le u)/(u-l)\)
  (uniform), giving \(w(x) = \varphi(x)\) up to the constant \((u-l)\)
  that cancels in the self-normalized estimator.
\item
  \textbf{Rejection sampling} via adaptive rejection sampling
  (\texttt{ars} package, applicable because \(\log\varphi\) is concave),
  producing exact iid draws.
\end{itemize}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# importance{-}sampling estimate of E[a(X) | l \textless{} X \textless{} u], X \textasciitilde{} N(0,1)}
\NormalTok{est\_tnorm\_imps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(a\_fn, l, u, S) \{}
\NormalTok{    X }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(S, l, u)}
\NormalTok{    W }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(X)}
\NormalTok{    ahat }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{a\_fn}\NormalTok{(X) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ W) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(W)}
    \FunctionTok{attr}\NormalTok{(ahat, }\StringTok{"ess"}\NormalTok{) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{ess}\NormalTok{(W)}
\NormalTok{    ahat}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# exact draws via adaptive rejection sampling (log phi is concave)}
\NormalTok{sample\_tnorm\_ars }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n, l, u) \{}
\NormalTok{    logf   }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    fprima }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{x}
    \FunctionTok{ars}\NormalTok{(n, }\AttributeTok{f =}\NormalTok{ logf, }\AttributeTok{fprima =}\NormalTok{ fprima, }\AttributeTok{x =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(l, (l }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ u) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, u),}
        \AttributeTok{lb =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ub =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlb =}\NormalTok{ l, }\AttributeTok{xub =}\NormalTok{ u)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The original version of \texttt{sample\_tnorm\_ars} always drew exactly
\(10{,}000\) points regardless of the requested \texttt{n}; the version
above respects \texttt{n}, which matters for the timing comparison next.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{a\_fn }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) x}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{midpoint\_ref }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(l, u, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{100000}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    h }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ (u }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ l) }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ n; xm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ l }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(n) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ h}
    \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{a\_fn}\NormalTok{(xm) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(xm)) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ h }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{pnorm}\NormalTok{(u) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{pnorm}\NormalTok{(l))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{intervals }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{tnorm\_tbl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{do.call}\NormalTok{(rbind, }\FunctionTok{lapply}\NormalTok{(intervals, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(iv) \{}
\NormalTok{    l }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ iv[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]; u }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ iv[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]; ref }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{midpoint\_ref}\NormalTok{(l, u)}
\NormalTok{    t\_imps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{system.time}\NormalTok{(EA\_imps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{est\_tnorm\_imps}\NormalTok{(a\_fn, l, u, }\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{)))}
\NormalTok{    t\_rej  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{system.time}\NormalTok{(EA\_rej  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{a\_fn}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sample\_tnorm\_ars}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, l, u)))))}
    \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{interval =} \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"(\%g, \%g)"}\NormalTok{, l, u), }\AttributeTok{reference =}\NormalTok{ ref,}
               \AttributeTok{mean\_imps =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(EA\_imps), }\AttributeTok{mse\_imps =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{((EA\_imps }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ ref)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}
               \AttributeTok{ms\_imps =} \DecValTok{1000} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ t\_imps[[}\StringTok{"elapsed"}\NormalTok{]] }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{,}
               \AttributeTok{mean\_rej =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(EA\_rej), }\AttributeTok{mse\_rej =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{((EA\_rej }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ ref)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{),}
               \AttributeTok{ms\_rej =} \DecValTok{1000} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ t\_rej[[}\StringTok{"elapsed"}\NormalTok{]] }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}))}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(tnorm\_tbl, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 14\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1268}}
  >{\raggedleft\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 14\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1408}}
  >{\raggedleft\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 14\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1408}}
