}

Both estimators are unbiased for this smooth, bounded target and agree
with the midpoint-rule reference; importance sampling is markedly faster
here because it needs no accept/reject loop or envelope construction, at
the cost of returning a \emph{weighted} rather than an exact sample.

\section{Application: Marginal Likelihood via Importance
Sampling}\label{application-marginal-likelihood-via-importance-sampling}

\subsection{Setup}\label{setup-1}

The \hyperref[numerical-quadrature]{quadrature} and
Laplace-approximation chapters compute the marginal likelihood
\(P(y) = \int L(\theta)\pi(\theta)\,d\theta\) of the same conjugate-free
Gaussian model --- unknown mean \(\mu\) and \(w=\log\sigma^2\),
independent normal priors --- by a product grid and by a
mode-and-Hessian approximation, respectively. Importance sampling offers
a third route, and one that comes with a built-in accuracy diagnostic
(\(S_{\text{eff}}\)) that neither of the other two methods provides
directly.

Take \(g(\mu,w)\) to be the \textbf{Gaussian approximation to the
posterior} obtained by a Laplace fit: locate the posterior mode
\((\hat\mu,\hat w)\) by optimization, take
\(g = N_2\big((\hat\mu,\hat w),\, H^{-1}\big)\) for \(H\) the Hessian of
\(-\log[L\pi]\) at the mode, and importance-sample from \(g\) with
\(f = L\pi\): \[
\widehat{P(y)} = \frac{1}{S}\sum_{s=1}^S w_s, \qquad
w_s = \frac{L(\theta_s)\pi(\theta_s)}{g(\theta_s)}, \qquad \theta_s \sim g.
\] Because \(g\) is shaped like the posterior near its mode, this is a
``good story'' proposal by construction --- in sharp contrast to
sampling directly from the (typically much wider, uninformative) prior,
which is exactly the \texttt{log\_marlik\_mc} function used elsewhere in
this book as a naive-Monte-Carlo check: it is importance sampling with
\(g = \pi\), and its weights are just the likelihood values
\(L(\theta_s)\).

\subsection{An efficient
implementation}\label{an-efficient-implementation-1}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{log\_lik   }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x, mu, w) }\FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x, mu, }\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(w }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{log\_prior }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(mu, w, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w) \{}
    \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(mu, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(w, w\_0, sigma\_w, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{neg\_log\_post }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(theta, x, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w) \{}
    \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{log\_lik}\NormalTok{(x, theta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], theta[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}
        \FunctionTok{log\_prior}\NormalTok{(theta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], theta[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{], mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# log density of N(mu, A\^{}\{{-}1\}) at every column of a 2 x S matrix of points at once,}
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# replacing a per{-}point apply() call with one vectorized quadratic form}
\NormalTok{log\_dmvnorm\_batch }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(Theta, mu, A) \{}
\NormalTok{    Tc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ Theta }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mu}
\NormalTok{    quad }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{colSums}\NormalTok{((A }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%}\NormalTok{ Tc) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ Tc)}
    \FloatTok{0.5} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{nrow}\NormalTok{(Theta) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ pi) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{svd}\NormalTok{(A)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{d)) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ quad)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# log marginal likelihood by importance sampling from a Laplace{-}shaped Gaussian}
\NormalTok{log\_marlik\_imps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w, S) \{}
\NormalTok{    fit }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{nlm}\NormalTok{(neg\_log\_post, }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(x), }\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(stats}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{var}\NormalTok{(x))), }\AttributeTok{hessian =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{,}
               \AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ x, }\AttributeTok{mu\_0 =}\NormalTok{ mu\_0, }\AttributeTok{sigma\_mu =}\NormalTok{ sigma\_mu, }\AttributeTok{w\_0 =}\NormalTok{ w\_0, }\AttributeTok{sigma\_w =}\NormalTok{ sigma\_w)}
\NormalTok{    mu\_hat }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ fit}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{estimate; H }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ fit}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{hessian}
\NormalTok{    L }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{t}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{chol}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{solve}\NormalTok{(H)))                        }\CommentTok{\# Sigma = H\^{}\{{-}1\} = L L\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{    Theta }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ L }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ S), }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, S) }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ mu\_hat}

\NormalTok{    log\_g }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log\_dmvnorm\_batch}\NormalTok{(Theta, mu\_hat, H)}
\NormalTok{    log\_f }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{apply}\NormalTok{(Theta, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, neg\_log\_post, }\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ x, }\AttributeTok{mu\_0 =}\NormalTok{ mu\_0, }\AttributeTok{sigma\_mu =}\NormalTok{ sigma\_mu,}
                     \AttributeTok{w\_0 =}\NormalTok{ w\_0, }\AttributeTok{sigma\_w =}\NormalTok{ sigma\_w)}
\NormalTok{    lw }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ log\_f }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ log\_g}
    \FunctionTok{structure}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log\_sum\_exp}\NormalTok{(lw) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(S), }\AttributeTok{ess =} \FunctionTok{log\_ess}\NormalTok{(lw))}
\NormalTok{\}}

\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# log marginal likelihood by naive Monte Carlo from the prior (g = pi): a}
\DocumentationTok{\#\#\# second, independent importance{-}sampling estimator with an unfavourable g}
\NormalTok{log\_marlik\_prior }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x, mu\_0, sigma\_mu, w\_0, sigma\_w, S) \{}
\NormalTok{    mus }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(S, mu\_0, sigma\_mu); ws }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(S, w\_0, sigma\_w)}
\NormalTok{    lw }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(S), }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(i) }\FunctionTok{log\_lik}\NormalTok{(x, mus[i], ws[i]), }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
    \FunctionTok{structure}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log\_sum\_exp}\NormalTok{(lw) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(S), }\AttributeTok{ess =} \FunctionTok{log\_ess}\NormalTok{(lw))}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Replacing the two \texttt{apply()} calls used in earlier drafts of this
code --- one for the proposal log-density, one for the log-posterior ---
with a single vectorized quadratic form (\texttt{log\_dmvnorm\_batch})
removes \(S\) separate R function calls for the proposal evaluation;
only the data-dependent log-likelihood still needs one call per draw,
since it sums over the \(n\) observations for each \(\theta_s\)
separately.

\subsection{Checking accuracy}\label{checking-accuracy}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x\_small }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{);  x\_large }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{check\_tbl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{do.call}\NormalTok{(rbind, }\FunctionTok{lapply}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n10 =}\NormalTok{ x\_small, }\AttributeTok{n200 =}\NormalTok{ x\_large), }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x) \{}
\NormalTok{    reps }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{replicate}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{log\_marlik\_imps}\NormalTok{(x, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{simplify =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    vals }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(reps, as.numeric, }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{    esss }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{vapply}\NormalTok{(reps, attr, }\FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{which =} \StringTok{"ess"}\NormalTok{)}
    \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mean\_log\_ml =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(vals), }\AttributeTok{sd\_log\_ml =} \FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(vals), }\AttributeTok{mean\_ess =} \FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(esss))}
\NormalTok{\}))}
\NormalTok{check\_tbl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{cbind}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{dataset =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"n = 10"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"n = 200"}\NormalTok{), check\_tbl)}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(check\_tbl, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llrrr@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-marlik-check}Log marginal likelihood by importance
sampling (Laplace-shaped proposal, S = 200) across 500 replications, for
two simulated data sets.}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
& dataset & mean\_log\_ml & sd\_log\_ml & mean\_ess \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
n10 & n = 10 & -19.2284 & 0.1447 & 133.4968 \\
n200 & n = 200 & -285.8823 & 0.0091 & 196.4443 \\

\end{longtable}

}

The Monte Carlo standard deviation across replications is small relative
to the estimate itself, and the effective sample size stays close to the
nominal \(S=200\) in both cases --- evidence that the Laplace-shaped
proposal tracks the posterior well even though it is, after all, only a
second-order approximation to it.

\subsection{Why the proposal matters: Laplace-shaped versus prior
sampling}\label{why-the-proposal-matters-laplace-shaped-versus-prior-sampling}

The prior is a valid proposal for the same target --- every posterior
draw is also a prior draw with positive density --- so
\texttt{log\_marlik\_prior} is a legitimate importance-sampling
estimator too. But the prior is far more diffuse than the posterior,
especially once \(n\) is not tiny, so it is a much poorer match to
\(f = L\pi\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ns }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{400}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ess\_tbl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{do.call}\NormalTok{(rbind, }\FunctionTok{lapply}\NormalTok{(ns, }\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(n) \{}
\NormalTok{    x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(n)}
    \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n =}\NormalTok{ n,}
               \AttributeTok{ess\_laplace\_proposal =} \FunctionTok{attr}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log\_marlik\_imps}\NormalTok{(x, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{), }\StringTok{"ess"}\NormalTok{),}
               \AttributeTok{ess\_prior\_sampling   =} \FunctionTok{attr}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{log\_marlik\_prior}\NormalTok{(x, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{), }\StringTok{"ess"}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{\}))}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(ess\_tbl, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}rrr@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-marlik-ess-vs-n}Effective sample size (of S = 500
draws) for the Laplace-shaped proposal versus prior sampling, as the
sample size n of the simulated data grows. Both target the same
posterior for (mu, w).}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
n & ess\_laplace\_proposal & ess\_prior\_sampling \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
5 & 258.8 & 19.5 \\
20 & 341.4 & 3.3 \\
100 & 476.0 & 1.0 \\
400 & 496.0 & 1.0 \\

\end{longtable}

}

As \(n\) grows the posterior sharpens around the truth while the prior
does not, so prior sampling's effective sample size deteriorates
steadily; the Laplace-shaped proposal, which re-centers and re-scales
itself around the posterior mode for every data set, keeps pace. This is
the marginal-likelihood instance of the same lesson as the missed-mode
example above: a fixed, data-independent proposal becomes an
increasingly bad match to a target that concentrates as more data
arrive, and only a proposal that adapts to the target avoids the
resulting collapse in efficiency.

\section{Summary}\label{summary-5}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Importance sampling reweights draws from a proposal \(g\) by
  \(w(\theta) = f(\theta)/g(\theta)\) instead of accepting or rejecting
  them, using \(\widehat{C_f/C_g} = \frac1S\sum_s w_s\) and the
  self-normalized \(\hat A = \sum_s a(\theta_s)\tilde w_s\); only the
  support condition \(\{g>0\}\supseteq\{f>0\}\) is required, not an
  envelope \(g \ge f\).
\item
  A good proposal shaped like the target on its support gives
  near-constant weights and \(S_{\text{eff}} \approx S\) (the
  tail-probability and Laplace-proposal examples).
\item
  A proposal that misses a mode or part of the support fails
  \textbf{silently}: the observed weights can look perfectly healthy
  while both \(C_f/C_g\) and \(E_f[a]\) are badly wrong, and no amount
  of extra sampling fixes it. \(S_{\text{eff}}\) diagnoses only the
  noisy failure (wildly varying observed weights), never the silent one.
\item
  For the marginal likelihood, a proposal built from a Laplace
  approximation at the posterior mode remains efficient as the data
  accumulate and the posterior sharpens, while a fixed, data-independent
  proposal such as the prior does not --- reinforcing why the
  mode-finding methods from earlier chapters remain useful even once we
  turn to sampling-based methods.
\end{itemize}

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{Markov Chain Monte Carlo}\label{markov-chain-monte-carlo}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(truncnorm)}
\FunctionTok{library}\NormalTok{(rstan)}
\FunctionTok{rstan\_options}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{auto\_write =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The last two chapters drew from a \textbf{fixed} proposal \(g\): an
envelope for rejection sampling, an importance distribution for
reweighting. Both work well when \(g\) can be made to resemble the
target \(f\) over its whole support, but in more than a handful of
dimensions no fixed \(g\) does --- the target occupies an ever-thinner
sliver of the space \(g\) covers, exactly the curse of dimensionality
met already for quadrature grids. \textbf{Markov chain Monte Carlo
(MCMC)} answers this differently: instead of one global proposal, build
a \textbf{random process}
\(\theta^{(0)}, \theta^{(1)}, \theta^{(2)}, \ldots\) that moves
\emph{locally} --- each step depends only on where the chain currently
is --- and is engineered so that, after enough steps, \(\theta^{(t)}\)
is (approximately) distributed as the target \(f\). The price is that
consecutive draws are dependent and the guarantee is only asymptotic;
the payoff is that the same local-move idea scales to any dimension.

This chapter develops the Markov chain theory that makes this work, then
builds three increasingly automatic ways to construct such a chain ---
Gibbs sampling, Metropolis--Hastings, and Hamiltonian Monte Carlo ---
and ends with Stan, which automates the last of these for essentially
arbitrary models.

\section{Markov Chains}\label{markov-chains}

\subsection{Definition and the convergence
question}\label{definition-and-the-convergence-question}

A sequence \(\theta^{(0)}, \theta^{(1)}, \theta^{(2)}, \ldots\) is a
\textbf{Markov chain} if the future depends on the past only through the
present, \[
P\big(\theta^{(i)} \mid \theta^{(0)}, \ldots, \theta^{(i-1)}\big) = T\big(\theta^{(i)} \mid \theta^{(i-1)}\big),
\] where \(T\), the \textbf{transition distribution}, does not depend on
\(i\). A chain is completely specified by a starting value (or
distribution) and \(T\); simulating it just means repeatedly drawing
\(\theta^{(i)} \sim T(\cdot \mid \theta^{(i-1)})\).

The question MCMC answers is: given a target \(\pi\) known only up to a
constant, can we \emph{design} a \(T\) such that, no matter where the
chain starts, \(\theta^{(i)}\) converges in distribution to \(\pi\) as
\(i \to \infty\)? If so, running the chain for a long time and averaging
\(a(\theta^{(i)})\) over the later iterations estimates
\(E_\pi[a(\theta)]\) --- this is what ``MCMC'' means in practice.

\subsection{A worked example: two random walks on a
cycle}\label{a-worked-example-two-random-walks-on-a-cycle}

To make ``designing \(T\)'' concrete before turning to theory, consider
a chain on the ten states \(\{0, 1, \ldots, 9\}\), arranged on a circle.
At each step it moves one position clockwise with probability \(0.45\),
one position counter-clockwise with probability \(0.45\), and stays put
with probability \(0.1\) --- a \emph{cyclic} random walk, since state
\(9\)'s clockwise neighbour wraps around to state \(0\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sim\_cyclic\_walk }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(ini, iters, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    forward  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(i) }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (i }\SpecialCharTok{==}\NormalTok{ n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\DecValTok{0} \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ i }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{    backward }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(i) }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (i }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1} \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{    mc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{integer}\NormalTok{(iters }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{); mc[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ ini}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{(iters }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)) \{}
\NormalTok{        u }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        mc[i] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (u }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{0.45}\NormalTok{) }\FunctionTok{forward}\NormalTok{(mc[i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
                 \ControlFlowTok{else} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (u }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.55}\NormalTok{) }\FunctionTok{backward}\NormalTok{(mc[i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
                 \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ mc[i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    mc}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Now change only the two boundary states: at \(0\), ``backward'' does
nothing (stays at \(0\)) instead of wrapping to \(9\); at \(9\),
``forward'' does nothing instead of wrapping to \(0\). This is a
\emph{reflecting} walk on a line segment rather than a cycle:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sim\_reflecting\_walk }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(ini, iters, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    forward  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(i) }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (i }\SpecialCharTok{==}\NormalTok{ n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1} \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ i }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{    backward }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(i) }\ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (i }\SpecialCharTok{==} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{) }\DecValTok{0} \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{    mc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{integer}\NormalTok{(iters }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{); mc[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ ini}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{(iters }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)) \{}
\NormalTok{        u }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        mc[i] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (u }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \FloatTok{0.4}\NormalTok{) }\FunctionTok{forward}\NormalTok{(mc[i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
                 \ControlFlowTok{else} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (u }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{) }\FunctionTok{backward}\NormalTok{(mc[i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
                 \ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ mc[i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    mc}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{cyc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sim\_cyclic\_walk}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20000}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{refl }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sim\_reflecting\_walk}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{20000}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{barplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(cyc[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)]) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(cyc[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)]), }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{,}
        \AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"state"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"frequency"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"cyclic walk (wraps around)"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{abline}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{h =} \FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lty =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{barplot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{table}\NormalTok{(refl[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)]) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(refl[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{)]), }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{,}
        \AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"state"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"frequency"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"reflecting walk (bounces off ends)"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit11_mcmc_files/figure-pdf/fig-two-walks-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-two-walks}Long-run state frequencies for the cyclic
walk (left, converges to uniform on all ten states) and the reflecting
walk (right, concentrates almost entirely at state 9).}

\end{figure}%

The two chains use almost the same rule and yet settle into completely
different long-run behaviour, and the theory below explains why. In the
cyclic walk every state has exactly the same \emph{local} picture ---
two neighbours, reached with equal probability \(0.45\) in each
direction --- so no state is structurally different from any other, and
the equilibrium distribution treats them all alike: uniform on
\(\{0,\ldots,9\}\). The reflecting walk changes two things at once, and
both matter. First, its forward and backward probabilities are
themselves unequal (\(0.4\) versus \(0.2\), with \(0.4\) probability of
staying put), so even on a cycle this chain would drift persistently in
one direction --- though a \emph{cyclic} chain with that same asymmetry
would still converge to uniform, because every state would still be
structurally identical to every other. Second, and decisively, states
\(0\) and \(9\) are no longer structurally identical to the interior
states: an attempted move off either end is turned into a self-loop
instead of wrapping around, so probability that would have left the
segment is returned to the same state. Combined with the rightward
drift, this reflecting boundary acts as a trap at state \(9\)
specifically --- mass drifts right, and once it arrives it accumulates
there rather than passing through --- which is exactly the extreme,
almost-all-mass-at-\(9\) pattern in the right panel above. This is the
invariance and detailed-balance machinery below made visible: a
transition rule under which every state looks alike to the chain
preserves the uniform distribution, and one that treats some states
differently does not.

\subsection{Invariance, detailed balance, and
convergence}\label{invariance-detailed-balance-and-convergence}

\(\pi\) is an \textbf{invariant} (or stationary) distribution of
transition \(T\) if \[
\int \pi(\theta)\, T(\theta \to \theta') \, d\theta = \pi(\theta') \qquad \text{for all } \theta'.
\] In words: if \(\theta \sim \pi\) and one step of the chain is
applied, the result is again distributed as \(\pi\) --- \(\pi\) is a
fixed point of \(T\). Invariance alone does not guarantee the chain
\emph{converges} to \(\pi\) from an arbitrary start (a chain that never
moves leaves every distribution invariant), so two more conditions are
needed: \textbf{aperiodicity} (the chain does not cycle
deterministically through a fixed sequence of states) and
\textbf{irreducibility} (every state can eventually be reached from
every other). Under all three, \(\theta^{(i)} \to \pi\) in distribution
from any starting point, and ergodic averages converge,
\(\frac1n\sum_i a(\theta^{(i)}) \to E_\pi[a(\theta)]\).

Invariance is a \emph{global} condition (it involves integrating over
all \(\theta\)) and is awkward to check directly for a proposed \(T\).
The standard shortcut is a \emph{local}, pairwise condition. \(T\)
satisfies \textbf{detailed balance} with respect to \(\pi\) if \[
\pi(\theta)\, T(\theta \to \theta') = \pi(\theta')\, T(\theta' \to \theta) \qquad \text{for all } \theta, \theta',
\] i.e.~the flow of probability from \(\theta\) to \(\theta'\) under
\(\pi\) exactly balances the reverse flow (the transition \(T\) itself
need not be symmetric --- only the \(\pi\)-weighted flow is). Detailed
balance implies invariance: integrating both sides over \(\theta\), \[
\int \pi(\theta)\, T(\theta \to \theta') \, d\theta = \int \pi(\theta')\, T(\theta' \to \theta) \, d\theta
= \pi(\theta') \underbrace{\int T(\theta' \to \theta)\, d\theta}_{=\,1} = \pi(\theta').
\] Every construction in this chapter --- Gibbs, Metropolis--Hastings,
Hamiltonian Monte Carlo --- is a recipe for building a \(T\) that
satisfies detailed balance (or, for Gibbs, invariance directly) with
respect to a \emph{given} target \(\pi\), so that simulating the chain
long enough and discarding an initial \textbf{burn-in} period produces
(dependent) draws from \(\pi\).

\section{Gibbs Sampling}\label{gibbs-sampling}

\subsection{The idea and why it works}\label{the-idea-and-why-it-works}

Suppose \(\theta = (\theta_1, \theta_2)\) and both full conditionals
\(\pi(\theta_1 \mid \theta_2)\) and \(\pi(\theta_2 \mid \theta_1)\) can
be sampled directly, even though the joint \(\pi(\theta_1, \theta_2)\)
has no closed form or cannot be sampled directly. The \textbf{Gibbs
sampler} alternates \[
\theta_1^{(t)} \sim \pi\big(\theta_1 \mid \theta_2^{(t-1)}\big), \qquad
\theta_2^{(t)} \sim \pi\big(\theta_2 \mid \theta_1^{(t)}\big).
\] Each half-step leaves \(\pi\) invariant: if
\((\theta_1, \theta_2) \sim \pi\), then \(\theta_2 \sim \pi(\theta_2)\)
(its marginal), and replacing \(\theta_1\) by a fresh draw from
\(\pi(\theta_1 \mid \theta_2)\) produces a pair with joint density
\(\pi(\theta_2)\,\pi(\theta_1 \mid \theta_2) = \pi(\theta_1, \theta_2)\)
--- exactly \(\pi\) again. Cycling through both coordinates therefore
also leaves \(\pi\) invariant, and as long as the full conditionals are
everywhere positive the chain is irreducible and aperiodic. No proposal,
no acceptance step, no tuning: Gibbs sampling only needs full
conditionals that can be sampled.

\subsection{Deriving the full conditionals for normal
data}\label{deriving-the-full-conditionals-for-normal-data}

Take the running example for this half of the book:
\(x_1, \ldots, x_n \mid \mu, \sigma^2 \overset{iid}{\sim} N(\mu, \sigma^2)\),
with independent priors \(\mu \sim N(\mu_0, \sigma_0^2)\) and
\(\sigma^2 \sim \mathrm{IG}(a_0, \lambda_0)\) (inverse-gamma:
\(1/\sigma^2 \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(a_0, \lambda_0)\), so \(a_0\) is a
shape and \(\lambda_0\) a rate/scale). The joint posterior is \[
P(\mu, \sigma^2 \mid x) \ \propto\ e^{-(\mu-\mu_0)^2 / (2\sigma_0^2)} \cdot (\sigma^2)^{-(a_0+1)} e^{-\lambda_0/\sigma^2} \cdot (\sigma^2)^{-n/2} \exp\Big\{-\sum_{i=1}^n \tfrac{(x_i-\mu)^2}{2\sigma^2}\Big\},
\] two-dimensional with no closed-form normalizing constant, but each
conditional is standard.

\textbf{Conditional of \(\mu\).} Collect the factors that involve
\(\mu\) and expand
\(\sum_i(x_i-\mu)^2 = n\mu^2 - 2n\mu\bar x + \sum_i x_i^2\): \[
P(\mu \mid \sigma^2, x) \ \propto\ \exp\Big\{-\tfrac12\Big[\big(\tfrac{1}{\sigma_0^2} + \tfrac{n}{\sigma^2}\big)\mu^2 - 2\big(\tfrac{\mu_0}{\sigma_0^2} + \tfrac{n\bar x}{\sigma^2}\big)\mu\Big]\Big\},
\] which is the kernel of \(N(m, v)\) with
\(1/v = 1/\sigma_0^2 + n/\sigma^2\) and
\(m/v = \mu_0/\sigma_0^2 + n\bar x/\sigma^2\) --- a precision-weighted
average of the prior mean and the sample mean, with \(n/\sigma^2\) the
weight the data contribute. As \(n \to \infty\), \(m \to \bar x\) and
\(v \approx \sigma^2/n\): the prior is overwhelmed.

\textbf{Conditional of \(\sigma^2\).} Collect the factors involving
\(\sigma^2\): \[
P(\sigma^2 \mid \mu, x) \ \propto\ (\sigma^2)^{-(a_0+1)} e^{-\lambda_0/\sigma^2} \cdot (\sigma^2)^{-n/2} e^{-\sum_i(x_i-\mu)^2/(2\sigma^2)}
= (\sigma^2)^{-\left(\frac n2 + a_0 + 1\right)} \exp\Big\{-\tfrac{\lambda_0 + \frac12\sum_i(x_i-\mu)^2}{\sigma^2}\Big\},
\] again an inverse-gamma kernel,
\(\sigma^2 \mid \mu, x \sim \mathrm{IG}\big(\tfrac n2 + a_0,\ \tfrac12\sum_i(x_i-\mu)^2 + \lambda_0\big)\):
the prior contributes \(a_0\) ``pseudo-observations'' worth of shape and
\(\lambda_0\) worth of sum-of-squares, and the data add \(n\) and
\(\sum_i(x_i-\mu)^2\) to each.

\subsection{Implementation}\label{implementation-3}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{gibbs\_norm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(iters, x, mu0, sigma0, alpha, w, }\AttributeTok{mu\_init =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{s2\_init =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    n }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(x); sumx }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(x)}
\NormalTok{    out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NA\_real\_}\NormalTok{, iters, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{dimnames =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NULL}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"mu"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"s2"}\NormalTok{)))}
\NormalTok{    mu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ mu\_init; s2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ s2\_init}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (t }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(iters)) \{}
\NormalTok{        v  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (n }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ s2 }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ sigma0)}
\NormalTok{        mu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, (sumx }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ s2 }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ mu0 }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ sigma0) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ v, }\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(v))}
\NormalTok{        s2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{rgamma}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, (alpha }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ n) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, (alpha }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ w }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((x }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mu)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        out[t, ] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(mu, s2)}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    out}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

A preallocated matrix filled by a plain \texttt{for} loop replaces the
original \texttt{replicate()}-with-\texttt{\textless{}\textless{}-}
pattern: it does the same work with one direct assignment per iteration
instead of a closure mutating variables in its enclosing scope, and it
makes the recurrence --- each row built from the previous one ---
visually explicit.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{draws\_gibbs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{gibbs\_norm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{10000}\NormalTok{, x, }\AttributeTok{mu0 =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sigma0 =} \FloatTok{1e10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{alpha =} \FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{w =} \FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{burn }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{200}
\NormalTok{mu\_g  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ draws\_gibbs[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{burn, }\StringTok{"mu"}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{sd\_g  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(draws\_gibbs[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{burn, }\StringTok{"s2"}\NormalTok{])}

\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(mu\_g, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"iteration"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(mu), }\AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"trace of mu"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{density}\NormalTok{(mu\_g), }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"posterior density of mu"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(mu))}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(sd\_g, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"iteration"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(sigma), }\AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"trace of sigma"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{density}\NormalTok{(sd\_g), }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"posterior density of sigma"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(sigma))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{Data augmentation: sampling with censored
data}\label{data-augmentation-sampling-with-censored-data}

Gibbs sampling extends immediately to models with \textbf{latent
variables}, by treating the latent quantities as extra parameters to
sample. Suppose the \(x_i\) are not observed exactly but only rounded
down to the nearest multiple of some interval length \(l\) (ages
recorded in whole years, measurements binned to the nearest instrument
tick): we observe \(y_i\) with \(x_i \in [y_i, y_i + l)\). \textbf{Data
augmentation} adds \(x_1,\ldots,x_n\) as unknowns and Gibbs-samples all
three blocks: \[
x_i^{(t)} \sim P\big(x_i \mid \mu^{(t-1)}, \sigma^{2(t-1)}, y_i\big), \qquad
\mu^{(t)} \sim P\big(\mu \mid x^{(t)}, \sigma^{2(t-1)}\big), \qquad
\sigma^{2(t)} \sim P\big(\sigma^2 \mid x^{(t)}, \mu^{(t)}\big).
\] The last two steps are exactly the conditionals derived above,
applied to the imputed \(x\). The first is a normal density restricted
to the interval \([y_i, y_i+l)\) and renormalized --- a
\textbf{truncated normal}, sampled directly with
\texttt{truncnorm::rtruncnorm} rather than by rejection:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{gibbs\_norm\_interval }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(iters, y, mu0, sigma0, alpha, w, }\AttributeTok{l =} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    n }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(y)}
\NormalTok{    mu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{; s2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{; x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rep}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, n)}
\NormalTok{    out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NA\_real\_}\NormalTok{, iters, }\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ n)}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (t }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(iters)) \{}
\NormalTok{        x }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rtruncnorm}\NormalTok{(n, }\AttributeTok{a =}\NormalTok{ y, }\AttributeTok{b =}\NormalTok{ y }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ l, }\AttributeTok{mean =}\NormalTok{ mu, }\AttributeTok{sd =} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(s2))}
\NormalTok{        sumx }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(x)}
\NormalTok{        v  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (n }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ s2 }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ sigma0)}
\NormalTok{        mu }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, (sumx }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ s2 }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ mu0 }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ sigma0) }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ v, }\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(v))}
\NormalTok{        s2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1} \SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{rgamma}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, (alpha }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ n) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, (alpha }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ w }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{((x }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mu)}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        out[t, ] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(mu, s2, x)}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mu =}\NormalTok{ out[, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{sd =} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(out[, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]), }\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ out[, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)])}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{x\_true }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{y\_obs  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{floor}\NormalTok{(x\_true }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \DecValTok{10}
\NormalTok{fit\_cens }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{gibbs\_norm\_interval}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{5000}\NormalTok{, y\_obs, }\AttributeTok{mu0 =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sigma0 =} \FloatTok{1e10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{alpha =} \FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{w =} \FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{l =} \DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{burn2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{200}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{quantity =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"true mu (unknown to the model)"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"true sigma"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"posterior mean of mu"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"posterior mean of sigma"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{value =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(fit\_cens}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{mu[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{burn2]), }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(fit\_cens}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sd[}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{burn2]))}
\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col.names =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"quantity"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"value"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{align =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"r"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lr@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-gibbs-censored}Recovering mu and sigma from data
rounded down to the nearest 10, n = 200.}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
quantity & value \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
true mu (unknown to the model) & 100.00 \\
true sigma & 40.00 \\
posterior mean of mu & 100.63 \\
posterior mean of sigma & 38.71 \\

}

The rounding destroys a great deal of information (each \(y_i\) only
says which decade \(x_i\) fell in), yet the augmented Gibbs sampler
still recovers \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\) close to their true values,
because it correctly propagates the \emph{uncertainty} in each \(x_i\)
rather than, say, treating \(y_i\) as if it were exact.

\section{Metropolis--Hastings}\label{metropolishastings}

\subsection{The algorithm}\label{the-algorithm-2}

Gibbs sampling needs full conditionals that are both known and directly
sample-able --- a strong requirement outside conjugate models.
\textbf{Metropolis--Hastings (MH)} needs only a target \(\pi(\theta)\)
computable up to a constant and \emph{any} proposal distribution
\(\tilde T(\theta^* \mid \theta)\) we can sample from. Starting from
\(\theta^{(0)}\), repeat:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Draw a proposal \(\theta^* \sim \tilde T(\cdot \mid \theta^{(t-1)})\).
\item
  Compute the acceptance ratio
  \(r = \min\Big\{\dfrac{\pi(\theta^*)\,\tilde T(\theta^{(t-1)} \mid \theta^*)}{\pi(\theta^{(t-1)})\,\tilde T(\theta^* \mid \theta^{(t-1)})},\ 1\Big\}\).
\item
  With probability \(r\) set \(\theta^{(t)} = \theta^*\) (accept);
  otherwise set \(\theta^{(t)} = \theta^{(t-1)}\) (reject \textbf{and
  repeat the current value}).
\end{enumerate}

Only the ratio \(\pi(\theta^*)/\pi(\theta^{(t-1)})\) is needed, so any
normalizing constant of \(\pi\) cancels --- exactly as in rejection
sampling, but here a rejected proposal still contributes a draw (the
repeated current value) rather than being discarded, which is what lets
MH work even when \(\pi\) has no available envelope. One can show (by
checking detailed balance directly, splitting into the ``propose and
accept'' and ``reject and stay'' cases and using the
\(\min\{\cdot,\cdot\}\) identity \(\min\{a,b\} = \min\{b,a\}\)) that
this \(T\) leaves \(\pi\) invariant regardless of what \(\tilde T\) is,
as long as its support is compatible with \(\pi\)'s.

\subsection{Random-walk Metropolis}\label{random-walk-metropolis}

If \(\tilde T\) is symmetric,
\(\tilde T(\theta^* \mid \theta) = \tilde T(\theta \mid \theta^*)\) ---
e.g.~\(\theta^* \sim N(\theta^{(t-1)}, \varsigma^2)\) --- the proposal
terms cancel and \(r = \min\{\pi(\theta^*)/\pi(\theta^{(t-1)}), 1\}\):
uphill proposals are always accepted, downhill ones accepted in
proportion to how much worse they are. \(\varsigma\), the \textbf{step
size}, is the only tuning parameter.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{met\_gauss }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(iters, log\_f, stepsizes, ini\_value, ...) \{}
\NormalTok{    state }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ ini\_value; no\_var }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(state)}
\NormalTok{    logf }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log\_f}\NormalTok{(ini\_value, ...)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\SpecialCharTok{!}\FunctionTok{is.finite}\NormalTok{(logf)) }\FunctionTok{stop}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Initial value has zero probability"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NA\_real\_}\NormalTok{, iters, no\_var); n\_acc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{0}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (t }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(iters)) \{}
\NormalTok{        proposal }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(no\_var, state, stepsizes)}
\NormalTok{        new\_logf }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log\_f}\NormalTok{(proposal, ...)}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ new\_logf }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ logf) \{}
\NormalTok{            state }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ proposal; logf }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ new\_logf; n\_acc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ n\_acc }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{        out[t, ] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ state}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \FunctionTok{attr}\NormalTok{(out, }\StringTok{"accept\_rate"}\NormalTok{) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ n\_acc }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ iters}
\NormalTok{    out}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

An indicator-shaped target needs no special handling: sampling a normal
truncated to \([l, u]\) by targeting
\(\pi(\theta) \propto \varphi(\theta)\,I(l \le \theta \le u)\), any
proposal landing outside \([l,u]\) has \(\pi(\theta^*) = 0\), hence
\(r = 0\), and is automatically rejected.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{log\_tnorm }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(x, mu, sigma, lb, ub) \{}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (x }\SpecialCharTok{\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ lb }\SpecialCharTok{\&\&}\NormalTok{ x }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ ub) }\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x, mu, sigma, }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{) }\ControlFlowTok{else} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\ConstantTok{Inf}
\NormalTok{\}}
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (step }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)) \{}
\NormalTok{    mc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{met\_gauss}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{5000}\NormalTok{, log\_tnorm, }\AttributeTok{stepsizes =}\NormalTok{ step, }\AttributeTok{ini\_value =} \FloatTok{4.5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{mu =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sigma =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lb =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ub =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{)}
    \FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(mc, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"iteration"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta),}
         \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"step = \%.1f, accept = \%.2f"}\NormalTok{, step, }\FunctionTok{attr}\NormalTok{(mc, }\StringTok{"accept\_rate"}\NormalTok{)))}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\centering{

}

\caption{\label{fig-tnorm-mh}Random-walk MH targeting N(0, 2\^{}2)
truncated to {[}4, 5{]}, for three step sizes.}

\subsection{Choosing the step size}\label{choosing-the-step-size}

The chain's draws are dependent, and what determines the precision of an
ergodic average is not the acceptance rate itself but the resulting
\textbf{autocorrelation}: for a stationary chain with lag-\(k\)
autocorrelation \(\rho_k\), \[
\mathrm{Var}\Big(\tfrac1T\sum_{t=1}^T \theta^{(t)}\Big) \approx \frac{\sigma^2}{T}\Big(1 + 2\sum_{k \ge 1} \rho_k\Big) = \frac{\sigma^2}{T_\text{eff}},
\] so the tuning goal is small autocorrelation, not a high acceptance
rate as such. A step too small is accepted almost every time but crawls
(highly autocorrelated); a step too large is almost always rejected, so
the chain barely moves either. Both extremes waste the same \(T\)
iterations on far fewer effective ones --- the figure below (three step
sizes on a \(N(0,1)\) target) makes this visible directly in the trace
and the ACF.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mh\_std\_normal }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(sig, }\AttributeTok{n =} \DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    th }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(n); th[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{; acc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{0}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (t }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{n) \{}
\NormalTok{        p }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ th[t }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, sig)}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(p) }\SpecialCharTok{/} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(th[t }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])) \{ th[t] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ p; acc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ acc }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ \} }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ th[t] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ th[t }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{th =}\NormalTok{ th, }\AttributeTok{acc =}\NormalTok{ acc }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (n }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{res }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{lapply}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{40}\NormalTok{), mh\_std\_normal)}
\NormalTok{labs }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"too small"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"about right"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"too large"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (k }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(res[[k]]}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{th[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"t"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta}\SpecialCharTok{\^{}}\NormalTok{(t)),}
                     \AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{3.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{3.5}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"\%s: accept \%.2f"}\NormalTok{, labs[k], res[[k]]}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{acc))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (k }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{) }\FunctionTok{acf}\NormalTok{(res[[k]]}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{th, }\AttributeTok{lag.max =} \DecValTok{50}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"ACF"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

}

\caption{\label{fig-stepsize}Step size too small, about right, and too
large: trace (top) and autocorrelation (bottom) for a random walk
targeting N(0, 1).}

For random-walk Metropolis on a smooth target, theory recommends an
acceptance rate near \(0.234\) in high dimension (up to about \(0.44\)
in one dimension) as the rate that minimizes autocorrelation for a given
computational budget --- a useful rule of thumb, not a target to hit
exactly.

\subsection{Example: the same normal-data model, by
Metropolis--Hastings}\label{example-the-same-normal-data-model-by-metropolishastings}

\(\sigma^2 > 0\) makes a symmetric normal random walk directly on
\(\sigma^2\) wasteful (many proposals land below zero), so reparametrize
\(w = \log \sigma^2\), which ranges over all of \(\mathbb R\). By the
change-of-variables formula, the posterior in \((\mu, w)\) picks up a
Jacobian factor \(e^w\) relative to the posterior in
\((\mu, \sigma^2)\); omitting it targets the wrong distribution.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{log\_post\_mu\_w }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(mu, w, x, mu0, sigma0, alpha, wprior) \{}
\NormalTok{    s2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(w)}
    \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(x, mu, }\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(s2), }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FunctionTok{dnorm}\NormalTok{(mu, mu0, }\FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(sigma0), }\AttributeTok{log =} \ConstantTok{TRUE}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+}
\NormalTok{        (}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(alpha }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(s2) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ alpha }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ wprior }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ s2)) }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ w   }\CommentTok{\# "+ w" is the log Jacobian}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{    log\_f }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(state) }\FunctionTok{log\_post\_mu\_w}\NormalTok{(state[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], state[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{], x, mu0, sigma0, alpha, w)}

    \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mu =}\NormalTok{ mc[, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{sd =} \FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{0.5} \SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ mc[, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]), }\AttributeTok{accept\_rate =} \FunctionTok{attr}\NormalTok{(mc, }\StringTok{"accept\_rate"}\NormalTok{))}

\subsection{Metropolis-within-Gibbs}\label{metropolis-within-gibbs}

The two ideas combine directly. If \(\theta = (\theta_1, \theta_2)\) and
\(\pi(\theta_1 \mid \theta_2)\) has no convenient sampler while
\(\pi(\theta_2 \mid \theta_1)\) does, replace only the first Gibbs step
by a Metropolis--Hastings update that targets
\(\pi(\theta_1 \mid \theta_2)\) (evaluated at the \emph{current}
\(\theta_2\)) and leave the second step as an exact conditional draw.
Each block transition leaves \(\pi\) invariant on its own --- the Gibbs
argument applies verbatim to any
\(\pi(\theta_j \mid \theta_{-j})\)-invariant transition, not only an
exact conditional draw --- so the composite sweep leaves the full joint
\(\pi\) invariant. This ``Gibbs where conjugate, Metropolis elsewhere''
pattern, iterated one parameter or block at a time, is how most
general-purpose Bayesian software before Hamiltonian methods actually
worked.

\section{Hamiltonian Monte Carlo}\label{hamiltonian-monte-carlo}

\subsection{Why random walks struggle}\label{why-random-walks-struggle}

A random-walk proposal has no sense of direction: reaching a point one
posterior standard deviation away takes \(O(d)\) steps in \(d\)
dimensions, and far more along a narrow, correlated ridge, since most
proposed directions immediately step off the ridge and get rejected.
Gibbs suffers the same way when parameters are strongly correlated,
since each step moves along only one coordinate axis.
\textbf{Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC)} uses the \emph{gradient}
\(\nabla \log \pi(\theta)\) to propose points far away with high
probability of acceptance.

Introduce an auxiliary \textbf{momentum} variable \(p \in \mathbb R^d\)
and the joint density \(\pi(\theta, p) \propto \exp\{-H(\theta,p)\}\)
with \[
H(\theta, p) = \underbrace{-\log \pi(\theta)}_{\text{potential } U(\theta)} + \underbrace{\tfrac12 p^\top M^{-1} p}_{\text{kinetic } K(p)},
\] so that the \(\theta\)-marginal is exactly the target and
\(p \sim N_d(0, M)\) independently. Physically: a particle at position
\(\theta\) with momentum \(p\) rolling frictionlessly on the potential
surface \(U(\theta)\). Hamilton's equations move \((\theta,p)\) so as to
\emph{conserve} \(H\) exactly and are volume-preserving and reversible;
discretizing them with the \textbf{leapfrog} integrator (step size
\(\epsilon\), \(L\) steps), \[
p \leftarrow p + \tfrac\epsilon2 \nabla\log\pi(\theta), \qquad \theta \leftarrow \theta + \epsilon M^{-1}p, \qquad p \leftarrow p + \tfrac\epsilon2\nabla\log\pi(\theta),
\] keeps volume-preservation and reversibility exactly (negating \(p\)
and re-running gives back the start) while introducing only an
\(O(\epsilon^2)\) error in \(H\), which a Metropolis accept/reject step
then corrects exactly.

\subsection{The algorithm}\label{the-algorithm-3}

Starting from \(\theta^{(0)}\), repeat: draw a fresh
\(p \sim N_d(0,M)\); run \(L\) leapfrog steps from
\((\theta^{(t-1)}, p)\) to reach \((\theta^*, p^*)\); accept
\(\theta^{(t)} = \theta^*\) with probability
\(\min\{\exp[H(\theta^{(t-1)},p) - H(\theta^*,p^*)], 1\}\), else repeat
\(\theta^{(t-1)}\). Drawing \(p\) is a Gibbs step from its own
conditional (it doesn't depend on \(\theta\)), and the
leapfrog-plus-accept step is a Metropolis update with a deterministic,
reversible proposal --- so each half leaves \(\pi(\theta,p)\) invariant,
and because \(H\) is nearly conserved, acceptance stays high (typically
\(0.65\)--\(0.8\)) even though \(\theta^*\) may be far from
\(\theta^{(t-1)}\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{hmc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(log\_pi, grad\_log\_pi, theta, eps, L, iters, }\AttributeTok{M =} \FunctionTok{diag}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(theta))) \{}
\NormalTok{    d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(theta); Minv }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{solve}\NormalTok{(M); R }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{chol}\NormalTok{(M)}
\NormalTok{    out }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NA\_real\_}\NormalTok{, iters, d); n\_acc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{0}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (t }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(iters)) \{}
\NormalTok{        p0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{as.vector}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{t}\NormalTok{(R) }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(d))}
\NormalTok{        th }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ theta; p }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ p0 }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{grad\_log\_pi}\NormalTok{(th)}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (l }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{seq\_len}\NormalTok{(L)) \{}
\NormalTok{            th }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ th }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{as.vector}\NormalTok{(Minv }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%}\NormalTok{ p)}
            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (l }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ L) p }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ p }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps }\SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{grad\_log\_pi}\NormalTok{(th)}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{        p }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ p }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{grad\_log\_pi}\NormalTok{(th)}
\NormalTok{        H0 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{log\_pi}\NormalTok{(theta) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FloatTok{0.5} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(p0 }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (Minv }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%}\NormalTok{ p0))}
\NormalTok{        H1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FunctionTok{log\_pi}\NormalTok{(th)    }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FloatTok{0.5} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(p  }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (Minv }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%}\NormalTok{ p))}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ H0 }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ H1) \{ theta }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ th; n\_acc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ n\_acc }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ \}}
\NormalTok{        out[t, ] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ theta}
\NormalTok{    \}}
    \FunctionTok{attr}\NormalTok{(out, }\StringTok{"accept\_rate"}\NormalTok{) }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ n\_acc }\SpecialCharTok{/}\NormalTok{ iters}
\NormalTok{    out}
\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{HMC versus random-walk Metropolis on a correlated
target}\label{hmc-versus-random-walk-metropolis-on-a-correlated-target}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{Sigma }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.98}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.98}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{); Sinv }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{solve}\NormalTok{(Sigma)}
\NormalTok{log\_pi\_corr  }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(z) }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.5} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{sum}\NormalTok{(z }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (Sinv }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%}\NormalTok{ z))}
