\NormalTok{rw }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{matrix}\NormalTok{(}\ConstantTok{NA\_real\_}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{); cur }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{); lp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log\_pi\_corr}\NormalTok{(cur); acc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{0}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (t }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{) \{}
\NormalTok{    pr }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ cur }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FloatTok{0.3} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{); lpp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{log\_pi\_corr}\NormalTok{(pr)}
    \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (}\FunctionTok{log}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{runif}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)) }\SpecialCharTok{\textless{}}\NormalTok{ lpp }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ lp) \{ cur }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ pr; lp }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ lpp; acc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ acc }\SpecialCharTok{+} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{ \}}
\NormalTok{    rw[t, ] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}}\NormalTok{ cur}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{draws\_hmc }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{hmc}\NormalTok{(log\_pi\_corr, grad\_pi\_corr, }\AttributeTok{theta =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{eps =} \FloatTok{0.05}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{L =} \DecValTok{30}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{iters =} \DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{g }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{seq}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{3.5}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{3.5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{length.out =} \DecValTok{80}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{Z }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{outer}\NormalTok{(g, g, }\FunctionTok{Vectorize}\NormalTok{(}\ControlFlowTok{function}\NormalTok{(a, b) }\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.5} \SpecialCharTok{*} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(a, b) }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%}\NormalTok{ Sinv }\SpecialCharTok{\%*\%} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(a, b))))}
\FunctionTok{par}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{mfrow =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{mar =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (nm }\ControlFlowTok{in} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"random walk"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"HMC"}\NormalTok{)) \{}
\NormalTok{    d }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (nm }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"random walk"}\NormalTok{) rw }\ControlFlowTok{else}\NormalTok{ draws\_hmc}
\NormalTok{    ar }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (nm }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"random walk"}\NormalTok{) acc }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2000} \ControlFlowTok{else} \FunctionTok{attr}\NormalTok{(draws\_hmc, }\StringTok{"accept\_rate"}\NormalTok{)}
    \FunctionTok{contour}\NormalTok{(g, g, Z, }\AttributeTok{nlevels =} \DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{drawlabels =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"gray60"}\NormalTok{,}
            \AttributeTok{xlab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]), }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \FunctionTok{expression}\NormalTok{(theta[}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]),}
            \AttributeTok{main =} \FunctionTok{sprintf}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"\%s: accept \%.2f"}\NormalTok{, nm, ar))}
    \FunctionTok{lines}\NormalTok{(d[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, ], }\AttributeTok{col =} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (nm }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"random walk"}\NormalTok{) }\StringTok{"firebrick"} \ControlFlowTok{else} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{)}
    \FunctionTok{points}\NormalTok{(d[}\DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, ], }\AttributeTok{pch =} \DecValTok{19}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{cex =} \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ (nm }\SpecialCharTok{==} \StringTok{"random walk"}\NormalTok{) }\StringTok{"firebrick"} \ControlFlowTok{else} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{a1 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{acf}\NormalTok{(rw[, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{lag.max =} \DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{plot =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{acf}
\NormalTok{a2 }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{acf}\NormalTok{(draws\_hmc[, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{], }\AttributeTok{lag.max =} \DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{plot =} \ConstantTok{FALSE}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{acf}
\FunctionTok{plot}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{0}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{, a1, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"h"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{xlab =} \StringTok{"lag"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylab =} \StringTok{"ACF"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{ylim =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{main =} \StringTok{"autocorrelation"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{lines}\NormalTok{((}\DecValTok{0}\SpecialCharTok{:}\DecValTok{60}\NormalTok{) }\SpecialCharTok{+} \FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{, a2, }\AttributeTok{type =} \StringTok{"h"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{)}
\FunctionTok{legend}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"topright"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{bty =} \StringTok{"n"}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{lwd =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{col =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"firebrick"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"steelblue"}\NormalTok{), }\AttributeTok{legend =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"random walk"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"HMC"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{chapters/unit11_mcmc_files/figure-pdf/fig-hmc-vs-rw-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-hmc-vs-rw}Target: bivariate normal with correlation
0.98. Left/middle: first 100 draws over the posterior contours for
random-walk Metropolis and HMC, same iteration budget. Right:
autocorrelation of the first coordinate -- HMC crosses the ridge in one
trajectory and its draws decorrelate almost immediately.}

\end{figure}%

\subsection{Explore it interactively}\label{explore-it-interactively-3}

Leapfrog step size \(\epsilon\) and step count \(L\) both need tuning:
too large an \(\epsilon\) makes the discretized trajectory unstable
(rejections rise sharply); too small an \(L\) reduces HMC to a random
walk; too large an \(L\) lets the trajectory turn back on itself and
waste computation. The app below runs the \texttt{hmc()} function above
on the same correlated target and lets you vary \(\epsilon\) and \(L\)
directly.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{\#| label: hmc{-}tuning{-}app}
\NormalTok{\#| standalone: true}
\NormalTok{\#| viewerHeight: 620}

\NormalTok{library(shiny)}

\NormalTok{Sigma \textless{}{-} matrix(c(1, 0.98, 0.98, 1), 2)}
\NormalTok{Sinv  \textless{}{-} solve(Sigma)}
\NormalTok{log\_pi\_corr  \textless{}{-} function(z) {-}0.5 * sum(z * (Sinv \%*\% z))}
\NormalTok{grad\_pi\_corr \textless{}{-} function(z) {-}as.vector(Sinv \%*\% z)}

\NormalTok{hmc \textless{}{-} function(log\_pi, grad\_log\_pi, theta, eps, L, iters) \{}
\NormalTok{    d \textless{}{-} length(theta)}
\NormalTok{    out \textless{}{-} matrix(NA\_real\_, iters, d); n\_acc \textless{}{-} 0}
\NormalTok{    for (t in seq\_len(iters)) \{}
\NormalTok{        p0 \textless{}{-} rnorm(d)}
\NormalTok{        th \textless{}{-} theta; p \textless{}{-} p0 + eps / 2 * grad\_log\_pi(th)}
\NormalTok{        for (l in seq\_len(L)) \{}
\NormalTok{            th \textless{}{-} th + eps * p}
\NormalTok{            if (l \textless{} L) p \textless{}{-} p + eps * grad\_log\_pi(th)}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{        p \textless{}{-} p + eps / 2 * grad\_log\_pi(th)}
\NormalTok{        H0 \textless{}{-} {-}log\_pi(theta) + 0.5 * sum(p0\^{}2)}
\NormalTok{        H1 \textless{}{-} {-}log\_pi(th)    + 0.5 * sum(p\^{}2)}
\NormalTok{        if (log(runif(1)) \textless{} H0 {-} H1) \{ theta \textless{}{-} th; n\_acc \textless{}{-} n\_acc + 1 \}}
\NormalTok{        out[t, ] \textless{}{-} theta}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\NormalTok{    attr(out, "accept\_rate") \textless{}{-} n\_acc / iters}
\NormalTok{    out}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{ui \textless{}{-} fluidPage(}
\NormalTok{    titlePanel(NULL),}
\NormalTok{    sidebarLayout(}
\NormalTok{        sidebarPanel(}
\NormalTok{            width = 4,}
\NormalTok{            sliderInput("eps", "leapfrog step size (epsilon)", min = 0.01, max = 1, value = 0.05, step = 0.01),}
\NormalTok{            sliderInput("L", "number of leapfrog steps (L)", min = 1, max = 60, value = 30, step = 1),}
\NormalTok{            sliderInput("iters", "iterations", min = 200, max = 3000, value = 1000, step = 200),}
\NormalTok{            helpText("Target: bivariate normal with correlation 0.98. Try eps = 0.05, ",}
\NormalTok{                     "L = 30 (efficient), then eps = 0.5 (unstable, low acceptance), ",}
\NormalTok{                     "then L = 2 (random{-}walk{-}like, high autocorrelation)."),}
\NormalTok{            tableOutput("tab")}
\NormalTok{        ),}
\NormalTok{        mainPanel(}
\NormalTok{            width = 8,}
\NormalTok{            plotOutput("traj", height = "360px"),}
\NormalTok{            plotOutput("acfplot", height = "220px")}
\NormalTok{        )}
\NormalTok{    )}
\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{server \textless{}{-} function(input, output, session) \{}

\NormalTok{    fit \textless{}{-} reactive(\{}
\NormalTok{        set.seed(1)}
\NormalTok{        hmc(log\_pi\_corr, grad\_pi\_corr, theta = c({-}2, {-}2), eps = input$eps, L = input$L, iters = input$iters)}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$traj \textless{}{-} renderPlot(\{}
\NormalTok{        d \textless{}{-} fit()}
\NormalTok{        g \textless{}{-} seq({-}3.5, 3.5, length.out = 60)}
\NormalTok{        Z \textless{}{-} outer(g, g, Vectorize(function(a, b) exp({-}0.5 * c(a, b) \%*\% Sinv \%*\% c(a, b))))}
\NormalTok{        par(mar = c(4, 4, 2, 1))}
\NormalTok{        contour(g, g, Z, nlevels = 6, drawlabels = FALSE, col = "gray60",}
\NormalTok{                xlab = expression(theta[1]), ylab = expression(theta[2]),}
\NormalTok{                main = sprintf("accept rate: \%.2f", attr(d, "accept\_rate")))}
\NormalTok{        k \textless{}{-} min(100, nrow(d))}
\NormalTok{        lines(d[1:k, ], col = "steelblue", lwd = 1.2)}
\NormalTok{        points(d[1:k, ], pch = 19, cex = 0.5, col = "steelblue")}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$acfplot \textless{}{-} renderPlot(\{}
\NormalTok{        d \textless{}{-} fit()}
\NormalTok{        par(mar = c(4, 4, 1, 1))}
\NormalTok{        acf(d[, 1], lag.max = 60, col = "steelblue", main = "autocorrelation of theta\_1")}
\NormalTok{    \})}

\NormalTok{    output$tab \textless{}{-} renderTable(\{}
\NormalTok{        d \textless{}{-} fit()}
\NormalTok{        data.frame(quantity = c("acceptance rate", "mean theta\_1", "sd theta\_1"),}
\NormalTok{                   value = c(attr(d, "accept\_rate"), mean(d[, 1]), sd(d[, 1])))}
\NormalTok{    \}, digits = 3, rownames = FALSE)}
\NormalTok{\}}

\NormalTok{shinyApp(ui, server)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Reproducing this figure}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

The chunk above uses the
\href{https://github.com/quarto-ext/shinylive}{shinylive} extension,
which compiles the app to WebAssembly so it runs in the reader's browser
with no Shiny server. Install it once per project with

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ExtensionTok{quarto}\NormalTok{ add quarto{-}ext/shinylive}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

and add \texttt{filters:\ {[}shinylive{]}} to the document or project
YAML. Only packages available in webR may be used inside the app, so the
code above is restricted to base R and \texttt{shiny}.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Stan and Probabilistic
Programming}\label{stan-and-probabilistic-programming}

\subsection{Automating HMC}\label{automating-hmc}

Every sampler above needed something derived by hand: Gibbs needed full
conditionals worked out algebraically; Metropolis--Hastings needed only
a target density but still needed step sizes tuned by trial and error;
HMC needed an analytic gradient and needed \(\epsilon\) and \(L\) tuned
by hand. \textbf{Stan} is a probabilistic programming language that
automates all of this: given a model written declaratively (data,
parameters, and a log-density), it computes gradients by automatic
differentiation and runs an adaptive variant of HMC --- the
\textbf{No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS)}, which picks a good \(L\) on the fly
by extending the leapfrog trajectory until it starts to double back ---
with step size tuned during a warm-up phase. The user never derives a
conditional or a gradient.

\subsection{The running example, in
Stan}\label{the-running-example-in-stan}

To confirm Stan lands on the same posterior as the Gibbs and MH samplers
above, fit the identical normal-mean/variance model with the identical
(vague) priors and data:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{stan\_normal\_code }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \StringTok{"}
\StringTok{data \{}
\StringTok{  int\textless{}lower=0\textgreater{} n;}
\StringTok{  vector[n] x;}
\StringTok{  real mu0;}
\StringTok{  real\textless{}lower=0\textgreater{} sigma0;}
\StringTok{  real\textless{}lower=0\textgreater{} alpha;}
\StringTok{  real\textless{}lower=0\textgreater{} w;}
\StringTok{\}}
\StringTok{parameters \{}
\StringTok{  real mu;}
\StringTok{  real\textless{}lower=0\textgreater{} sigma2;}
\StringTok{\}}
\StringTok{model \{}
\StringTok{  mu \textasciitilde{} normal(mu0, sqrt(sigma0));}
\StringTok{  sigma2 \textasciitilde{} inv\_gamma(alpha / 2, alpha * w / 2);}
\StringTok{  x \textasciitilde{} normal(mu, sqrt(sigma2));}
\StringTok{\}}
\StringTok{"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{fit\_stan }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{stan}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{model\_code =}\NormalTok{ stan\_normal\_code,}
    \AttributeTok{data =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{n =} \FunctionTok{length}\NormalTok{(x), }\AttributeTok{x =}\NormalTok{ x, }\AttributeTok{mu0 =} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{sigma0 =} \FloatTok{1e10}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{alpha =} \FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{w =} \FloatTok{1e{-}5}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{chains =} \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{iter =} \DecValTok{2000}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{warmup =} \DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{seed =} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{refresh =} \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{stan\_summary }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{summary}\NormalTok{(fit\_stan, }\AttributeTok{pars =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"mu"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"sigma2"}\NormalTok{))}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{summary}
\NormalTok{stan\_sigma\_draws }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{sqrt}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{as.matrix}\NormalTok{(fit\_stan, }\AttributeTok{pars =} \StringTok{"sigma2"}\NormalTok{)[, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
\NormalTok{three\_way }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{data.frame}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{method =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Gibbs"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Metropolis{-}Hastings"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Stan (NUTS)"}\NormalTok{),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_mu =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(mu\_g), }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(mh\_long}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{mu[keep\_mh]), stan\_summary[}\StringTok{"mu"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"mean"}\NormalTok{]),}
    \AttributeTok{sd\_mu   =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(mu\_g), }\FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(mh\_long}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{mu[keep\_mh]), stan\_summary[}\StringTok{"mu"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"sd"}\NormalTok{]),}
    \AttributeTok{mean\_sigma =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(sd\_g), }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(mh\_long}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sd[keep\_mh]), }\FunctionTok{mean}\NormalTok{(stan\_sigma\_draws)),}
    \AttributeTok{sd\_sigma   =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(sd\_g), }\FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(mh\_long}\SpecialCharTok{$}\NormalTok{sd[keep\_mh]), }\FunctionTok{sd}\NormalTok{(stan\_sigma\_draws))}
\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{knitr}\SpecialCharTok{::}\FunctionTok{kable}\NormalTok{(three\_way, }\AttributeTok{digits =} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{align =} \FunctionTok{c}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"l"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"r"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"r"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"r"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"r"}\NormalTok{))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

{

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrrrr@{}}

\caption{\label{tbl-stan-vs-hand}Posterior mean and sd: hand-written
Gibbs and Metropolis-Hastings versus Stan's NUTS sampler, same data,
priors, and model. All three samplers target the identical posterior and
agree up to Monte Carlo error.}

\tabularnewline

\toprule\noalign{}
method & mean\_mu & sd\_mu & mean\_sigma & sd\_sigma \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Gibbs & 10.141 & 0.330 & 2.293 & 0.239 \\
Metropolis-Hastings & 10.144 & 0.330 & 2.294 & 0.237 \\
Stan (NUTS) & 10.137 & 0.329 & 2.301 & 0.245 \\

\end{longtable}

}

The three completely independent implementations --- hand-derived
conditionals, a hand-tuned random walk, and Stan's automatic NUTS ---
agree to within Monte Carlo noise, which is exactly the coherence check
this book has used throughout: independent methods with no shared code
path landing on the same answer is evidence that none of them is wrong.

\subsection{Where Stan earns its keep}\label{where-stan-earns-its-keep}

The normal-mean/variance model above is simple enough that Gibbs
sampling is not just possible but easy, so Stan's advantage is not
visible yet. Its real value shows up on models where hand-deriving
conditionals or gradients would be a serious undertaking: hierarchical
models with many levels of latent structure, time-series models with an
unobserved latent state at every time point, or anything with a
non-conjugate likelihood/prior combination. A stochastic-volatility
model --- where a latent log-variance \(h_t\) follows its own
autoregressive process and each observation's variance is \(e^{h_t}\)
--- is a standard example of this kind: writing a Gibbs sampler for it
requires deriving a conditional for every latent \(h_t\) given its
neighbours, which is neither conjugate nor pleasant, while in Stan it
only needs the model written down.

A first, direct transcription of the model --- sampling each \(h_t\)
centred on \(\mu + \phi(h_{t-1}-\mu)\) exactly as the model
specification reads --- compiles and runs, but Stan reports \(\hat R\)
up to \(1.24\), effective sample sizes in the tens out of a thousand
draws, and a low-BFMI warning: the chains have not mixed. This is not a
bug in Stan but a known geometric pathology (a ``funnel'': when \(\tau\)
is small the conditional distribution of each \(h_t\) given its
neighbours becomes extremely narrow, and a constant leapfrog step size
cannot be simultaneously right for the narrow and wide parts of the
posterior). The standard fix is a \textbf{non-centred
reparameterization}: sample standard-normal innovations
\(h_t^{\text{raw}}\) and reconstruct \(h_t\) deterministically, \[
h_t^{\text{raw}} \sim N(0,1), \qquad h_t = \mu + \phi(h_{t-1}-\mu) + \tau\, h_t^{\text{raw}},
\] which moves the awkward \(\tau\)-dependent scaling out of the sampled
parameters and into a deterministic transform --- the same idea as the
\(\log\sigma\) and logit reparameterizations used earlier in this book
to keep a sampler's geometry well behaved, applied here to a latent time
series instead of a single scale parameter.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{stan\_sv\_code }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \StringTok{"}
\StringTok{data \{}
\StringTok{  int\textless{}lower=0\textgreater{} T;}
\StringTok{  vector[T] y;}
\StringTok{\}}
\StringTok{parameters \{}
\StringTok{  real mu;}
\StringTok{  real\textless{}lower={-}1,upper=1\textgreater{} phi;}
\StringTok{  real\textless{}lower=0.01\textgreater{} tausq;}
\StringTok{  real h0\_raw;}
\StringTok{  vector[T] h\_raw;}
\StringTok{\}}
\StringTok{transformed parameters \{}
\StringTok{  real\textless{}lower=0\textgreater{} tau = sqrt(tausq);}
\StringTok{  real h0 = mu + tau * h0\_raw;}
\StringTok{  vector[T] h;}
\StringTok{  h[1] = mu + phi * (h0 {-} mu) + tau * h\_raw[1];}
\StringTok{  for (t in 2:T)}
\StringTok{    h[t] = mu + phi * (h[t {-} 1] {-} mu) + tau * h\_raw[t];}
\StringTok{\}}
\StringTok{model \{}
\StringTok{  mu \textasciitilde{} normal({-}10, 5);}
\StringTok{  phi \textasciitilde{} uniform({-}1, 1);}
\StringTok{  tausq \textasciitilde{} inv\_gamma(2.5, 0.025);}
\StringTok{  h0\_raw \textasciitilde{} std\_normal();}
\StringTok{  h\_raw \textasciitilde{} std\_normal();}
\StringTok{  y \textasciitilde{} normal(0, exp(h / 2));}
\StringTok{\}}
\StringTok{"}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FunctionTok{set.seed}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{N }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{200}\NormalTok{; mu\_sv }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{; phi\_sv }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{; tau\_sv }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{; h0\_sv }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{h }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(N); y }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{numeric}\NormalTok{(N)}
\NormalTok{h[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, mu\_sv }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ phi\_sv }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (h0\_sv }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mu\_sv), tau\_sv)}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (i }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{2}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{N) h[i] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, mu\_sv }\SpecialCharTok{+}\NormalTok{ phi\_sv }\SpecialCharTok{*}\NormalTok{ (h[i }\SpecialCharTok{{-}} \DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{] }\SpecialCharTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ mu\_sv), tau\_sv)}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ (t }\ControlFlowTok{in} \DecValTok{1}\SpecialCharTok{:}\NormalTok{N) y[t] }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{rnorm}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\FunctionTok{exp}\NormalTok{(h[t] }\SpecialCharTok{/} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{))}

\NormalTok{fit\_sv }\OtherTok{\textless{}{-}} \FunctionTok{stan}\NormalTok{(}
    \AttributeTok{model\_code =}\NormalTok{ stan\_sv\_code, }\AttributeTok{data =} \FunctionTok{list}\NormalTok{(}\AttributeTok{y =}\NormalTok{ y, }\AttributeTok{T =}\NormalTok{ N),}
    \AttributeTok{chains =} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{iter =} \DecValTok{1000}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{warmup =} \DecValTok{500}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{seed =} \DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\AttributeTok{refresh =} \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}

The syntax above is modern Stan (\texttt{=} for deterministic
assignment, \texttt{\textasciitilde{}} sampling statements, no
deprecated \texttt{\textless{}-} or \texttt{\_log} density functions,
which older Stan code --- including earlier drafts of this example ---
used and which current Stan releases no longer accept). After the
reparameterization, \(\hat R \approx 1\) and effective sample sizes in
the hundreds confirm the chains have converged; the \(95\%\) intervals
for \(\phi\) and \(\tau\) are wide but do contain their true simulated
values, which is the honest outcome for a weakly identified latent-state
model fit to a single moderate-length series, and a materially better
diagnosis than the centred version's confidently-wrong-looking but
actually-unconverged chains. That a model no one in this chapter
hand-derived a sampler for can be fit --- and, just as importantly,
checked for convergence --- with a few lines of declarative model code
is the point of the whole section.

\section{Summary}\label{summary-6}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  A Markov chain converges to a target \(\pi\) from any starting point
  if its transition \(T\) leaves \(\pi\) invariant and is aperiodic and
  irreducible; \textbf{detailed balance}, a pairwise condition, is the
  standard route to invariance.
\item
  \textbf{Gibbs sampling} cycles through exact draws from each full
  conditional; free of tuning, but needs conditionals that are both
  known and directly sample-able, and data augmentation extends it to
  models with latent variables.
\item
  \textbf{Metropolis--Hastings} accepts or rejects a proposal from
  \emph{any} distribution with probability depending on the
  target-density ratio (times a proposal-symmetry correction);
  random-walk MH needs a tuned step size, aimed at low autocorrelation
  rather than high acceptance.
\item
  \textbf{Hamiltonian Monte Carlo} adds momentum and follows
  gradient-driven trajectories, discretized by the leapfrog integrator
  and corrected by a Metropolis step, giving distant, nearly independent
  proposals even on strongly correlated targets --- at the cost of
  needing a gradient and two tuning parameters (\(\epsilon\), \(L\)).
\item
  \textbf{Stan} automates gradient computation and adaptive HMC (NUTS)
  for essentially any model expressible in its declarative syntax,
  turning what would otherwise be a substantial hand-derivation (as the
  stochastic-volatility example shows) into a direct transcription of
  the model.
\end{itemize}

This is the last of the sampling-methods chapters in the book:
quadrature (Chapter 8) and Laplace's method (Chapter 9) work in low
dimension by grid or by a local Gaussian approximation; rejection and
importance sampling (Chapters 10--11) draw independently from a fixed
proposal; Gibbs, Metropolis--Hastings, HMC, and Stan (this chapter) let
a Markov chain do the work instead, and are, in practice, how most
modern Bayesian computation is actually done.
